The spectral theorem for normal operators on a Clifford module
Fabrizio Colombo, David P. Kimsey

Abstract
. In this paper, using the recently discovered notion of the S S -spectrum, we prove the spectral theorem for a bounded or unbounded normal operator on a Clifford module (i.e., a two-sided Hilbert module over a Clifford algebra based on units that all square to be − 1 -1 ). Moreover, we establish the existence of a Borel functional calculus for bounded or unbounded normal operators on a Clifford module. Towards this end, we have developed many results on functional analysis, operator theory, integration theory and measure theory in a Clifford setting which may be of an independent interest. Our spectral theory is the natural spectral theory for the Dirac operator on manifolds in the non-self adjoint case. Moreover, our results provide a new notion of spectral theory and a Borel functional calculus for a class of n n -tuples of commuting or non-commuting operators on a real or complex Hilbert space. Moreover, for a special class of n n -tuples of operators on a Hilbert space our results provide a complementary functional calculus to the functional calculus of J. L. Taylor.



AMS Classification 47A10, 47A60.

Keywords: Clifford spectral theorem,
S
-spectrum, Clifford measure theory, Dirac operator on manifolds, continuous and Borel functional calculi, spectral integrals.

1.
Introduction

The spectral theorem for a normal operator on a complex Hilbert space is an incredibly elegant result which lies at the heart of operator theory, harmonic analysis and mathematical physics. In this paper we endeavour to generalise the spectral theorem to a noncommutative setting where a complex Hilbert space is replaced by a Hilbert module over a Clifford algebra
\mathcal{H}_{n}:=\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathbb{R}_{n}
, where
\mathcal{H}
is a real Hilbert space and
\mathbb{R}_{n}:=\mathbb{R}_{0,n}
is a Clifford algebra which is generated by units
e_{0}=1
and
e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}
, where
e_{i}^{2}=-1
and
e_{i}e_{j}=-e_{j}e_{i}
for
i,j=1,\ldots,n
with
n>0
(we will favour the term Clifford module for brevity) and the notion of spectrum is replaced by the recently discovered notion of
S
-spectrum. We wish to stress that given the well-known classification of Clifford algebras
\mathbb{R}_{n}
for
n=1,2,\ldots
(see, e.g.,
[
50
]
), our results can easily be translated to handle spectral theory for a linear operator on any Hilbert module over a finitely generated unital ring.

The
S
-spectrum can be characterised by the invertibility of a second order operator and it is defined to be a subset of the set of paravectors in
\mathbb{R}_{n}
(where one can instil a natural complex structure corresponding to any paravector). More precisely, corresponding to a right linear operator
T
on a Clifford module
\mathcal{H}_{n}
over
\mathbb{R}_{n}
, we define

(1.1)

\sigma_{S}(T):=\{s=\sum_{j=0}^{n}s_{j}e_{j}:(T^{2}-2s_{0}T+(s_{0}^{2}+\ldots+s_{n}^{2})I)^{-1}\notin\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})\},

where
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
denotes the set of bounded linear operators on
\mathcal{H}_{n}
. The
S
-spectrum was discovered by the first author and I. Sabadini in the context of arbitrary operators on a quaternionic Banach space (i.e., a Clifford module over
\mathbb{R}_{2}
) and paravector operators
T
on a Banach module over a Clifford algebra
\mathscr{X}_{n}=\mathscr{X}\otimes\mathbb{R}_{n}
, where
\mathscr{X}
is a real Banach space and
\mathbb{R}_{n}
is as above with
n>2
, i.e., right linear operators of the form

T=T_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}e_{j}T_{j},

where
T_{0},\ldots,T_{n}
are linear operators on the real Banach space
\mathscr{X}
(see Appendix
A
for more details).

Recently we realised that we can dispense with the restriction that
T
is a paravector operator, i.e., the
S
-spectrum can be defined for an arbitrary right linear operator on
\mathscr{X}_{n}
. This observation will turn out to be critical for this manuscript. Moreover, the consequences of this observation on the
S
-functional calculus and the slice hyperholomorphic function theory have been investigated in
[
17
]
and
[
18
]
, respectively.

It turns out that Clifford numbers of the form
s=\sum_{j=0}^{n}s_{j}e_{j}
embed naturally into a complex plane and hence

\sigma_{S}(T)\subseteq\bigcup_{\mathfrak{I}\,\in\,\mathbb{S}}\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},

where
\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}=\{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}\mathfrak{I}:\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in\mathbb{R}\}
and
\mathbb{S}:=\{s=\sum_{j=1}^{n}s_{j}e_{j}:s^{2}=-1\}
. A moment’s consideration of (
1.1
) will reveal that the upper half plane
\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}
can identified in a natural way with
\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}
for any choice of
\mathfrak{I},\mathfrak{J}\in\mathbb{S}
. Thus, one may think of the
S
-spectrum as a complex notion of spectrum with an elegant symmetry. For a concise background of the history of the
S
-spectrum, related function theory and known results in the quaternionic case see Appendix
A
.

We shall see that corresponding to every densely defined normal operator
T
on a Clifford module
\mathcal{H}_{n}
, there exists an imaginary operator
J
(i.e.,
J^{*}J=I
and
J^{*}=-J
) and a uniquely determined spectral measure
E:=E_{\mathfrak{I}}
such that

(1.2)

T=\int_{\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}{\rm Re}(\lambda)dE(\lambda)+\int_{\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}{\rm Im}(\lambda)dE(\lambda)\,J\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad\mathfrak{I}\in\mathbb{S}.

We shall also see that

(1.3)

{\rm supp}\,\,E=\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}.

Moreover, we establish a full analogue of spectral integrals corresponding to a spectral measure
E
, the Borel functional calculus associated with
T
and the spectral mapping theorem in the bounded and unbounded case. The fact that
J
is an imaginary operator on
\mathcal{H}_{n}
allows one to think of
J
as an operator-valued analogue of an imaginary unit. It is worth pointing out that in the present setting the spectral measure
E
gives rise to a family of
\mathbb{R}_{n}
-valued measures which are positive as elements of the Clifford algebra
\mathbb{R}_{n}
. This creates a significant technical difficulty when building the requisite machinery to prove (
1.2
) and the Borel functional calculus.

Motivation

We mention some of the main motivations to study the spectral theorem on a Clifford module.

(I)
Spectral theory for vector operators
.

The spectral theory on the
S
-spectrum is a very natural tool for studying vector operators
that come from vector analysis
such as the gradient operators with nonconstant coefficients

(1.4)

T=\sum_{j=1}^{n}e_{j}a_{j}(x)\partial_{x_{j}},\ \ \ x=(x_{1},...,x_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

where
e_{j}
is an orthonormal basis in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and

a_{j}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
,
j=1,...,n

are given functions with suitable regularity and
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
is an open set.
With our spectral theory we can define, for example, the fractional powers of operators of the form (
1.4
) which can be used to represent fractional Fourier’s law for the propagation of the heat in nonhomogeneous materials
contained in
\Omega
.
In the quaternionic setting, fractional Fourier’s law,
the has been considered in various papers, see for example
[
15
,
19
]
, and the references therein.

(II)
Dirac operator on manifolds
.

In a great preponderance of the papers on spectral theory for the Dirac operators the self-adjoint case is considered. This is most likely the cause due to the associated difficulties of defining in an appropriate way the spectrum of the non self-adjoint Dirac operator. However, the Dirac operator is just
a particular case of a Clifford operator, and on manifolds, it has in general non-constant coefficients, so its natural notion of spectrum is the
S
-spectrum. The
S
-spectrum of a self-adjoint Clifford operator (in particular the Dirac operator) is real.
Let us explain with more details the above motivation.
Assume that
g:U\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, given by

g(x)=(g_{ij}(x))_{i,j=1}^{n},

is a smooth matrix-valued function defined on the open set
U
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}

where
g(x)
will always be taken to be positive-definite and symmetric.
Then

(1.5)

g_{x}(\xi,\eta)=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}g_{ij}(x)\xi_{i}\eta_{j},\ \ \xi,\ \eta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

is a positive-definite inner product on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and

(1.6)

g_{x}(X,Y)=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}g_{ij}(x)a_{i}b_{j},\ \ {\rm where}\ \ X=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}(x)\partial_{x_{i}},\ \ \ Y=\sum_{j=1}^{n}b_{j}(x)\partial_{x_{j}},

defines a positive-definite inner product space on the tangent space
\mathcal{T}_{x}(U)
to
U
at
x
.
So
U
can be seen as a coordinate neighbourhood for the Riemannian manifold
M
taking
x=(x_{1},...,x_{n})
as coordinates and (
1.6
) as inner product, making it possible to study the Dirac operator on
M
, by introducing it as a nonconstant coefficients nonhomogeneous first-order systems of differential operators on
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U,\mathcal{H})
.
To give a precise definition of the spectrum of the Dirac operator on
M
, (i..e, the
S
-spectrum), we need to perform the following steps.

(a)
We need to give the definition of the Dirac operator
D
on
M
.

(b)
Next, we need to write
D^{2}
explicitly in terms of a second-order Laplacian and a curvature operator,
via the Bochner-Weitzenböck theorem.

The precise expression of the operator
D^{2}
is of crucial importance in order to define the
S
-spectrum because it is associated with the operator

\mathcal{Q}_{s}(D):=D^{2}-2s_{0}D+|s|^{2}\,I,

here
s
is a paravector in the Clifford algebra
\mathbb{R}_{n}
.
Before we can define the Dirac operator, we require some additional notions. Let
g:\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}\to U
be an invertible function on
\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
and write

(1.7)

g^{-1}:U\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n},\ \ g^{-1}(x)=(g^{ij}(x))_{i,j=1}^{n}.

Next, let

\gamma(x)=(\gamma_{ij}(x))_{i,j=1}^{n},\ \ \gamma^{-1}(x)=(\gamma^{ij}(x))_{i,j=1}^{n}:U\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n},

be the unique square roots of
g
and
g^{-1}
, respectively.

Let
e_{1},...,e_{n}
be the skew-adjoint operators
satisfying the Clifford relations

e_{j}e_{k}+e_{k}e_{j}=-2\delta_{jk}
, set

e_{i}(x)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\gamma^{ij}(x)e_{j},\ \ \ x\in U,

for every
i=1,...,n.

By definition we have that

(1.8)

e_{j}(x)e_{k}(x)+e_{k}(x)e_{j}(x)=-2g^{jk}(x),\ \ \ x\in U.

Let
d\tau:{\rm spin}(n)\to\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H})
be the
representation of the Lie algebra of
{\rm spin}(n)

derived from the representation
\tau:{\rm spin}(n)\to\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H})
.
For more details see
[
39
]
.

As a differential operator on
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U,\mathcal{H})
the standard Dirac operator
D
on
(U,g)
is defined by

D=\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}(x)\Big(\partial_{x_{i}}+d\tau(\omega_{i}(x))\Big)

where
\omega_{1},...,\omega_{n}:U\to{\rm spin}(n)

are smooth functions uniquely determined by
g
.

Some more work is necessary to define
D^{2}
, but clearly the spectral theory on the
S
-spectrum allows one to consider non-self adjoint Dirac operators.
We recall that the scalar curvature of
(U,g)
is defined by

(1.9)

\kappa(x)=-\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}R_{ijij}(x)

where
R_{ijk\ell}(x)
is the Riemann curvature tensor.
As an example we recall Spinor Laplacian, that is we
assume
\mathcal{H}=\Theta_{n}
and
\tau
is the Spin representation of
S
of
{\rm spin}(n)
on
\Theta_{n}
,
n=2m
, (see
[
39
]
) the second order operator

D^{2}
is often called spinor Laplacian is given by the Lichnerowicz’s formula

D^{2}=-\Delta_{S}+\frac{1}{4}\kappa(x).

So in similar cases the
S
-spectrum can be written explicitly.
Several results, such as
[
11
,
32
,
47
,
48
,
52
]
and also

[
31
,
43
,
49
,
56
,
60
,
61
]

in spectral theory for the Dirac operators can now be seen in a new
light using the spectral theory on the
S
-spectrum for the Dirac operator,
also using the
S
-functional calculus
[
3
,
7
,
24
,
35
]
and the function theory

[
2
,
21
,
22
,
23
]
on which this calculus is based on for
n
-tuples of operators.

(III)
Complementarity to the Taylor spectrum for a class of tuples of commuting operators or non-commuting operators and harmonic analysis
.

Given an
n
-tuple of bounded or unbounded operators
(T_{1},…,T_{n})
on a real Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, we can form the right linear operator

T=\sum_{j=1}^{n}e_{\alpha_{j}}T_{j},

where
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\in\raisebox{1.79993pt}{\Large$\wp$}(\{1,\ldots,n\})
. Now if there exists a configuration of units such that
T
is normal, then one may define the spectrum of
(T_{1},\ldots,T_{n})
to be the
S
-spectrum of
T
, i.e.,
\sigma_{S}(T)
. Moreover, one has a Borel functional calculus at hand for a reasonably large class of functions of
T
, which is helpful in problems in harmonic analysis and partial differential equations. It is worth mentioning that one can find the relations between the monogenic functional calculus, Taylor functional calculus and the Weyl functional calculus (see,

[
45
,
46
,
53
,
54
,
63
]
).
In harmonic analysis in higher dimensions, singular integrals and in the study of the Fourier transform
one can find various connections with Clifford analysis in the recent book
[
64
]
. Boundary value problems treated with quaternionic techniques can be found in
[
41
]
. Clifford wavelets, singular integrals, and Hardy spaces are studied in
[
55
]
.

(IV)
Spectral theory for linear operators on a Hilbert module over a finitely generated unital ring
.

We first note that we may use the classification of Clifford modules (see, e.g.,
[
50
]
) and the fact that every finitely generated unital ring is isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
to see that there is a natural isomorphism between any Hilbert module over a finitely generated unital ring
R
and a Clifford module
\mathcal{H}_{n}
for an appropriate choice of
n
. Thus, given a normal right linear operator
T
on a Hilbert module over a finitely generated unital ring
R
, we may view
T
as a being a right linear operator on a Clifford module
\mathcal{H}_{n}
for a suitable choice of
n
. We can then utilise the spectral theory and functional calculi in the Clifford setting and translate back to the Hilbert module setting in a straight forward manner.

For the function theory of slice hyperholomorphic functions the
main books are
[
5
,
25
,
26
,
27
,
33
,
36
]
, while
for the spectral theory on the
S
-spectrum we mention
[
6
,
12
,
14
,
16
,
27
]
.
For the Fueter and monogenic function theory and related topics
see the books
[
10
,
20
,
29
,
39
,
40
,
44
,
65
]
.

Strategy

We wish to summarise our strategy for proving the spectral theorem for a normal operator on a Clifford module.

(I)
It turns out that the
S
-spectrum and
S
-resolvent set
can be defined for all bounded or unbounded operators (not necessarily paravector operators). This is the first crucial intuition for the decomposition of normal operators and
we show that the
S
-spectrum of a bounded operator is a non-empty compact subset of
\{s\in\mathbb{R}_{n}:s=s_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}s_{j}e_{j}\}
.

(II)
We define a spectral measure on a Clifford module and spectral integrals
\mathbb{I}(f)
,
where
f
belongs to a suitable class of functions (see Section 4).

Moreover, we point out that an absolutely key result is Theorem
5.3.14
which connects

\sigma_{S}(\mathbb{I}(f))
to
{\rm supp}\,E
. Dealing with the
S
-spectrum requires to overcome substantially
different difficulties with respect to the classical complex or quaternionic Hilbert space case (where the spectral measure
E
gives rise to a family of positive measures in the usual sense).

(III) We prove a spectral theorem for a bounded self-adjoint operator
(see Theorem
6.0.1
) where the important result given in Theorem
5.3.14

is being used to show that the spectral measure
E
has the property that
{\rm supp}\,E=\sigma_{S}(T)
.

(IV) We prove a polar decomposition for a bounded Clifford operator
T

and specialise the result to the case when
T
is normal (see Theorem
7.0.1
).
This then enables one to prove that
every
bounded normal operator can be written as

T=A+BJ
, where
A=A^{*}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
,
B\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is positive,
JJ^{*}=I
and
J^{*}=-J
and
A
,
B
and
J
all mutually commute (see Theorem
8.0.4
).

(V)
To prove the spectral theorem for bounded normal operators
(see Theorem
9.0.2
), one needs to apply a technical result (see Theorem
5.3.17
) to manufacture a uniquely determined spectral measure
E
which lives on
\sigma_{S}(A)\times\sigma_{S}(B)
which will be identified with the complex plane
\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}
, where
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathbb{S}
.

One applies the key result Theorem
5.3.14
with the identity function
f(\lambda)=\lambda
to see that
\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}={\rm supp}\,E\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}
. One can get a suitable integral representation for
T
which resembles the quaternionic case.

(VI)
We use the bounded case of the spectral theorem (see Theorem
9.0.2
) to prove the unbounded case (see Theorem
10.0.4
).

For the convenience of the reader we have compiled a list of commonly used notation that appears throughout this manuscript.

Notation

\mathbb{R}_{n}:=\mathbb{R}_{0,n}
will denote the Cifford algebra generated by the units
e_{0}:=1
and
e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}
, where
e_{i}e_{j}=-e_{j}e_{i}
for
i\neq j
. An element
a\in\mathbb{R}_{n}
can be written as

a=\sum_{\alpha}a_{\alpha}e_{\alpha}:=\sum_{\alpha\in\raisebox{1.79993pt}{\Large$\wp$}(\{1,\ldots,n\})}a_{\alpha}e_{\alpha},

where
\emptyset
can be identified with
0
and
\alpha\neq\emptyset
can be interpreted as a
n
-tuple
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})
with
i_{1}<\ldots<i_{n})
.

\mathbb{S}:=\{\mathfrak{I}=(0,\mathfrak{I}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{I}_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}:\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathfrak{I}_{i}=1\}
. A typical element of
\mathbb{S}
will be denoted by
\mathfrak{I}
or
\mathfrak{J}
.

For any
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathbb{S}
, we shall let
\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}=\{\lambda_{0}+\lambda_{1}\,\mathfrak{I}:\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1}\in\mathbb{R}\}
and
\mathbb{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{I}}=\{\lambda_{0}+\lambda_{1}\,\mathfrak{I}:\lambda_{0}\in\mathbb{R},\lambda_{1}\geq 0\}
.

\mathfrak{S}(\mathbb{R}_{n})
will denote the set of self-adjoint Clifford numbers in
\mathbb{R}_{n}
, i.e., all
a\in\mathbb{R}_{n}
such that
\bar{a}=a
.

\mathfrak{P}(\mathbb{R}_{n})
will denote the set of positive semidefinite Clifford numbers in
\mathbb{R}_{n}
, i.e., all
a\in\mathbb{R}_{n}
such that
a=b\bar{b}
for some
b\in\mathbb{R}_{n}
. In this case, we will write
a\succeq 0
.

\chi:\mathbb{R}_{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{2^{n}\times 2^{n}}
will denote the injective
*
-homomorphism from the Clifford algebra
\mathbb{R}_{n}
to the set of real matrices of size
2^{n}
(see Definition
2.1.7
).

\mathcal{H}_{n}=\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathbb{R}_{n}
will denote a two-sided Clifford module over
\mathbb{R}_{n}
, where
\mathcal{H}
is a real Hilbert space (see Definition
2.1.6
).

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:\mathcal{H}_{n}\times\mathcal{H}_{n}\to\mathbb{R}_{n}
given by (
2.9
) and
\|x\|:=\sqrt{{\rm Re}\,\langle x,x\rangle}
for
x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
.

For any closed submodule
Y\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{n}
, we shall let

Y^{\perp}:=\{y\in\mathcal{H}_{n}:\text{$\langle x,y\rangle=0$ for all $x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}$}\}.

\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
will denote the set of right linear operators on
\mathcal{H}_{n}
. The domain of an operator
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
will be denoted by
\mathcal{D}(T)
(see Definition
3.0.1
).

{\rm Ran}\,T
and
{\rm Ker}\,T
will denote the range and kernel of
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
, respectively (see Definition
3.0.3
).

\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
will denote the set of bounded linear operators on
\mathcal{H}_{n}
(see Definition
3.0.4
. We shall let

\|T\|:=\sup_{\|x\|\leq 1}\|Tx\|=\lim_{\|x\|=1}\|Tx\|.

\mathcal{G}(T)
will denote the graph of a linear operator (see Definition
3.0.6
).

For
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
, we shall let
\langle x,y\rangle_{T}:=\langle x,y\rangle+\langle Tx,Ty\rangle
and
\|x\|_{T}:=(\|x\|+\|Tx\|^{2})^{1/2}
for
x\in\mathcal{D}(T)
.

For
S,T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
, we will write
S\subseteq T
if
\mathcal{D}(S)\subseteq\mathcal{D}(T)
and
Sx=Tx
for
x\in\mathcal{D}(S)
.

|T|:=(T^{*}T)^{1/2}
(see Theorem
7.0.1
).

For any closable operator
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
, we shall let
\overline{T}
denote the closure of
T
(see Definition
3.0.14
).

We shall let
\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T):=T^{2}-2{\rm Re}(s)T+|s|^{2}I
.

\rho_{S}(T)
denotes the
S
-resolvent set of
T
and
\sigma_{S}(T)
denotes the
S
-spectrum of
T
(see Definition
3.1.1
).

We shall let
S_{L}^{-1}(s,T)
and
S_{R}^{-1}(s,T)
denote the left
S
-resolvent operator and right
S
-resolvent operator of
T
at
s
, respectively (see Definition
3.1.4
).

For
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
, we shall let
r_{S}(T)
denote the spectral radius of
T
with respect to the
S
-spectrum (see (
3.22
)).

For
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
, we shall let
\sigma_{PS}(T)
,
\sigma_{RS}(T)
and
\sigma_{CS}(T)
denote the point
S
-spectrum of
T
, residual
S
-spectrum of
T
and continuous
S
-spectrum of
T
, respectively (see Definition
3.1.11
).

For
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
, we shall let
\Pi_{S}(T)
and
\Gamma_{S}(T)
denote the approximate point
S
-spectrum of
T
and compression
S
-spectrum of
T
, respectively (see Definition
3.1.11
).

T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
will be called normal if
T
is densely defined, closed,
\mathcal{D}(T)=\mathcal{D}(T^{*})
and
TT^{*}=T^{*}T
.

T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
will be called self-adjoint if
\mathcal{D}(T)=\mathcal{D}(T^{*})
and
Tx=T^{*}x
for all
x\in\mathcal{D}(T)
.

T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
will be called positive if
T
is
\mathcal{D}(T)=\mathcal{D}(T^{*})
and
\langle Tx,x\rangle\succeq 0
for all
x\in\mathcal{D}(T)
, i.e.,
\langle Tx,x\rangle\in\mathfrak{P}(\mathbb{R}_{n})
for all
x\in\mathcal{D}(T)
.

T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
will called anti self-adjoint if
\mathcal{D}(T)=\mathcal{D}(T^{*})
and
T=-T^{*}
for
x\in\mathcal{D}(T)
.

T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
will be called unitary if
TT^{*}=I
.

J\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
will be called imaginary if
J
is anti self-adjoint and
J
is unitary.

\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
will denote the set of orthogonal projections on
\mathcal{H}_{n}
.

s-\lim_{i\to\infty}T_{i}
will denote the limit of a sequence of operators
(T_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
, where
T_{i}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
for
i=1,2,\ldots
, in the strong operator topology.

E
will denote a spectral measure on
(\Omega,\mathscr{A})
, where
\mathscr{A}
is a
\sigma
-algebra of sets generated by
\Omega
.

The set of Borel sets generated by
\Omega
will be denoted by
\mathscr{B}(\Omega)
.

Given a spectral measure
E
on
(\Omega,\mathscr{B}(\Omega))
,
{\rm supp}\,E
will denote the support of
E
(see Definition
5.1.9
).

Given an
\mathbb{R}_{n}
-valued measure
\nu
the total variation of
\nu
will be denoted by
|\nu|
(see (
5.5
)).

Given an imaginary operator
J\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
, we will say that
J
is associated with a spectral measure
E
on
(\Omega,\mathscr{A})
if
E(M)J=JE(M)
for all
M\in\mathscr{A}
(see Definition
5.2.1
).

\mathfrak{B}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}})
will denote the Banach space of all bounded
\mathscr{A}
-measurable functions
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}
equipped with the norm

\|f\|_{\infty}=\sup_{\lambda\in\Omega}|f(\lambda)|.

\mathfrak{B}_{s}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}})
will denote the subspace of
\mathfrak{B}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}})
of all simple functions.

Given
f\in\mathfrak{B}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}})
and a spectral measure
E
on
(\Omega,\mathscr{A})
, we shall let
\mathbb{I}(f)
denote the spectral measure of
f
with respect to
E
(see Definition
5.2.3
).

Given a spectral measure
E
on
(\Omega,\mathscr{A})
, we shall let
\mathfrak{F}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E)
denote the set of all
\mathscr{A}
-measurable functions
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}\cup\{\infty\}
which are
E
-a.e. finite, i.e.,
E(\{\lambda\in\Omega:f(\lambda)=\infty\})=0
.

Given
f\in\mathfrak{F}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E)
and a spectral measure
E
on
(\Omega,\mathscr{A})
, we shall let
\mathbb{I}(f)
denote the spectral measure of
f
with respect to
E
(see Theorem
5.3.4
(ii)).

Given an imaginary operator
J\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
and
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathbb{S}
,
\mathcal{H}_{\pm}(J,\mathfrak{I})=\{x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}:Jx=x(\pm\mathfrak{I})\}
is a complex subspace of
\mathcal{H}_{n}
(see (
8.5
)).

Given a densely defined operator
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
, we shall let
C_{T}:=(I+T^{*}T)^{-1}
and
Z_{T}:=TC_{T}^{1/2}
(see Definition
10.0.1
).

2.
Preliminaries

In this section we will formulate a number of definitions and results on Clifford algebras, Clifford modules, linear operators on Clifford modules, the
S
-spectrum and measure theory and integration theory with respect to a Clifford algebra-valued measure. While the topic of Clifford algebras is very classical and well-known, the theory of linear operators on Clifford modules and measure theory and integration theory with respect to a Clifford algebra-valued measure are not so well developed. We have furnished proofs for Clifford algebra/module analogues of results whenever the proof differs from the classical case with the aim of making the present manuscript as self contained as possible.

2.1.
Clifford algebras

Definition 2.1.1
.

A collection of
n
elements
e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}
, with
n=p+q
and
p,q\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
will be called
imaginary units
if

\displaystyle e_{i}^{2}=

\displaystyle\;+1\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad i=1,\ldots,p

\displaystyle e_{j}^{2}=

\displaystyle\;-1\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad j=p+1,\ldots,n

\displaystyle e_{j}e_{k}+e_{k}e_{j}=

\displaystyle\;0\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad j\neq k

(2.1)

\displaystyle e_{1}\ldots e_{n}\neq

\displaystyle\;\pm 1.

We shall denote the real algebra generated by the imaginary units
e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}
the universal Clifford algebra and denote it by
\mathbb{R}_{p,q}
. An element of
\mathbb{R}_{p,q}
is called a
Clifford number
.

Remark 2.1.2
.

We note that it is only necessary to assume (
2.1
) if
p-q\equiv 1({\rm mod}\;4)
. It is easy to check that
\{e_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\raisebox{1.79993pt}{\Large$\wp$}(\{1,\ldots,n\})}
is linearly independent and hence
\mathbb{R}_{p,q}
has dimension
2^{n}
(as a real vector space).

Let
\alpha\in\raisebox{1.79993pt}{\Large$\wp$}(\{1,\ldots,n\})
and if
\alpha\neq\emptyset
, then we may write
\alpha=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\}
, with
i_{1}<\ldots<i_{r}
. Then we may let

\displaystyle e_{\alpha}:=

\displaystyle\;e_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}:=e_{i_{1}}\ldots e_{i_{r}}\quad\quad{\rm if}\quad\alpha=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\},

\displaystyle e_{0}:=

\displaystyle\;e_{\emptyset}=1\quad\quad{\rm if}\quad\alpha=\emptyset

and write an arbitrary Clifford number
a\in\mathbb{R}_{p,q}
as

a=\sum_{\alpha}a_{\alpha}e_{\alpha}:=\sum_{\alpha\,\in\,\raisebox{1.79993pt}{\Large$\wp$}(\{1,\ldots,n\})}a_{\alpha}e_{\alpha},

where the sum is taken over
\emptyset
and subsets
\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\}
with
i_{1}<\ldots<i_{r}
.

Remark 2.1.3
.

The only Clifford algebra considered in the remainder of this paper will be
\mathbb{R}_{n}:=\mathbb{R}_{0,n}
.

We will let

\bar{a}:=\sum_{\alpha}a_{\alpha}\bar{e}_{\alpha}=\sum_{\alpha}a_{\alpha}(-e_{\alpha})

and

|a|:=\left(\sum_{\alpha}a_{\alpha}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad a=\sum_{\alpha}a_{\alpha}e_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}_{n}.

Hence one can easily check that for all
a,b\in\mathbb{R}_{n}
, we have

\displaystyle\overline{ab}=

\displaystyle\;\bar{b}\,\bar{a}

\displaystyle\overline{a+b}=

\displaystyle\;\bar{a}+\bar{b}

(2.2)

\displaystyle|a+b|\leq

\displaystyle\;|a|+|b|

(2.3)

\displaystyle|ab|=

\displaystyle\;|a|\,|b|\quad\quad{\rm whenever}\quad b\bar{b}=|b|^{2}

(2.4)

\displaystyle|ab|\leq

\displaystyle\;2^{n-1}|a|\,|b|.

Definition 2.1.4
.

Given
s=(s_{0},s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, we may identify the vector
s\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
with the
paravector

\mathfrak{s}=\sum_{i=0}^{n}s_{i}e_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{n}
. With a slight abuse of notation, for the remainder of this paper, we will use
s
in place of
\mathfrak{s}
.

Let
\mathbb{S}
denote the unit sphere of vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, i.e.,

\mathbb{S}:=\{\mathfrak{I}=(0,\mathfrak{I}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{I}_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}:\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathfrak{I}^{2}_{i}=1\}.

It is easy to see that
\mathbb{S}
is an
(n-1)
-sphere in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathbb{S}
implies that
\mathfrak{I}^{2}=-1
. Note that the real two-dimensional subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
generated by
1
and
\mathfrak{I}
is complex plane
\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}:=\mathbb{R}+\mathbb{R}\,\mathfrak{I}
. It is not hard to see that
\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}
is isomorphic to the usual complex plane
\mathbb{C}
.

Given a paravector
s=\sum_{i=0}^{n}s_{i}e_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, it is possible to find
\mathfrak{I}_{s}\in\mathbb{S}
such that
s\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}_{s}}
. Indeed, if
s\neq 0
, then we may write

s=s_{0}+\left(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}s_{i}e_{i}}{|s|}\right)|s|.

Thus, if we let
\mathfrak{I}_{s}:=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}s_{i}e_{i}}{|s|}
, then one can check that
\mathfrak{I}_{s}\in\mathbb{S}
and hence
s\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}_{s}}
.

Definition 2.1.5

(self-adjoint Clifford numbers)
.

We will call
a\in\mathbb{R}_{n}

self-adjoint
if
a=\bar{a}
. The set of all self-adjoint Clifford numbers in
\mathbb{R}_{n}
shall be denoted by
\mathfrak{S}(\mathbb{R}_{n})
. We note that
\mathfrak{S}(\mathbb{R}_{n})
is a real vector space.

Definition 2.1.6

(positive semidefinite Clifford numbers)
.

We will call
a\in\mathbb{R}_{n}

positive semidefinite
if there exists
b\in\mathbb{R}_{n}
such that
a=b\bar{b}
. In this case, we shall write
a\succeq 0
. The set of all positive semidefinite Clifford numbers in
\mathbb{R}_{n}
shall be denoted by
\mathfrak{P}(\mathbb{R}_{n})
.

We will need to recall a well-known injective
*
-homomorphism
\chi:\mathbb{R}_{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{2^{n}\times 2^{n}}
which can be found, e.g., in
[
55
]
.

Definition 2.1.7
.

Let
\chi:\mathbb{R}_{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{2^{n}\times 2^{n}}
be the injective
*
-homomorphism given by the following inductive construction. We will first give meaning to
\chi(e_{j})
for
j=0,1,\ldots,n
. Let
\chi(e_{0})=I_{2^{n}}
and
\chi(e_{j}):=E_{j}^{n}
for
j=1,\ldots,n
, where
\{E_{j}^{k}\}_{j=1}^{k}
are inductively defined via

E_{1}^{1}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},\quad E_{j}^{k+1}:=\begin{pmatrix}E_{j}^{k}&0\\
0&-E_{j}^{k}\end{pmatrix}\quad{\rm and}\quad E_{k+1}^{k+1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-I_{2^{k}}\\
I_{2^{k}}&0\end{pmatrix}

for
j=1,\ldots,k
and
k=1,\ldots,n-1
. Next, we let

\chi(e_{\alpha}):=\chi(e_{i_{1}})\cdots\chi(e_{i_{k}})\quad{\rm for}\quad\alpha=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\},

where
i_{1}<\ldots<i_{k}
. Finally for
a=\sum_{\alpha}a_{\alpha}e_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}_{n}
, we let

\chi(a):=\sum_{\alpha}a_{\alpha}\chi(e_{\alpha}).

Remark 2.1.8
.

Let
a\in\mathbb{R}_{n}
. It is easy to check that
\chi(a)
is a positive semidefinite matrix in
\mathbb{R}^{2^{n}\times 2^{n}}
if and only if
a
is a positive semidefinite Clifford number.

2.2.
Clifford modules

Definition 2.2.1

(Clifford modules over
\mathbb{R}_{n}
)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a real Hilbert space with an inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
and a natural norm
\|x\|_{\mathcal{H}}:=\langle x,x\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}^{1/2}
for
x\in\mathcal{H}
. Then by
\mathcal{H}_{n}
we mean the
two-sided Clifford module generated by
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathbb{R}_{n}
with
n>0
, i.e.,
\mathcal{H}_{n}
consists of all vectors of the form

(2.5)

x=\sum_{\alpha}x_{\alpha}\otimes e_{\alpha},

with

(2.6)

\displaystyle x+y:=

\displaystyle\;\sum_{\alpha}(x_{\alpha}+y_{\alpha})\otimes e_{\alpha},

(2.7)

\displaystyle xa:=

\displaystyle\;\sum_{\alpha}x_{\alpha}\otimes(e_{\alpha}a)=\sum_{\alpha,\beta}(x_{\alpha}a_{\beta})\otimes(e_{\alpha}e_{\beta})

(2.8)

\displaystyle ax:=

\displaystyle\;\sum_{\alpha}x_{\alpha}\otimes(ae_{\alpha})=\sum_{\alpha,\beta}(x_{\alpha}a_{\beta})\otimes(e_{\beta}e_{\alpha})

for
y=\sum_{\alpha}y_{\alpha}\otimes e_{\alpha}
and
a=\sum_{\alpha}a_{\alpha}e_{\alpha}
. We will employ the short-hand
x=\sum_{\alpha}x_{\alpha}e_{\alpha}
in place of
x=\sum_{\alpha}x_{\alpha}\otimes e_{\alpha}
. We may then endow a Clifford module
\mathcal{H}_{n}
with the
\mathbb{R}_{n}
-valued “
inner product
”
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
given by

(2.9)

\langle x,y\rangle:=\sum_{\alpha,\beta}e_{\alpha}\bar{e}_{\beta}\langle x_{\alpha},y_{\beta}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad x,y\in\mathcal{H}_{n}.

Remark 2.2.2
.

For the great preponderance of this work, we will not need the fact that
\mathcal{H}_{n}
is a two-sided module. Thus, whenever possible we will make use of the fact that
\mathcal{H}_{n}
is a right module. However, for Theorem
3.1.10
we will need to use the two-sided which will be utilised when proving the spectral theorem for a bounded self-adjoint operator in Section
6
.

Remark 2.2.3
.

It is easy to check that the following facts hold:

(2.10)

\displaystyle\langle x,x\rangle\succeq

\displaystyle\;0\quad{\rm and}\quad\langle x,x\rangle=0\Longleftrightarrow x=0

(2.11)

\displaystyle\langle x+y,z\rangle=

\displaystyle\;\langle x,y\rangle+\langle y,z\rangle,

(2.12)

\displaystyle\overline{\langle x,y\rangle}=

\displaystyle\;\langle y,x\rangle

(2.13)

\displaystyle\langle xa,y\rangle=

\displaystyle\;\langle x,y\rangle a\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad x,y,z\in\mathcal{H}_{n}\quad{\rm and}\quad a\in\mathbb{R}_{n}.

Furthermore, if we define

(2.14)

\|x\|:=\left(\sum_{\alpha}\|x_{\alpha}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}\right)^{1/2}\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad x=\sum_{\alpha}x_{\alpha}e_{\alpha},

then
\|\cdot\|
is a “norm” on
\mathcal{H}_{n}
, i.e., for all
x,y\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
and
a\in\mathbb{R}_{n}
, we have

(2.15)

\displaystyle\|x\|>

\displaystyle\;0\quad\quad\text{ whenever $x$ is nonzero,}

(2.16)

\displaystyle\|xa\|=

\displaystyle\;\|ax\|=|a|\cdot\|x\|\quad{\rm for}\quad a\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1},

(2.17)

\displaystyle\|xa\|=\|ax\|\leq

\displaystyle\;2^{n-1}|a|\|x\|\quad{\rm for}\quad a\in\mathbb{R}_{n}

(2.18)

\displaystyle\|x+y\|\leq

\displaystyle\;\|x\|+\|y\|.

Remark 2.2.4
.

In view of (
2.15
), (
2.16
) and (
2.18
), the fact that
\mathcal{H}
is complete and the definition of a Clifford module (see Definition
2.1.6
), it is very easy to see that
\mathcal{H}_{n}
together with
\|\cdot\|
can be viewed as a complex Banach space over
\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}
for any choice of
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathbb{S}
.

Definition 2.2.5

(orthonormal basis in
\mathcal{H}_{n}
)
.

We will call
(\xi_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{n}

linearly independent
if for any finite subset
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}\subseteq\mathcal{I}
, we have that the equation

\sum_{i\in\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}}\xi_{i}a_{i}=0,

where
(a_{i})_{i\in\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{n}
, only has the trivial solution, i.e.,
a_{i}=0
for all
i\in\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}
. Next, we will say that
\mathcal{B}:=(\xi_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{n}
is an
orthonormal basis
for
\mathcal{H}_{n}
if
\mathcal{B}
is linearly independent, the closed right linear span of
\mathcal{B}
is
\mathcal{H}_{n}
, i.e., every
x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
belongs to the closure (with respect to the norm topology on
\mathcal{H}_{n}
) of the set

\{\sum_{i\in\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}}\xi_{i}a_{i}:\text{$a_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{n}$ for all $i\in\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}$ with $\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}$ finite}\}

(in this case, we will utilise the short-hand
x=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\xi_{i}a_{i}
)
and

\langle\xi_{i},\xi_{j}\rangle=\begin{cases}1&{\rm if}\quad i=j,\\
0&{\rm if}\quad i\neq j.\end{cases}

While, it is not true that

\|x\|=\langle x,x\rangle^{1/2}\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad x\in\mathcal{H}_{n},

we do have the following facts which are reminiscent of the classical Hilbert space setting.

Lemma 2.2.6
.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{n}
be a Clifford module over
\mathbb{R}_{n}
. Then the following facts hold
:

(i)

\|x\|^{2}={\rm Re}\,\langle x,x\rangle
for
x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
.

(ii)

|\langle x,y\rangle|\leq\|x\|\,\|y\|
for
x,y\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
.

(iii)

Every Clifford module
\mathcal{H}_{n}
has an orthonormal basis. Moreover, if
\mathcal{B}:=(\xi_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
is a basis for
\mathcal{H}
, then
\mathcal{B}
is an orthonormal basis for
\mathcal{H}_{n}
.

(iv)

For any orthonormal basis
(\xi_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
, we have

(2.19)

x=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\xi_{i}\langle x,\xi_{i}\rangle.

Proof.

See, e.g., Proposition 1.9 in
[
55
]
for (i) and (ii). We will now prove (iii). Let
\mathcal{B}:=(\xi_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
be an orthonormal basis for the real Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. Then, in view of (
2.5
),
\mathcal{B}
is a basis for
\mathcal{H}_{n}
. In view of (
2.9
), we have

\langle\xi_{i},\xi_{j}\rangle=\langle\xi_{i},\xi_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=\begin{cases}1&{\rm if}\quad i=j\\
0&{\rm if}\quad i\neq j.\end{cases}

Thus,
\mathcal{B}
is an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{H}_{n}
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be as above. Since
\mathcal{B}
is a basis for the real Hilbert basis
\mathcal{H}
, we may use the classical orthonormal expansion for a real Hilbert space to obtain

\displaystyle x=

\displaystyle\;\sum_{\alpha}x_{\alpha}e_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\sum_{\alpha}\left(\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\langle x_{\alpha},\xi_{i}\rangle\right)e_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\sum_{\alpha}\xi_{i}\langle x_{\alpha},\xi_{i}\rangle e_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\sum_{\alpha}\xi_{i}\langle x_{\alpha}e_{\alpha},\xi_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\xi_{i}\langle x,\xi_{i}\rangle.

∎

In the following lemma, by
\dim\mathfrak{S}(\mathbb{R}_{n})
, we mean the dimension of the real vector space of self-adjoint Clifford numbers in
\mathbb{R}_{n}
(see Definition
2.1.5
). For example,
\dim\mathfrak{S}(\mathbb{R}_{0})=\dim\mathfrak{S}(\mathbb{R}_{1})=\dim\mathfrak{S}(\mathbb{R}_{2})=1
and
\dim\mathfrak{S}(\mathbb{R}_{3})=4
.

Lemma 2.2.7

(
[
38
]
, Polarisation formula for a Clifford module)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{n}
be a Clifford module over
\mathbb{R}_{n}
. Then

(2.20)

4\dim\mathfrak{S}(\mathbb{R}_{n})\langle x,y\rangle=\sum_{\alpha}(\langle x+ye_{\alpha},x+ye_{\alpha}\rangle-\langle x-ye_{\alpha},x-ye_{\alpha}\rangle)e_{\alpha}

for all
x,y\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
.

Lemma 2.2.8

(Parallelogram law)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{n}
be a Clifford module. Then for any
x,y\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
, we have

(2.21)

\|x\|^{2}+\|y\|^{2}=2\|x\|^{2}+2\|y\|^{2},

where
\|\cdot\|
is defined by (
2.14
).

Proof.

Since

\displaystyle\|x+y\|^{2}+\|x+y\|^{2}=

\displaystyle\;{\rm Re}(\langle x,x\rangle+\langle x,y\rangle+\langle y,x\rangle+\langle y,y\rangle)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\;{\rm Re}(\langle x,x\rangle-\langle x,y\rangle-\langle y,x\rangle+\langle y,y\rangle)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;2{\rm Re}\,\langle x,x\rangle+2{\rm Re}\,\langle y,y\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;2\|x\|^{2}+2\|y\|^{2},

we have (
2.21
).
∎

Definition 2.2.9
.

Let
K\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{n}
. We will call
K

convex
if for any choice of
x,y\in K
, we have

c\,x+(1-c)\,y\in K\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad 0\leq c\leq 1.

The next lemma provides a natural generalisation of the closed point in a closed convex subset property that holds for any complex Hilbert space.

Lemma 2.2.10
.

Let
K
be a non-empty, closed and convex subset of a Clifford module
\mathcal{H}_{n}
. For any
x\in K
, there exists a unique vector
y\in K
such that
\|x-y\|
is as small as possible.

Proof.

We begin by letting

m:=\inf_{z\in K}\|x-z\|.

Let
(y_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
be any minimising sequence for
x
, i.e.,

(2.22)

\lim_{i\to\infty}m_{i}=m\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad m_{i}:=\|x-y_{i}\|.

Next, if we apply (
2.21
) to the vectors
x=(x-y_{i})/2
and
y=(x-y_{j})/2
, we obtain

(2.23)

\left\|x-\frac{y_{i}+y_{j}}{2}\right\|^{2}+\left\|\frac{y_{i}-y_{j}}{2}\right\|^{2}=\frac{d_{i}^{2}+d_{j}^{2}}{2}.

Since
K
is a convex set, we must have that
(y_{i}+y_{j})/2\in K
. Consequently, we have
\|x-(y_{i}+y_{j})/2\|\geq m
. Thus, (
2.22
) and (
2.23
) imply that
(y_{i})_{i=0}^{\infty}
is a Cauchy sequence in
\mathcal{H}_{n}
. Thus, as
K
is closed, we have that

y:=\lim_{i\to\infty}y_{i}\in K.

Notice that
y
has the property that
\|x-y\|
is as small as possible, since

\|x-y\|=\lim_{i\to\infty}\|x-y_{i}\|=m.

Finally, suppose
y^{\prime}\in K
such that
\|x-y^{\prime}\|=m
. Then simply use (
2.21
) with
x-y
and
x-y^{\prime}
to deduce that
y=y^{\prime}
.
∎

Theorem 2.2.11
.

Let
Y
be a closed right submodule of a Clifford module
\mathcal{H}_{n}
and

Y^{\perp}:=\{x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}:\text{$\langle x,y\rangle=0$ for all $y\in Y$}\}.

Then the following statement hold
:

(i)

Y^{\perp}
is a closed right submodule of
\mathcal{H}_{n}
.

(ii)

\mathcal{H}_{n}=Y\oplus Y^{\perp}
.

(iii)

(Y^{\perp})^{\perp}
.

Proof.

We will first show (i). Fix any
y\in Y
and suppose
x,z\in Y^{\perp}
and
a\in\mathbb{R}_{n}
. Then

\displaystyle\langle xa+z,y\rangle=

\displaystyle\;\langle x,y\rangle a+\langle z,y\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;0+0=0.

Thus,
Y^{\perp}
is a right submodule of
\mathcal{H}_{n}
. Next, suppose
(x_{i})_{i=0}^{\infty}
is a convergent sequence, where
x_{i}\in Y^{\perp}
for
i=0,1,\ldots
and let

x:=\lim_{i\to\infty}x_{i}.

Then

\langle x,y\rangle=\langle x-x_{i},y\rangle+\langle x_{i},y\rangle=\langle x-x_{i},y\rangle

and hence for any
\epsilon>0
, we have

\displaystyle|\langle x,y\rangle|=

\displaystyle\;|\langle x-x_{i},y\rangle|\leq\|x-x_{i}\|\cdot\|y\|

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\;\varepsilon\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad\text{$i$ sufficiently large.}

But then we have
|\langle x,y\rangle|=0
, in which case
x\in Y^{\perp}
. Thus, (i) holds.

We will now prove (ii). Suppose
x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
is arbitrary. Then by Lemma
2.2.10
, we can find a unique vector
y\in Y
such that
\|x-y\|
is as small as possible. Consequently, if we put
z:=x-y
, then for any
v\in Y
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\|z\|^{2}\leq\|z+t\,v\|^{2}.

Thus,

\displaystyle\|z\|^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\;{\rm Re}\,\langle z+t\,v,z+t\,v\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;{\rm Re}\,\langle z,z\rangle+t({\rm Re}\langle z,v\rangle+{\rm Re}\,\langle v,z\rangle)+t^{2}{\rm Re}\,\langle v,v\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\|z\|^{2}+2t\,{\rm Re}\langle z,v\rangle+t^{2}\|v\|^{2},

in which case we have

(2.24)

{\rm Re}\,\langle z,v\rangle=0\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad v\in Y.

Since
Y
is a submodule, we can replace
y
by
ye_{\alpha}
in (
2.24
) and realise that
\langle z,v\rangle=0
for all
y\in Y
. Thus,
z\in Y^{\perp}
and we have the decomposition
\mathcal{H}_{n}=Y\oplus Y^{\perp}
.

To prove that the decomposition
\mathcal{H}_{n}=Y\oplus Y^{\perp}
is unique, suppose
x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
can be written as
x=y+z
and
x=y^{\prime}+z^{\prime}
, where
y,y^{\prime}\in Y
and
z,z^{\prime}\in Y^{\perp}
. But then
y-y^{\prime}=z-z^{\prime}
will simultaneously belong to
Y
and
Y^{\perp}
. Consequently,
\langle y-y^{\prime},y-y^{\prime}\rangle=0

forces
y=y^{\prime}
(see Definition
2.2.1
) and hence
z=z^{\prime}
.

Finally, we note that (iii) is a direct consequence of (ii).
∎

3.
Linear operators on Clifford modules

Definition 3.0.1

(linear operator on a Clifford module)
.

Let
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
denote the set of linear operators
\widetilde{T}:\mathcal{D}(\widetilde{T})\to\mathcal{H}
, where
\mathcal{D}(T)\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{n}
. The subspace
\mathcal{D}(\widetilde{T})\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{n}
will be called the
domain
of
\widetilde{T}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
. We shall let
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
denote the set of all operators
T:\mathcal{D}(T)\to\mathcal{H}_{n}
of the form

T=\sum_{\alpha}e_{\alpha}T_{\alpha}
, where
T_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
which acts on

\mathcal{D}(T)=\bigcap_{\alpha}\mathcal{D}(T_{\alpha})\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{n}
via

(3.1)

Tx=\sum_{\alpha,\beta}T_{\alpha}(x_{\beta})e_{\alpha}e_{\beta}\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad x=\sum_{\alpha}x_{\alpha}e_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{D}(T).

Remark 3.0.2
.

Let
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
. One can use (
3.1
) to check that for all
x,y\in\mathcal{D}(T)
and
a\in\mathbb{R}_{n}
, we have

(3.2)

\displaystyle T(xa+y)=

\displaystyle\;(Tx)a+Ty

Thus, in view of (
3.2
),
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
consists of right linear operators.

Definition 3.0.3

(kernel and range)
.

Given
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
, with domain of
T
denoted by
\mathcal{D}(T)
.
Then the range and kernel of
T
will be given by

{\rm Ran}\,\,T=\{\text{$y\in\mathcal{H}_{n}${\rm:} $Tx=y$ for $x\in\mathcal{D}(T)$}\}

and

{\rm Ker}\,\,T=\{\text{$x\in\mathcal{D}(T)${\rm:} $Tx=0$}\},

respectively.

Definition 3.0.4

(bounded linear operator on a Clifford module)
.

We will call an operator
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})

bounded
if

(3.3)

\mathcal{D}(T)=\mathcal{H}_{n}

and

(3.4)

\|T\|:=\sup_{\|x\|\leq 1}\|Tx\|<\infty\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}.

We will let
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
denote the set of all operators
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
such that (
3.3
) and (
3.4
) hold.

Remark 3.0.5
.

One can use (
2.15
), (
2.16
) and (
2.18
) and the definition of the operator norm on
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
(see (
3.4
)) to see that
\delta(T,W)=\|T-W\|
is a metric on
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
. One can proceed as in the classical case when
\mathcal{H}_{n}
is a complex Hilbert space to see that
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is complete with respect to the metric
\delta
.

Definition 3.0.6

(graph of a linear operator)
.

Suppose
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
. The
graph of
T
is the set

\mathcal{G}(T):=\{(x,Tx):x\in\mathcal{D}(T)\}.

Lemma 3.0.7
.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{n}
be a Clifford module over
\mathbb{R}_{n}
. A right submodule
\mathcal{K}
of
\mathcal{H}_{n}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{n}
satisfies

(3.5)

\mathcal{K}=\{(x,Tx):x\in\mathcal{D}(T)\},

for some
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
if and only if

(3.6)

(0,y)\in\mathcal{K}\Longrightarrow y=0.

Proof.

If
\mathcal{K}
is as in (
3.5
), then (
3.6
) obviously holds. Conversely, if (
3.6
) holds, then
(x,y)
and
(x,z)
belonging to
\mathcal{K}
implies that
y=z
, i.e., there exists a function
T:\mathcal{D}(T)\to\mathcal{H}_{n}
. The fact that
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
follows easily from the right linearity of
\mathcal{K}
. Thus, (
3.5
) holds.
∎

A very simple consequence of the polarisation formula (
2.20
) is the following lemma.

Lemma 3.0.8
.

Suppose
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is a densely defined operator. If

\langle Tx,x\rangle=0
for all
x\in\mathcal{D}(T)
, then
Tx=0
for all
x\in\mathcal{D}(T)
.

Lemma 3.0.9
.

Suppose
S,T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
such that
S\subseteq T
,
S
is surjective and
T
is injective. Then
S=T
.

Proof.

Fix
x\in\mathcal{D}(T)
. Thus, as
S
is surjective, we can find
y\in\mathcal{D}(S)
such that
Sy=Tx
. Since
S\subseteq T
, we have
Ty=Tx
. By the injectivity of
T
, we have
x=y
. Thus,
\mathcal{D}(T)\subseteq\mathcal{D}(S)
and hence
\mathcal{D}(T)=\mathcal{D}(S)
, in which case
S=T
.
∎

Definition 3.0.10

(graph norm)
.

Suppose
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
. It is easy to check that
\mathcal{D}(T)
is a right submodule of
\mathcal{H}_{n}
which can be endowed with
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{T}:\mathcal{D}(T)\times\mathcal{D}(T)\to\mathbb{R}_{n}
given by

(3.7)

\langle x,y\rangle_{T}:=\langle x,y\rangle+\langle Tx,Ty\rangle\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad x,y\in\mathcal{D}(T),

where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{T}
obeys (
2.10
)-(
2.13
)
and the corresponding norm

(3.8)

\|x\|_{T}:=(\|x\|^{2}+\|Tx\|^{2})^{1/2}\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad x\in\mathcal{D}(T).

Definition 3.0.11

(closed operator)
.

An operator
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is called
closed
if the set
\{(x,Tx):x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}\}
is a closed subset of
\mathcal{H}_{n}\times\mathcal{H}_{n}
(endowed with the product topology). Let
S
and
T
both belong
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
. We will write
S=T
if
\mathcal{D}(S)=\mathcal{D}(T)
and
Sx=Tx
for all
x\in\mathcal{D}(S)=\mathcal{D}(T)
. We will write
S\subseteq T
if
\mathcal{D}(S)\subseteq\mathcal{D}(T)
and
Sx=Tx
for all
x\in\mathcal{D}(S)
. Clearly,
S=T
if and only if
S\subseteq T
and
T\subseteq S
. An operator
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is called
closable
if there exists a closed operator
X\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
so that
T\subseteq X
.

Theorem 3.0.12
.

Let
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
. The follow statements are equivalent
:

(i)

T
is closed.

(ii)

For any sequence
(x_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
, with
x_{i}\in\mathcal{D}(T)
for
i=1,2,\ldots
, such that

\lim_{i\to\infty}x_{i}=x,

where
x\in\mathcal{D}(T)
, and

\lim_{i\to\infty}T(x_{n})=y,

where
y\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
, we have
Tx=y
.

(iii)

\mathcal{D}(T)
together with
\|\cdot,\cdot\|_{T}

(
see (
3.11
)
)
is a complete normed right module over
\mathbb{R}_{n}
.

Proof.

In view of Definition
3.0.11
,
{\rm(i)}\Longleftrightarrow{\rm(ii)}
is immediate. We will now show
{\rm(i)}\Longleftrightarrow{\rm(iii)}
. In view of (
3.8
),
\mathcal{D}(T)
together with
\|\cdot\|_{T}
is a complete normed right module over
\mathbb{R}_{n}
if and only if
\mathcal{G}(T)
is complete, i.e.,
\mathcal{G}(T)
is a closed.
∎

Theorem 3.0.13
.

Let
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
. The following statements are equivalent
:

(i)

T
is closable.

(ii)

\overline{\{(x,Tx):x\in\mathcal{D}(T)\}}=\{(x,Wx):\text{for some operator $W\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})$}\}
.

(iii)

For any sequence
(x_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
, where
x_{i}\in\mathcal{D}(T)
for
i=1,2,\ldots
, such that

\lim_{i\to\infty}x_{i}=0

and

\lim_{i\to\infty}T(x_{i})=y,

where
y\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
, then
y=0
.

Proof.

We will first show
{\rm(i)}\Longrightarrow{\rm(ii)}
. If
S\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is any closed operator such that
T\subseteq S
, then

\{(x,Tx):x\in\mathcal{D}(T)\}\subseteq\{(x,Sx):x\in\mathcal{D}(S)\}.

Hence, as
S
is closed,

\overline{\{(x,Tx):x\in\mathcal{D}(T)\}}\subseteq\{(x,Sx):x\in\mathcal{D}(S)\}.

Therefore, in view of Lemma
3.0.7
, (ii) holds.

We will now show
{\rm(ii)}\Longrightarrow{\rm(i)}
. If (ii) holds, then
T\subseteq W
and hence
W
is closed since

\{(x,Wx):x\in\mathcal{D}(W)\}

is closed. Thus,
T
is closable.

The proof of
{\rm(ii}\Longrightarrow{\rm(iii)}
follows immediately from the Lemma
3.0.7
and the fact that
(0,y)\in\overline{\mathcal{G}(T)}
implying that
y=0
.

∎

Definition 3.0.14
.

Let
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
be closable. We let

\overline{T}x:=\lim_{i\to\infty}T(x_{i})

denote the operator in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
with domain

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(T)=

\displaystyle\;\{x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}:x=\lim_{i\to\infty}i_{n}\ \text{for $(x_{i})_{n=0}^{\infty}\subseteq\mathcal{D}(T)$ and $\{T(x_{i})\}_{i=0}^{\infty}$}

\displaystyle\;\text{converges in $\mathcal{H}_{n}$}\}.

In view of Theorem
3.0.13
, the definition of
\overline{T}
is independent of the choice of sequence
(x_{i})_{i=0}^{\infty}
. Note that for any closed operator
W\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
such that
T\subseteq W
,

\overline{T}\subseteq W.

Definition 3.0.15

(continuous operator)
.

Let
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
.
T
will be called
continuous
if for any sequence
(x_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
, where
x_{i}\in\mathcal{D}(T)
for
i=1,2,\ldots
, such that

\lim_{i\to\infty}x_{i}=x,

where
x\in\mathcal{D}(T)
, we have

\lim_{i\to\infty}T\,x_{i}=Tx.

Theorem 3.0.16
.

Let
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
. Then
T
is continuous if and only if
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
.

Proof.

The proof can be carried out as in the classical complex Hilbert space case, see, e.g.,
[
51
]
.
∎

Definition 3.0.17
.

Let
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
. A subset
\mathcal{E}
of
\mathcal{D}(T)
will be called
core
of
\mathcal{D}(T)
if
\mathcal{E}
is a right submodule of
\mathcal{D}(T)
and
\mathcal{E}
is dense in
(\mathcal{D}(T),\|\cdot\|_{T})
.

Definition 3.0.18

(adjoint operator)
.

Given
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
which is densely defined, we let
T^{*}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
denote the unique operator so that

\langle Tx,y\rangle=\langle x,T^{*}y\rangle,\quad x\in\mathcal{D}(T),y\in\mathcal{D}(T^{*}),

where the domain of
T^{*}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(T^{*})=

\displaystyle\;\{\text{$y\in\mathcal{H}_{n}$ : there exists $z\in\mathcal{H}_{n}$ with $\langle Tx,y\rangle=\langle x,z\rangle$}

\displaystyle\text{for every $x\in\mathcal{D}(T)$}\}.

Remark 3.0.19
.

Let
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
. Then, in view of Definition
3.0.18
, it is easy to check that
(T^{*})^{*}=T
.

Lemma 3.0.20
.

Let
T,W\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
. Then
TW\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
,

(3.9)

\|TW\|\leq\|T\|\cdot\|W\|

and

(3.10)

\|T^{*}T\|=\|T^{*}\|^{2}=\|T\|^{2}.

Proof.

The fact that
TW\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
, (
3.9
) and (
3.10
) can be proved exactly as in the classical complex Banach algebra case, see, e.g., Theorem 8 on page 168 of
[
51
]
for a proof of the classical complex Hilbert space case of (
3.9
) and
[
28
]
for a classical complex Hilbert space case of (
3.10
).
∎

Definition 3.0.21

(self-adjoint, anti self-adjoint and unitary)
.

Let
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
. We will call
T

self-adjoint
,
anti self-adjoint
and
unitary
if
T=T^{*}
with
\mathcal{D}(T)=\mathcal{D}(T^{*})
,
T=-T^{*}
with
\mathcal{D}(T)=\mathcal{D}(T^{*})
and
TT^{*}=T^{*}T=I
, respectively.

Remark 3.0.22
.

In view of Theorem
3.0.23
(i), we have that if
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is self-adjoint or anti self-adjoint operator, then
T
is closed.

Theorem 3.0.23
.

If
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is densely defined and
W\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
, then
:

(i)

T^{*}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is closed.

(ii)

{\rm{\rm Ran}\,}(T)^{\perp}={\rm Ker}(T^{*})
.

(iii)

If
T\subseteq W
, then
W^{*}\subseteq T^{*}
.

Proof.

The proofs can completed in much the same way as the case when
\mathcal{H}_{n}
is a complex Hilbert space (see, e.g., Proposition 1.6 in
[
66
]
).
∎

Theorem 3.0.24
.

If
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is densely defined, then
:

(i)

T
is closable if and only if
\mathcal{D}(T^{*})
is dense in
\mathcal{H}_{n}
.

(ii)

If
T
is closable, then
\overline{T}=T^{**}
, where
T^{**}:=(T^{*})^{*}
.

(iii)

T
is closed if and only if
T=T^{**}
.

(iv)

If
T
is closable and
{\rm Ker}(T)=\{0\}
and
{\rm Ran}\,(T)
is dense in
\mathcal{H}_{n}
, then
T^{*}
is invertible and
(T^{*})^{-1}=(T^{-1})^{*}
.

(v)

If
T
is closable and
{\rm Ker}\,(T)=\{0\}
, then
T^{-1}
is closable if and only if
{\rm Ker}\,(\overline{T})=\{0\}
. In this case, we have
(\overline{T})^{-1}=\overline{T^{-1}}
.

(vi)

Suppose
T
is invertible. Then
T
is closed if and only if
T^{-1}
is closed.

Proof.

The proofs can completed in much the same way as the case when
\mathcal{H}_{n}
is a complex Hilbert space (see, e.g., Theorem 1.8 in
[
66
]
).
∎

Lemma 3.0.25
.

Let
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
be a densely defined operator. Then the graph of
T^{*}
satisfies

(3.11)

\mathcal{G}(T^{*})=V(\mathcal{G}(T))^{\perp}=V(\mathcal{G}(T)^{\perp}),

where
V:\mathcal{H}_{n}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{n}\to\mathcal{H}_{n}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{n}
denotes the unitary operator given by

V(x,y)=(-y,x).

Proof.

Suppose
x\in\mathcal{D}(T)
and
y\in\mathcal{D}(T^{*})
. Using Definition
3.0.18
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\langle V(x,Tx),(y,T^{*}y)\rangle=

\displaystyle\;\langle(-Tx,x),(y,T^{*}y)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\langle-Tx,y\rangle+\langle x,T^{*}y\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;0,

in which case we have
\mathcal{G}(T^{*})\subseteq V(\mathcal{G}(T))^{\perp}
.

Conversely, suppose
(y,z)\in V(\mathcal{G}(T))^{\perp}
. Then for any
x\in\mathcal{D}(T)
, we have

\langle V(x,Tx),(y,z)\rangle=\langle-Tx,y\rangle+\langle x,z\rangle=0,

and hence
\langle Tx,y\rangle=\langle x,z\rangle
. But then we have again use Definition
3.0.18
to obtain
y\in\mathcal{D}(T^{*})
and
z=T^{*}y
, i.e.,
(y,z)\in\mathcal{G}(T^{*})
. Therefore, we have
V(\mathcal{G}(T))^{\perp}\subseteq\mathcal{G}(T^{*})
.

The second equality is an immediate consequence of
V
being unitary on
\mathcal{H}_{n}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{n}
.
∎

Theorem 3.0.26

(Riesz representation theorem for Clifford modules)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{n}
be a Clifford module over
\mathbb{R}_{n}
. Suppose
B:\mathcal{H}_{n}\times\mathcal{H}_{n}\to\mathbb{R}_{n}
is bounded, i.e., there exists
M\geq 0
such that

|B(x,y)|\leq M\|x\|\|y\|\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad x,y\in\mathcal{H}_{n},

and satisfies the following
:

(i)

B(x+y,z)=B(x,z)+B(y,z)
and
B(x,y+z)=B(x,y)+B(x,z)
for
x,y,z\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
.

(ii)

B(x\,a,y)=B(x,y)a
and
B(x,y\,a)=\bar{a}\,B(x,y)
for
x,y\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
and
a\in\mathbb{R}_{n}
.

Then there exists a unique
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
such that

(3.12)

B(x,y)=\langle Tx,y\rangle\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad x,y\in\mathcal{H}_{n}.

Proof.

The proof of Corollary 1.11 in
[
55
]
can easily be adjusted to our present right Clifford module setting.
∎

The following theorem appears in
[
55
]
.

Theorem 3.0.27

(Hahn-Banach theorem for a Clifford module)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{n}
be a Clifford module over
\mathbb{R}_{n}
and
\mathcal{S}_{n}
be a right submodule of
\mathcal{H}_{n}
. Suppose
\mathscr{L}:\mathcal{S}_{n}\to\mathbb{R}_{n}
is continuous (i.e., bounded) and satisfies

(3.13)

\mathscr{L}(xa+y)=\mathscr{L}(x)a+\mathscr{L}(y)\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad x,y\in\mathcal{S}_{n}\quad{\rm and}\quad a\in\mathbb{R}_{n}.

Then
\mathscr{L}
has a continuous extension
\mathscr{L}:\mathcal{H}_{n}\to\mathbb{R}_{n}

(
with a slight abuse of notation, we shall use
\mathscr{L}
to denote the extension
)
such that (
3.13
) holds for all
x,y\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
and
a\in\mathbb{R}_{n}
.

Theorem 3.0.28

(closed graph theorem for a Clifford module)
.

Suppose
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is a closed operator with
\mathcal{D}(T)=\mathcal{H}_{n}
. Then
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
.

Proof.

The proof given in Theorem 2.2.7 in
[
62
]
can easily be adjusted to the present Clifford module setting.
∎

Lemma 3.0.29
.

Suppose
C\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is invertible in
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
. Then the following statements hold
:

(i)

C-D
is invertible in
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
whenever

\|D\|<\frac{1}{\|C^{-1}\|}.

(ii)

Suppose
C
is invertible in
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
and
\|C-D\|<\|C^{-1}\|^{-1}
. Then
D
is invertible in
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
.

Proof.

The proof of (i) and (ii) is exactly the same as in the classical case, see, e.g., Theorem 2 in Chapter 17 of
[
51
]
for the proof of (i).
∎

Definition 3.0.30

(normal operator on a Clifford module)
.

Suppose
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
. We will call
T

normal
if
T
is densely defined,
T
is closed,
\mathcal{D}(T)=\mathcal{D}(T^{*})
and
TT^{*}=T^{*}T
.

Lemma 3.0.31
.

Let
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
be normal. If
S\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
so that
T\subseteq S
and
\mathcal{D}(S)\subseteq D(S^{*})
, then
S=T
.

Proof.

If
T\subseteq S
, then
S^{*}\subseteq T^{*}
and hence

\mathcal{D}(T)\subseteq\mathcal{D}(S)\subseteq\mathcal{D}(S^{*})\subseteq\mathcal{D}(T^{*})=\mathcal{D}(T),

i.e.,
\mathcal{D}(S)=\mathcal{D}(T)
. Therefore,
S=T
.
∎

3.1.
S
-resolvent set,
S
-spectrum for a linear operator and the spectral radius formula

Definition 3.1.1

(
S
-resolvent set and
S
-spectrum for
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
)
.

Let

T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
be densely defined and
\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T):\mathcal{D}(T^{2})\to\mathcal{H}
be given by

(3.14)

\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T)x=(T^{2}-2{\rm Re}(s)T+|s|^{2}I)x\quad{\rm for}\quad x\in\mathcal{D}(T^{2}).

The
S
-resolvent set of
T
is defined as follows

\displaystyle\rho_{S}(T)=

\displaystyle\;\{\text{$s\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}$ {\rm:} ${\rm Ker}\,\,\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T)=\{0\}$, ${\rm Ran}\,\,\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T)$ is dense in $\mathcal{H}_{n}$ and}

\displaystyle\;\text{$\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T)^{-1}:{\rm Ran}\,\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T)\to\mathcal{D}(T^{2}$) is bounded}\}.

The
S
-spectrum is defined as

\sigma_{S}(T)=\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\,\setminus\,\rho_{S}(T).

Remark 3.1.2
.

In
[
27
]
, the
S
-spectrum for
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
was considered
for paravector operators, i.e., operators of the form

T=\sum_{i=0}^{n}e_{i}T_{i},

because the purpose of this theory was to define
a functional calculus for
(n+1)
-
tuples of noncommuting operators.
Quite early on in our investigation of the Clifford spectral theorem, we directed our attention to fully Clifford operators because of the crucial decomposition

(3.15)

T=A+J_{0}B.

of normal bounded operators given in Theorem
8.0.4
.
This decomposition implies that, even if
T
is a paravector operator,
the operators
J_{0}
and
B
will not be paravector operators in general.

A further observation is that
in the case
T
is a paravector operator with noncommuting components
the
S
-spectrum is defined by
the operator
T^{2}-2{\rm Re}(s)T+|s|^{2}I
that is not a paravector.

For an explanation of why this theory is so flexible see the introduction of the paper
[
18
]
,
where some considerations are made on various Cauchy formulas that
define various holomorphic functional calculi.
The properties of the
S
-spectrum and the
S
-resolvent operators
for fully Clifford operators
remain the same with the same proofs valid for
paravector operators, see
[
17
,
18
,
37
]
.

Remark 3.1.3
.

For any
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
, it is easy to show that the
S
-resolvent set is equal to

\displaystyle\rho_{S}(T)=\{s\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}:\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T)^{-1}:

\displaystyle\;{\rm Ran}\,\,\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T)\to\mathcal{D}(T^{2})

(3.16)

\displaystyle\;\text{is bijective and bounded}\}

Definition 3.1.4

(left and right
S
-resolvent operator)
.

Suppose
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
. For any choice of
s\in\rho_{S}(T)
, we shall define the
left
S
-resolvent operator
via

(3.17)

S_{L}^{-1}(s,T):=-\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T)^{-1}(T-\bar{s}\,I)\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})

and the
right
S
-resolvent operator
via

(3.18)

S_{R}^{-1}(s,T):=-(T-\bar{s}\,I)\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T)^{-1}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n}).

Remark 3.1.5
.

One can check that
S_{L}^{-1}(s,T):\rho_{S}(T)\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is a
right slice hyperholomorphic function
(see Lemma 3.10 in
[
17
]
for details). Analogously, one can check that
S_{R}^{-1}(s,T):\rho_{S}(T)\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is a
left slice hyperholomorphic function
.

Definition 3.1.6

(axially symmetric)
.

We will call a set
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n+1}

axially symmetric
, if whenever
s=s_{0}+s_{1}\mathfrak{I}\in\Omega
, where
s_{0},s_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathbb{S}
, then
s_{0}+s_{1}\mathfrak{J}\in\Omega
for all
\mathfrak{J}\in\mathbb{S}
.

Remark 3.1.7

(the
S
-spectrum is axially symmetric)
.

Let
T\in\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
. Then
\sigma_{S}(T)
is axially symmetric. If
\sigma_{S}(T)=\emptyset
, then there is nothing to prove. If
\sigma_{S}(T)\not=\emptyset
, then one need only notice that
s\in\rho_{S}(T)
depends only on
{\rm Re}(s)
and
|s|
.

Theorem 3.1.8

(properties of the
S
-spectrum for
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
)
.

Suppose

T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
. Then
\sigma_{S}(T)
is a non-empty compact subset of

\{s\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}:0\leq|s|\leq\|T\|\}.

Proof.

We will first show that
\sigma_{S}(T)
is non-empty. For any choice of
\varepsilon>0
, the series
\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}T^{i}s^{-i-1}
converges uniformly in norm on
s\in\Omega_{\varepsilon}:=\{a\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}:|a|=\|T\|+\varepsilon\}
to
S_{L}^{-1}(s,T)
. Thus, for any
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathbb{S}
, we may use the fact that

\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}}s^{-i-1}\,ds(-\mathfrak{I})=\begin{cases}2\pi&{\rm if}\quad i=0,\\
0&{\rm if}\quad i\neq 0,\end{cases}

to obtain

(3.19)

\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}S_{L}^{-1}(s,T)ds(-\mathfrak{I})=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}T^{i}\left(\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}s^{-i-1}ds(-\mathfrak{I})\right)=2\pi\,I.

If
M_{\varepsilon}:=\overline{\{a\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}:|a|\leq\|T\|+\varepsilon\}}\subseteq\rho_{S}(T)
, then Remark
3.1.5
asserts that
S_{L}^{-1}(s,T)|_{M_{\varepsilon}}
is a right slice hyperholomorphic
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
-valued function. However, an analogue of Cauchy’s integral formula for
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
-valued functions asserts that (
3.19
) must be
0
, which is clearly not the case. Thus,
M_{\varepsilon}
is not a subset of
\rho_{S}(T)
. But then
\sigma_{S}(T)
cannot be empty.

We will now show that
\sigma_{S}(T)
is a closed subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. Notice that
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
given by
\varphi(s)=\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T)
is a continuous function. Lemma
3.0.29
can be used to show that the set of invertible operators in
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is open. Thus,
\rho_{S}(T)=\varphi^{-1}(\{W\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n}):W^{-1}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})\})
is open. Consequently,
\sigma_{S}(T)=\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\setminus\rho_{S}(T)
is closed.

Next, we will show that
\sigma_{S}(T)\subseteq\{s\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}:|s|\leq\|T\|\}
. In view of (
3.9
), we have
\|T^{i}\|\leq\|T\|^{i}
for
i=0,1,\ldots
. Thus, as
s\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, we have the estimate

\|T^{i}s^{-i-1}\|\leq 2^{n-1}|s^{-i-1}|\cdot\|T^{i}\|=\frac{\|T\|^{i}}{|s|^{i+1}}\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad i=0,1,\ldots

and hence

(3.20)

\text{$\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\|T^{i}s^{-i-1}\|$ converges if and only if $|s|<\|T\|$}.

We now claim that

(3.21)

(T^{2}-2{\rm Re}(s)T+|s|^{2}\,I)\left(\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}T^{i}s^{-i-1}\right)=\bar{s}\,I-T.

Indeed, notice that

\displaystyle\;(T^{2}-2{\rm Re}(s)T+|s|^{2}\,I)\left(\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}T^{i}s^{-i-1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\left(T^{i+1}s^{-i-1}-T^{i+1}s^{-i-1}(s+\bar{s})+T^{i}s^{-i-1}s\bar{s}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\left(T^{i+1}s^{-i}-T^{i+1}s^{-i}-T^{i+1}s^{-i-1}\bar{s}+T^{i}s^{-i}\bar{s}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\bar{s}\,I-T.

Thus, we have (
3.21
). Putting together (
3.20
) and (
3.21
), we have that
\sigma_{S}(T)\subseteq\{s\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}:|s|\leq\|T\|\}
. As
\sigma_{S}(T)
is closed, we have that is a closed subset of a compact set, i.e.,
\sigma_{S}(T)
is compact.

Finally, we will show that
\sigma_{S}(T)
is closed. Suppose
s\in\rho_{S}(T)
and
\tilde{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
be such that
|s-\tilde{s}|
is sufficiently small. Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T)-\mathcal{Q}_{\tilde{s}}(T)=

\displaystyle\;2{\rm Re}(\tilde{s}-s)T+(|s|^{2}-|\tilde{s}|^{2})I

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\frac{1}{|s|^{2}-|\tilde{s}|^{2}}\left(\frac{2{\rm Re}(\tilde{s}-s)}{|s|^{2}-|\tilde{s}|^{2}}\,T+I\right),

one can use Lemma
5.3.11
to see that
\mathcal{Q}_{\tilde{s}}
is invertible in
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
. Thus,
\tilde{s}\in\rho_{S}(T)
and
\rho_{S}(T)
is open, i.e.,
\sigma_{S}(T)
is closed.
∎

Definition 3.1.9

(spectral radius)
.

Let
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
. Then we shall denote the
spectral radius
of
T
by
r_{S}(T)
which is given by

(3.22)

r_{S}(T):=\sup_{s\in\sigma_{S}(T)}|s|=\max_{s\in\sigma_{S}(T)}|s|.

Theorem 3.1.10

(spectral radius formula)
.

Let
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
be normal. Then

(3.23)

\|T\|=r_{S}(T).

Proof.

Theorem 6.7 in [
[
17
]
asserts, in particular, that for any
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
, we have

\lim_{i\to\infty}(\|T^{i}\|_{1})^{\frac{1}{i}}=r_{S}(T),

where

\|T\|_{1}:=\sum_{\alpha}\|T_{\alpha}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad T=\sum_{\alpha}T_{\alpha}e_{\alpha}.

Notice that

\displaystyle\|T\|:=

\displaystyle\;\sup_{\|x\|=1}\|Tx\|=\sup_{\|x\|=1}\|\sum_{\alpha,\beta}T_{\alpha}x_{\beta}e_{\alpha}e_{\beta}\|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;\sup_{\|x\|=1}\sum_{\alpha,\beta}\|T_{\alpha}x_{\beta}e_{\alpha}e_{\beta}\|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;2^{n-1}\sup_{\|x\|=1}\sum_{\alpha,beta}\|T_{\alpha}x_{\beta}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\cdot|e_{\alpha}e_{\beta}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;2^{n-1}2^{n-1}\sum_{\alpha,\beta}\left(\sup_{\|x\|=1}\|T_{\alpha}x_{\beta}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;2^{2(n-1)}\sum_{\alpha,\beta}\|T_{\alpha}\|_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;2^{3n-2}\|T\|_{1}.

In view of (
3.10
), one can use induction on
i
to show that

\|T^{2^{i}}\|^{\frac{1}{2^{i}}}=\|T\|
. In view of Theorem
3.1.8
, we have
r_{S}(T)\leq\|T\|
. Thus,

r_{S}(T)\leq\|T\|=\|T^{2^{i}}\|^{\frac{1}{2i}}\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad i=1,2,\ldots

and hence

\displaystyle r_{S}(T)\leq

\displaystyle\;\lim_{i\to\infty}\|T^{2^{i}}\|^{\frac{1}{2i}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\lim_{i\to\infty}\left(2^{\frac{3n-2}{i}}(\|T^{2^{i}}\|_{1})^{\frac{1}{2^{i}}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;r_{S}(T).

and hence we have (
3.23
).
∎

Following the possible splittings of the classical spectrum for operators on complex Banach space we can give the same spitting also for fully linear operators on Clifford modules.
We recall that the splitting of the spectrum is defined according to where an operators is not invertible.
We will mention two possible splittings. The first one is the point, residual, continuous
S
-spectrum of a Clifford operator.

Definition 3.1.11

(point, residual, continuous
S
-spectrum)
.

Let
T:\mathcal{D}(T)\to\mathcal{H}_{n}
. We split the
S
-spectrum into the three disjoint sets:

(P)

The point
S
-spectrum of
T
:

\sigma_{PS}(T)=\{s\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\ :\ \ {\rm Ker}(\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T))\not=\{0\}\}.

(R)

The residual
S
-spectrum of
T
:

\sigma_{RS}(T)=\left\{s\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\ :\ \ {\rm Ker}(\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T))=\{0\},\ \overline{{\rm Ran}(\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T))}\not=\mathcal{H}_{n}\right\}.

(C)

The continuous
S
-spectrum of
T
:

\displaystyle\sigma_{CS}(T)=

\displaystyle\;\{s\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\ :\ {\rm Ker}(\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T))=\{0\},\ \overline{{\rm Ran}(\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T))}=\mathcal{H}_{n}

\displaystyle\;\;{\rm and}\;\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T)^{-1}\not\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})\}.

Remark 3.1.12
.

Notice that if
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
that satisfies the two conditions:

(i)

There exists
K>0
such that
\|Av\|\geq K\|v\|
for
v\in\mathcal{D}(A)
(bounded from below)

(ii)

the range of
A
is dense in
\mathcal{H}_{n}
,

then
A
is invertible.

So in analogy to the classical case for the
S
-spectrum we have:

Definition 3.1.13

(approximate point and compression
S
-spectrum)
.

Let
T
be a Clifford bounded linear operator.
The approximate point
S
-spectrum of
T
, denoted by
\Pi_{S}(T)
, is defined as

\Pi_{S}(T)=\{s\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\ :\ T^{2}-2{\rm Re}(s)T+|s|^{2}\mathcal{I}\text{ is not bounded from below}\}.

The compression
S
-spectrum of
T
, denoted by
\Gamma_{S}(T)
, is defined as

\Gamma_{S}(T)=\{s\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\ :\ \text{ the range of }\ T^{2}-2{\rm Re}T+|s|^{2}\mathcal{I}\text{ is not dense}\}.

The set
\Pi_{S}(T)
contains the
S
-eigenvalues.

3.2.
Basic facts for normal operators

In this section we are defining self-adjoint, anti self-adjoint operators and positive operators on a Clifford module. We are also formulating and proving a number of facts which will be useful when proving the spectral theorem for a bounded self-adjoint operator (see Section
6
) and also the spectral theorem for an unbounded normal operator (see
10
).

For the quaternionic setting similar properties hold, see the book

[
16
]
and the references therein.

Lemma 3.2.1
.

Let
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
be given. The following statements are equivalent
:

(i)

T
is self-adjoint.

(ii)

\overline{\langle Tx,x\rangle}=\langle Tx,x\rangle
for all
x\in\mathcal{D}(T)
.

Proof.

If
T
is self-adjoint, then

\langle Tx,x\rangle=\langle x,Tx\rangle\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad x\in\mathcal{D}(T)

and hence

(3.24)

\overline{\langle x,Tx\rangle}=\langle Tx,x\rangle\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad x\in\mathcal{D}(T).

On the other hand, if (ii) is in force, then (
2.20
) can be used to show that
\langle Tx,y\rangle=\langle x,Ty\rangle
for all
x,y\in\mathcal{D}(T)
. Thus, (i) holds.
∎

Definition 3.2.2

(positive operator on a Clifford module)
.

Let
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
be given. The operator
T
is called
positive
if
\langle Tx,x\rangle\succeq 0
for all
x\in\mathcal{D}(T)
.

The following theorem will be useful when considering the unbounded case of the spectral theorem for normal operators.

Theorem 3.2.3
.

Suppose
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is a densely defined closed operator. Then the following statement hold
:

(i)

I+T^{*}T
is a bijective mapping on
\mathcal{H}_{n}
. If
C_{T}:=(I+T^{*}T)^{-1}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
, then
C_{T}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
,
C_{T}
is positive and
I-C_{T}
is positive.

(ii)

The operator
T^{*}T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is positive and
\mathcal{D}(T^{*}T)
is a core for
T
. In particular, if
T
is self-adjoint, then
\mathcal{D}(T^{2})
is a core for
T
.

Proof.

We will first prove (i). In view of Lemma
3.0.25
, we have
\mathcal{G}(T^{*})=V(\mathcal{G}(T))^{\perp}
, where
V
denotes the unitary operator in the statement of Lemma
3.0.25
. Notice that
\mathcal{H}_{n}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{n}=\mathcal{G}(T^{*})\oplus V(\mathcal{G}(T))
. Consequently, corresponding to every
z\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
, there exist
x\in\mathcal{D}(T)
and
y\in\mathcal{D}(T^{*})
such that

(0,z)=(y,T^{*}y)=V(x,Tx)=(y-Tx,T^{*}y+x).

Thus,
y=Tx
and
z=x+T^{*}y=(I+T^{*}T)x
, in which case the operator
I+T^{*}T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is surjective. To see that
I+T^{*}T
is injective, notice that for any
x,y\in\mathcal{D}(T^{*}T)
, we have

\displaystyle\|(I+T^{*}T)(x-y)\|^{2}=

\displaystyle\;{\rm Re}\langle(x-y)+T^{*}T(x-y),(x-y)+T^{*}T(x-y)\rangle

(3.25)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;{\rm Re}\,\langle x-y,x-y\rangle+2{\rm Re}\,\langle T(x-y),T(x-y)\rangle

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\;{\rm Re}\,\langle T^{*}T(x-y),T^{*}T(x-y)\rangle

(3.26)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\|x-y\|^{2}+2\|T(x-y)\|^{2}+\|T^{*}T(x-y)\|^{2}.

Thus, as
I+T^{*}T
is injective and surjective, we have that
I+T^{*}T
is bijective and let
C_{T}:=(I+T^{*}T)^{-1}
.

Suppose
z=(I+T^{*}T)x
for some
x\in\mathcal{D}(T^{*}T)
. Then
C_{T}z=x
and one can use (
3.26
) with
y=0
to obtain

\|C_{T}z\|=\|x\|\leq\|(I+T^{*}T)x\|=\|z\|

and hence
C_{T}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
. The fact that
C_{T}
is self-adjoint is an immediate consequence of

\langle C_{T}z,z\rangle=\langle x,z\rangle=\langle x,(I+T^{*}T)x\rangle=\langle(I+T^{*}T)x,x\rangle.

The remaining conclusions in (i) can be easily justified via (
3.26
).

We will now prove (ii). Since
C_{T}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is self-adjoint, we have that
C_{T}^{-1}
is also self-adjoint by Theorem
3.0.24
(iv), i.e.,
I+T^{*}T
is self-adjoint. The fact that
T^{*}T
is positive follows from

\langle T^{*}Tx,x\rangle=\langle Tx,Tx\rangle\succeq 0\quad{\rm for}\quad x\in\mathcal{D}(T^{*}T).

To check that
\mathcal{D}(T^{*}T)
is a core for
T
, we must check that
\mathcal{D}(T^{*}T)
is dense in the Clifford module
(\mathcal{D}(T),\|\cdot\|_{T})
. If
y\in\mathcal{D}(T)
satisfies
\langle y,x\rangle_{T}=0
for all
x\in\mathcal{D}(T^{*}T)
, then realise that

0=\langle y,x\rangle+\langle Ty,Tx\rangle=\langle y,(I+T^{*}T)x\rangle

for every
x\in\mathcal{D}(T^{*}T)
. Consequently,
y=0
since
{\rm Ran}\,(I+T^{*}T)=\mathcal{H}_{n}
, in which case we have that
\mathcal{D}(T^{*}T)
is a core for
T
.

The second assertion is very obvious.
∎

Lemma 3.2.4
.

Let
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
be self-adjoint. Then
\sigma_{S}(T)\subseteq\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

In order to show that
\sigma_{S}(T)\subseteq\mathbb{R}
, it suffices to show that

\rho_{S}(T)\subseteq\{s\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}:{\rm Re}(s)=0\}
. Let
s=s_{0}+s_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, where
s_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
and
s_{1}\in{\rm Im}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})\setminus\{0\}
. Notice that

\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T):=T^{2}-2{\rm Re}(s)T+|s|^{2}I=(T-s_{0}\,I)^{2}+|s_{1}|^{2}I

and hence
\mathcal{D}(T^{2})=\mathcal{D}((T-s_{0}\,I)^{2})
and as
T
is self-adjoint, we have that
(T-s_{0}\,I)^{2}+|s|^{2}I
is self-adjoint. Moreover, in view of the second assertion in Theorem
3.2.3
(ii), we have that
\mathcal{D}(T^{2})
is dense in
\mathcal{H}_{n}
. We claim that
{\rm Re}(\langle(T-s_{0}\,I)^{2}\,x,x\rangle)\geq 0
for all
x\in\mathcal{D}(T^{2})
. Indeed,

\displaystyle{\rm Re}(\langle(T-s_{0}\,I)^{2}x,x\rangle=

\displaystyle\;{\rm Re}(\langle(T-s_{0}\,I)x,(T-s_{0}\,I)x\rangle)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\|(T-s_{0}\,I)x\|^{2}\geq 0\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}.

Notice that

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T)x\|^{2}=

\displaystyle\;{\rm Re}\langle\{(T-s_{0}\,I)^{2}+|s_{1}|^{2}I\}x,\{(T-s_{0}\,I)^{2}+|s_{1}|^{2}\,I\}x\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\|(T-s_{0}\,I)^{2}x\|^{2}+2{\rm Re}\langle(T-s_{0}\,I)^{2}x,|s_{1}|^{2}x\rangle+|s_{1}|^{4}\|x\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\|(T-s_{0}\,I)^{2}x\|^{2}+2|s_{1}|^{2}\|(T-s_{0}\,I)x\|^{2}+|s_{1}|^{4}\|x\|^{2}

(3.27)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\;|s_{1}|^{4}\|x\|^{2}\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad x\in\mathcal{D}(T^{2})

and hence
\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T)^{-1}:{\rm Ran}\,\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T)\to\mathcal{D}(T^{2})
is bounded (just take
x=\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T)^{-1}y
for
y\in{\rm Ran}\,\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T)
in (
3.27
)) and
{\rm Ker}\,\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T)=\{0\}.
As
\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T)
is self-adjoint, we have that

\displaystyle\overline{{\rm Ran}\,\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T)}=

\displaystyle\;{\rm Ran}\,(\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T)^{\perp})^{\perp}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;{\rm Ker}\,(\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T)^{*})^{\perp}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;{\rm Ker}\,\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T))^{\perp}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\{0\}^{\perp}=\mathcal{H}_{n}.

Thus, for all
s=s_{0}+s_{1}
, with
s_{1}\in{\rm Im}(\mathcal{H}_{n})\setminus\{0\}
, we have
s\in\rho_{S}(T)
, i.e.,
\rho_{S}(T)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\setminus\mathbb{R}
, i.e.,
\sigma_{S}(T)\subseteq\mathbb{R}
.

∎

Lemma 3.2.5
.

Let
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
be a positive operator. Then
\sigma_{S}(T)\subseteq[0,\infty)
.

Proof.

A careful inspection of the proof of Lemma
3.2.4
, bearing in mind the additional hypothesis that
T
is a positive operator, will reveal that for all
s<0
, we have that
\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T)^{-1}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
and hence
(-\infty,0)\subseteq\rho_{S}(T)
, i.e.,
\sigma_{S}(T)\subseteq[0,\infty)
.
∎

Lemma 3.2.6
.

Let
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
be anti self-adjoint. Then

(3.28)

\displaystyle\sigma_{S}(T)\subseteq{\rm Im}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1}):=\{a\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}:{\rm Re}(a)=0\}.

Proof.

Since
T=-T^{*}
, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T)x\|^{2}=

\displaystyle\;{\rm Re}\langle T^{2}x,T^{2}x\rangle+2(s_{0}^{2}-|s_{1}|^{2}){\rm Re}\langle Tx,Tx\rangle+(s_{0}^{2}+|s_{1}|^{2}){\rm Re}\langle x,x\rangle

(3.29)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\|T^{2}x\|+2(s_{0}^{2}-|s_{1}|^{2})\|Tx\|^{2}+(s_{0}^{2}+|s_{1}|^{2})\|x\|^{2}\quad{\rm for}\;x\in\mathcal{D}(T^{2}).

Therefore, if
|s_{0}|\geq|s_{1}|
, then we have

(3.30)

\|\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T)x\|^{2}\geq s_{0}^{4}\|x\|^{2}\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad x\in\mathcal{D}(T^{2})

and we may proceed as in Lemma
3.2.4
to show that
s=s_{0}+s_{1}\in\rho_{S}(T)
, i.e., (
3.28
) holds.

If
|s_{0}|<|s_{1}|
, then

(s_{0}^{2}-|s_{1}|^{2})\|T^{2}x\|\|x\|\leq\|(s_{0}^{2}-|s_{1}|^{2})\|Tx\|^{2}
for
x\in\mathcal{D}(T)
together with (
3.29
) can be used to show that (
3.30
) holds. But then we may proceed as above to obtain (
3.28
).
∎

Theorem 3.2.7
.

Let
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
be a normal operator. Then we have

\sigma_{PS}(T)=\sigma_{PS}(T^{*}),\ \ \ \ \ \ \sigma_{RS}(T)=\sigma_{RS}(T^{*})=0,\ \ \ \ \ \sigma_{CS}(T)=\sigma_{CS}(T^{*}).

Proof.

Since
T
is normal and
\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T)^{*}=\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T^{*})
it is clear that
\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T)^{*}
is normal.
For bounded linear operators the kernel
T
and the kernel of its adjoint are equal so

{\rm Ker}\,(\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T))={\rm Ker}\,(\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T^{*}))

so by the definition of point
S
-spectrum se have

\sigma_{PS}(T)=\sigma_{PS}(T^{*}).

The fact that
\sigma_{RS}(T)=\sigma_{RS}(T^{*})=0
follows by contradiction, in fact if
0\not=s\in\sigma_{RS}(T)
we get

\{0\}={\rm Ker}\,(\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T))={\rm Ker}\,(\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T^{*}))=({\rm Ran}\,(\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T))^{\perp}\not=\{0\}.

In the same way we can prove that
\sigma_{RS}(T^{*})=0
.
Since
T
and
T^{*}
have the same
S
-spectrum and the three components of the
S
-spectrum, by definition,
