are pairwise disjoint it follows that
\sigma_{CS}(T)=\sigma_{CS}(T^{*})
.

∎

4.
Measure theory and integration theory for
\mathbb{R}_{n}
-valued measures

Definition 4.0.1

(positive
\mathbb{R}_{n}
-valued measure)
.

Let
\Omega
be a non-empty set and
\mathscr{A}
denote a
\sigma
-algebra on
\Omega
. We will call
\mu:\mathscr{A}\to\mathbb{R}_{n}\cup\{\infty\}

positive
if
\mu(M)\succeq 0
(see Definition
2.1.6
) for every
M\in\mathscr{A}
such that
\mu(M)\neq\infty
and
\mu
is
\sigma
-additive, i.e.,

(4.1)

\mu\left(\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}M_{n}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mu(M_{n})

for every sequence
M:=(M_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
, where
M_{i}\cap M_{j}=\emptyset
for
i\neq j
and
M_{i}\in\mathscr{A}
for
i=1,2,\ldots
. In this case, we shall write
\mu
is
\mathfrak{P}(\mathbb{R}_{n})
-valued.

Definition 4.0.2

(finite, semi-finite and
\sigma
-finite
\mathfrak{P}(\mathbb{R}_{n})
-valued measure)
.

Let
\Omega
be a non-empty set and
\mathscr{A}
denote a
\sigma
-algebra on
\Omega
. We will call a
\mathfrak{P}(\mathbb{R}_{n})
-valued measure
\mu

finite
if
\mu(\Omega)<\infty
,
semi-finite
if for every
M\in\mathscr{A}
such that

\mu(M)=\infty
, there exists
N\in\mathscr{A}
such that
\mu(N)<\infty
and
N\subseteq M

and
\sigma
-finite
if

\Omega=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}M_{i},

where
\mu(M_{i})<\infty
for
i=1,2,\ldots
. Note that if
\mu
is finite, then the finite additivity of
\mu
together with
\mu(\Omega)<\infty
implies that
\mu(M)<\infty
for every
M\in\mathscr{A}
.

Definition 4.0.3

(Borel measure)
.

Let
X
be a Hausdorff space. We will call a
\mathfrak{P}(\mathbb{R}_{n})
-valued measure
\mu
on the Borel
\sigma
-algebra generated by
X
a
positive
\mathbb{R}_{n}
-valued Borel measure
.

Definition 4.0.4

(
\mathbb{R}_{n}
-valued measure)
.

Let
\Omega
be a non-empty set and
\mathscr{A}
denote a
\sigma
-algebra on
\Omega
. We will call
\mu:\mathscr{A}\to\mathbb{R}_{n}
a
\mathbb{R}_{n}
-valued measure if
\mu
is
\sigma
-additive. In this case,
\mu
has the
Jordan decomposition

(4.2)

\mu=\sum_{\alpha}\{\mu^{(\alpha)}_{+}-\mu^{(\alpha)}_{-}\}e_{\alpha},

where
\mu_{\pm}^{(\alpha)}
are positive measures (in the usual sense) for every

\alpha\in\raisebox{1.79993pt}{\Large$\wp$}(\{1,\ldots,n\})
.
The
support of a
\mathbb{R}_{n}
-valued measure
\mu
shall be denoted by
{\rm supp}\,\mu
and is defined as the set
N
which satisfies

\mu(M)=\mu(M\cap N)\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad M\in\mathscr{A}.

We will call
\mu

finite
if
\mu(\Omega)<\infty
.

Definition 4.0.5

(Integral with respect to a
\mathbb{R}_{n}
-valued measure)
.

Let
\mu
be a finite
\mathbb{R}_{n}
-valued measure on a non-empty set
\Omega
,
\mathscr{A}
be a
\sigma
-algebra generated by
\Omega
,
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathbb{S}
and
\mu=\sum_{\alpha}\{\mu_{+}^{(\alpha)}-\mu_{-}^{(\alpha)}\}e_{\alpha}
be the Jordan decomposition for
\mu
. Then for any
\mathscr{A}
-measurable function
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
, we shall define

(4.3)

\int_{\Omega}f(\lambda)\,d\mu(\lambda):=\sum_{\alpha}\left(\int_{\Omega}f(\lambda)\,d\mu_{+}^{(\alpha)}-\int_{\Omega}f(\lambda)\,d\mu_{-}^{(\alpha)}\right)e_{\alpha}

and for any
\mathscr{A}
-measurable function
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}
, we shall define

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}f(\lambda)\,d\mu(\lambda):=

\displaystyle\;\sum_{\alpha}\left(\int_{\Omega}{\rm Re}(f(\lambda))\,d\mu_{+}^{(\alpha)}-\int_{\Omega}{\rm Re}(f(\lambda))\,d\mu_{-}^{(\alpha)}\right)e_{\alpha}

(4.4)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\;\sum_{\alpha}\left(\int_{\Omega}{\rm Im}(f(\lambda))\,d\mu_{+}^{(\alpha)}-\int_{\Omega}{\rm Im}(f(\lambda))d\mu_{-}^{(\alpha)}\right)\,\mathfrak{I}\,e_{\alpha},

provided that all of the four integrals on the right-hand side exist and we do not end up with the indeterminate expression
\infty-\infty
. Similarly, for any
\mathscr{A}
-measurable function
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}
, we can define an integral with
f(\lambda)
on the right via

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}d\mu(\lambda)\,f(\lambda):=

\displaystyle\;\sum_{\alpha}\left(\int_{\Omega}{\rm Re}(f(\lambda))\,d\mu_{+}^{(\alpha)}-\int_{\Omega}{\rm Re}(f(\lambda))\,d\mu_{-}^{(\alpha)}\right)e_{\alpha}

(4.5)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\;\sum_{\alpha}\left(\int_{\Omega}{\rm Im}(f(\lambda))\,d\mu_{+}^{(\alpha)}-\int_{\Omega}{\rm Im}(f(\lambda))\,d\mu_{-}^{(\alpha)}\right)\,\mathfrak{I}\,e_{\alpha}.

Definition 4.0.6

(measure space and
\mu
-integrability)
.

Let
\Omega
be a non-empty set and
\mathscr{A}
denote a
\sigma
-algebra on
\Omega
. Suppose
\mu=\sum_{\alpha}\mu^{(\alpha)}e_{\alpha}
is a
\mathfrak{P}(\mathbb{R}_{n})
-valued measure on
\mathscr{A}
. We shall call the triple
(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mu)
a
measure space
. We will write that a property holds
\mu
-a.e. on
\Omega
whenever the desired property holds except on a set
M\in\mathscr{A}
, where
\mu(M)=0
. A function
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
is called
measurable
if
\{\lambda\in\Omega:f(\lambda)\leq t\}\in\mathscr{A}
for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
. We will call a function
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}\cup\{\infty\}
, where
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathbb{S}
,
\mu
-integrable if
\int_{\Omega}f(\lambda)d\mu^{(\alpha)}(\lambda)
converge for all
\alpha\in\raisebox{1.79993pt}{\Large$\wp$}(\{1,\ldots,n\}).

Theorem 4.0.7
.

Let
X
be a compact Hausdorff space and
\mathscr{C}(X,\mathbb{R})
denote the normed space of real-valued continuous functions on
X
together with the supremum norm
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
. Corresponding to any bounded positive linear functional
\mathscr{L}:\mathscr{C}(X,\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}
, there exists a unique positive Borel measure
\mu
on
X
such that

\mathscr{L}(p)=\int_{X}p(t)\,d\mu(t)\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad p\in\mathscr{C}(X,\mathbb{R}).

In this case,
\mu(M)\leq\|\mathscr{L}\|
for every set
M
that belongs to the Borel
\sigma
-algebra generated by
X
, i.e.,
\mathscr{B}(X)
.

Proof.

The existence and uniqueness of
\mu
is a special case of Theorem D in Section 56 of
[
42
]
. The last assertion follows immediately from the fact that

\|\mathscr{L}\|\geq\mathscr{L}(1)=\int_{X}d\mu(t)=\mu(X)\geq\mu(M)\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad M\in\mathscr{B}(X).

∎

Corollary 4.0.8
.

Let
X
be a compact Hausdorff space. Corresponding to any bounded linear functional
\mathscr{L}:\mathscr{C}(X,\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}_{n}
, there exists a unique
\mathbb{R}_{n}
-valued Borel measure
\mu
on
X
such that

(4.6)

\mathscr{L}(p)=\int_{X}p(t)\,d\mu(t)\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad p\in\mathscr{C}(X,\mathbb{R}).

In this case,
|\mu(M)|\leq\|\mathscr{L}\|
for every set
M\in\mathscr{B}(X)
.

Proof.

Write
\mathscr{L}(f)=\sum_{\alpha}\{\mathscr{L}_{+}^{(\alpha)}(f)-\mathscr{L}_{-}^{(\alpha)}(f)\}e_{\alpha}
, where
\mathscr{L}_{\pm}^{(\alpha)}:\mathscr{C}(X,\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}
are positive linear functionals for
\alpha\in\raisebox{1.79993pt}{\Large$\wp$}(\{1,\ldots,n\})
. It is easy to see that
\mathscr{L}_{\pm}^{(\alpha)}
are bounded linear functionals and we may apply Theorem
4.0.7
to obtain the unique positive Borel measures
\mu^{(\alpha)}_{\pm}
so that

\mathscr{L}_{\pm}^{(\alpha)}(f)=\int_{X}f(t)d\mu_{\pm}^{(\alpha)}(t)\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad f\in\mathscr{C}(X,\mathbb{R}).

Thus, if we let
\mu=\sum_{\alpha}\{\mu_{+}^{(\alpha)}-\mu_{-}^{(\alpha)}\}e_{\alpha}
, then we obtain (
4.6
).

To see that
\mu
is unique, suppose there is another
\mathbb{R}_{n}
-valued measure
\nu
on
X
so that (
4.6
) holds. Write the Jordan decomposition
\nu=\sum_{\alpha}\{\nu^{(\alpha)}_{+}-\nu^{(\alpha)}_{-}\}e_{\alpha}
, where
\nu^{(\alpha)}_{\pm}
are positive Borel measures on
X
. Then, for any
\alpha\in\raisebox{1.79993pt}{\Large$\wp$}(\{1,\ldots,n\})
, we must have
\mu_{\pm}^{(\alpha)}=\nu_{\pm}^{(\alpha)}
. Thus,
\mu=\nu
.

The final assertion can be shown in much the same way as the final assertion of Theorem
4.0.7
.
∎

Definition 4.0.9

(transformation of a
\mathbb{R}_{n}
-valued measure)
.

Suppose
\mu
is a positive
\mathbb{R}_{n}
-valued measure on a
\sigma
-algebra of sets
\mathscr{A}
generated by a set
\Omega
and
\psi:\Omega\to\Omega^{\prime}
. Let
\mathscr{A}^{\prime}
be the family of sets given by
M^{\prime}\in\mathscr{A}^{\prime}
if
\psi^{-1}(M^{\prime})\in\mathscr{A}
. Notice that
\mathscr{A}^{\prime}
is a
\sigma
-algebra on
\Omega^{\prime}
and
\mu^{\prime}(M^{\prime}):=\mu(\psi^{-1}(M^{\prime}))
is a positive
\mathbb{R}_{n}
-valued measure on
\mathscr{A}^{\prime}
.

Theorem 4.0.10
.

Let
\Omega,\Omega^{\prime},\mathscr{A},\mathscr{A}^{\prime},\mu
and
\mu^{\prime}
be as in Definition
4.0.9
. Suppose
f:\Omega^{\prime}\to\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}
, where
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathbb{S}
, is a
\mu^{\prime}
-a.e. finite
\Omega^{\prime}
-measurable function. Then
f\circ\psi
is a
\mu
-a.e. finite
\mathscr{A}
-measurable function on
\Omega
and

(4.7)

\int_{\Omega^{\prime}}f(\lambda^{\prime})d\mu^{\prime}(\lambda^{\prime})=\int_{\Omega}f(\psi(\lambda))d\mu(\lambda).

Proof.

Let
\mu^{\prime}=\sum_{\alpha}\{\nu_{+}^{(\alpha)}-\nu_{-}^{(\alpha)}\}e_{\alpha}
and
\mu=\sum_{\alpha}\{\mu_{+}^{(\alpha)}-\mu_{-}^{(\alpha)}\}e_{\alpha}
be Jordan decompositions (see
4.2
) for
\mu^{\prime}
and
\mu
, respectively. Applying the classical result to
\nu_{\pm}^{(\alpha)}
and
\mu_{\pm}^{(\alpha)}
(which are just positive measures in the usual sense), see, e.g., Theorem C in Section 39 of
[
42
]
, we obtain both assertions.
∎

Theorem 4.0.11
.

Let
X
be a compact Hausdorff space. Corresponding to any bounded positive linear functional
\mathscr{L}:\mathscr{C}(X,\mathbb{R})\to\mathfrak{S}(\mathbb{R}_{n})
, there exists a unique positive
\mathbb{R}_{n}
-valued Borel measure
\mu
on
X
such that

(4.8)

\mathscr{L}(p)=\int_{X}p(t)\,d\mu(t)\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad p\in\mathscr{C}(X,\mathbb{R}).

In this case,
|\mu(M)|\leq\|\mathscr{L}\|
for every set
M\in\mathscr{B}(X)
.

Proof.

First, let
K\geq 0
be such that

|\mathscr{L}(p)|\leq K\|p\|_{\infty}\quad\quad{\rm for}\;{\rm every}\;p\in\mathscr{C}(X,\mathbb{R}).

Next, note that for every
p\in\mathscr{C}(X,\mathbb{R})
, we may uniquely decompose
\mathscr{L}(p)
as
\mathscr{L}(p)=\sum_{\alpha}\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}(p)e_{\alpha}
, where
\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}(p)\in\mathbb{R}
. Let
L(p):\mathscr{C}(X,\mathbb{R})\to\chi(\mathbb{R}_{n})
given by
L(p)=\chi(\mathscr{L}(p))
for
p\in\mathscr{C}(X,\mathbb{R})
. Since
\chi
is a
*
-homomorphism and
\mathscr{L}:\mathscr{C}(X,RR)\to\mathfrak{S}(\mathbb{R}_{n})
, we have that
L(p)
is a real Hermitian matrix for every
p\in\mathscr{C}(X,\mathbb{R})
. Moreover, in view of the first assertion in Remark
2.1.8
and the estimate

\displaystyle\|L(p)\|_{\infty}=

\displaystyle\;\|\chi(\mathscr{L}(p))\|=\sum_{\alpha}|\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}(p)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;2^{n/2}(\sum_{\alpha}\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}(p)^{2})^{1/2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;2^{n/2}|\mathscr{L}(p)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;2^{n/2}K\|p\|_{\infty}

where
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
denotes the maximum row sum norm of a matrix,
we have that
L(p)
is a bounded positive
\mathbb{R}^{2^{n}\times 2^{n}}
-valued linear functional. Consequently, a finite dimensional version of the operator-valued version of the Riesz representation theorem (see, e.g., Theorem 19 in
[
8
]
) gives us the existence of a uniquely determined positive
\mathbb{R}^{2^{n}\times 2^{n}}
-valued measure
\nu:=(\nu_{ik})_{i,j=1}^{2^{n}}
such that

L(p)=\int_{X}p(t)\,d\nu(t):=\left(\int_{X}p(t)d\nu_{ij}(t)\right)_{i,j=1}^{2^{n}}\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad p\in\mathscr{C}(X,\mathbb{R}).

If we write
L(p)=(L_{ij}(p))_{i,j=1}^{2^{n}}
, then
L_{ij}:\mathscr{C}(X,\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}
is a bounded linear functional such that

(4.9)

L_{ij}(p)=\int_{X}p(t)d\nu_{ij}(t)\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad p\in\mathscr{C}(X,\mathbb{R}).

Consequently, Corollary
4.0.8
, with
n=0
, asserts that
\nu_{ij}
is the only
\mathbb{R}
-valued Borel measure such that (
4.9
) holds. If we use the fact that
L(p)=\chi(\mathscr{L}(p))
and the aforementioned uniqueness of the
\mathbb{R}
-valued measure in (
4.9
), then for any
M\in\mathscr{B}(X)
, we have that
\nu(M)=\chi(a_{M})
for some
a_{M}\in\mathbb{R}_{n}
. In view of Remark
2.1.8
, the fact that
\nu(M)
is a positive semidefinite matrix implies and
\nu(M)=\chi(a_{M})
implies that
a_{M}\in\mathfrak{P}(\mathbb{R}_{n})
for all
M\in\mathscr{B}(X)
. It is easy to check that
\mu(M):=a_{M}=\chi^{-1}(\nu(M))
is a positive
\mathbb{R}_{n}
-valued measure with the property that

\mathscr{L}(p)=\chi^{-1}(L(p))=\int_{X}p(t)\,d\mu(t)\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad p\in\mathscr{C}(X,\mathbb{R}),

i.e., (
4.8
) holds.

The uniqueness of
\mu
such that (
4.8
) holds follows immediately from the uniqueness of
\nu
and the injectivity of
\chi
. The final assertion can be proved in much the same way as the proof of the final assertion in Theorem
4.0.7
.
∎

5.
Spectral integrals

Throughout this section,
\mathscr{A}
will denote an algebra of subsets of
\Omega
,
\mathcal{H}_{n}
will denote a Clifford module over
\mathbb{R}_{n}
and all infinite sums of operators in
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
will be meant in the strong operator topology, i.e.,
\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}T_{k}=T
if and only if

Tx=\lim_{k\to\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{k}T_{j}x\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}.

5.1.
Basic properties

Definition 5.1.1

(orthogonal projection)
.

An operator
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is called an
orthogonal projection
if
T
is self-adjoint and
T^{2}=T
. The set of orthogonal projections on
\mathcal{H}_{n}
will be denoted by
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
.

Definition 5.1.2

(spectral premeasure and spectral measure)
.

Let
\mathscr{A}
be an algebra of subsets in
\Omega
. We will call
E:\mathscr{A}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
a
spectral premeasure
if the following conditions hold:

(i)

E
is countably additive, i.e.,

E\left(\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}M_{i}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}E(M_{i})

for every sequence of mutually disjoint sets
(M_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
such that
M_{i}\in\Omega
for
i=1,2,\ldots
and
\cup_{i=1}^{\infty}M_{i}\in\Omega
.

(ii)

E(\Omega)=I
.

If
\mathscr{A}
is a
\sigma
-algebra, then
E
will be called a
spectral measure
. In this case, we will write that
E
is a spectral premeasure (resp., measure) on
(\Omega,\mathscr{A})
.

Lemma 5.1.3
.

Let
\mathscr{A}
be an algebra of sets in
\Omega
. Suppose
E:\mathscr{A}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is a finitely additive map, i.e.,

E\left(\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}M_{i}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}E(M_{i})

for every collection of mutually disjoint sets
(M_{n})_{i=1}^{k}
such that
M_{i}\in\Omega
for
i=1,\ldots,k
and
\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}M_{i}\in\Omega
. Then

(5.1)

E(M)E(N)=E(M\cap N)\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad M,N\in\mathscr{A}.

In particular, we have
E(M)E(N)=0
if
M\cap N=\emptyset
.

Proof.

The proof can be carried out in the same way as the complex Hilbert space case (see, e.g., Lemma 4.3 in
[
66
]
).
∎

Lemma 5.1.4
.

Suppose
\mathscr{A}
is an algebra of sets in
\Omega
. The mapping

E:\mathscr{A}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is a spectral premeasure if and only if the following conditions hold
:

(i)

For any
x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
, the set function
E_{x}(\cdot)
given by

E_{x}(M):=\langle E(M)x,x\rangle,

where
M\in\mathscr{A}
, is a countably additive
\mathfrak{P}(\mathbb{R}_{n})
-valued set function.

(ii)

E(\Omega)=I
.

Proof.

Suppose
E
is a spectral premeasure. Then, by Definition
5.1.2
, (ii) holds. It follows from Lemma
5.1.3
with
M=N
that
E(M)^{2}x=E(M)x
for
x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
and
M\in\mathscr{A}
, we have

E_{x}(M)=\langle E(M)x,E(M)x\rangle\in\mathfrak{P}(\mathbb{R}_{n})\quad\text{for $M\in\mathscr{A}$ and $x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}$.}

Thus, (i) holds.

Conversely, suppose (i) and (ii) hold. Suppose
(M_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
be a sequence of mutually disjoint sets such that
M_{i}\in\mathscr{A}
for
i=1,2,\ldots
and hence
M:=\cup_{i=1}^{\infty}M_{n}\in\mathscr{A}
, since
\mathscr{A}
is a
\sigma
-algebra. For any
x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
, we note that
E_{x}
is finitely additive and hence
E
is finitely additive as well. Thus, we may use Lemma
5.1.3
to deduce that
(E(M_{i}))_{i=1}^{\infty}
is a sequence of orthogonal projections such that
E(M_{j})E(M_{i})=0
whenever
j\neq n
. Consequently,
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}E(M_{i})
has a limit in the strong operator topology of
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
. Since

\displaystyle E_{x}(M)=

\displaystyle\;\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}E_{x}(M_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\langle E(M_{i})x,x\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\left\langle\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}E(M_{i})x,x\right\rangle\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad x\in\mathcal{H}_{n},

we may use the polarisation formula (
2.20
) to obtain

\langle E(M)x,y\rangle=\langle\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}E(M_{i})x,y\rangle\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad x,y\in\mathcal{H}_{n},

i.e.,
E(M)=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}E(M_{i})
. Thus,
E
is a spectral premeasure.
∎

Remark 5.1.5
.

In the event that
\mathscr{A}
is a
\sigma
-algebra, a careful inspection of the proof of Lemma
5.1.4
shows that we can easily adapt the proof to obtain the following result where spectral premeasure and countably additive
\mathfrak{P}(\mathbb{R}_{n})
-valued set function are replaced by spectral measure and
\mathfrak{P}(\mathbb{R}_{n})
-valued measure, respectively.

Lemma 5.1.6
.

Suppose
\mathscr{A}
is a
\sigma
-algebra of sets in
\Omega
. The mapping
E:\mathscr{A}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is a spectral measure if and only if the following conditions hold
:

(i)

For any
x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
, the set function
E_{x}(\cdot)
given by

E_{x}(M):=\langle E(M)x,x\rangle
, where
M\in\mathscr{A}
, is a
\mathfrak{P}(\mathbb{R}_{n})
-valued measure.

(ii)

E(\Omega)=I
.

Moreover,
E_{x}(\cdot)
is a finite
\mathfrak{P}(\mathbb{R}_{n})
-valued measure.

Proof.

See Remark
5.1.5
for the characterisation of spectral measures. The second assertion is a direct consequence of
E(\Omega)=I
. Indeed,
E_{x}(\Omega)=\langle E(\Omega)x,x\rangle=\langle x,x\rangle<\infty
.
∎

Remark 5.1.7
.

Fix
x,y\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
and let
E
be a spectral measure on
\mathscr{A}
. Then consider the
\mathbb{R}_{n}
-valued measure
E_{x,y}(M):=\langle E(M)x,y\rangle
for
M\in\mathscr{A}
. One can use the polarisation formula (
2.20
) to verify

(5.2)

E_{x,y}=\frac{\sum_{\alpha}(E_{x+ye_{\alpha}}-E_{x-ye_{\alpha}})e_{\alpha}}{4\dim\mathfrak{S}(\mathbb{R}_{n})}.

It turns out that the
\mathbb{R}_{n}
-valued measure
E_{x,y}
has the properties detailed in the following lemma.

Lemma 5.1.8
.

Let
\mathscr{A}
be an algebra of sets in
\Omega
. Suppose
E
is a spectral premeasure on
\mathscr{A}
. Then for any sequences of sets
(M_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
and
(N_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
in
\mathscr{A}
such that
M_{i+1}\subseteq M_{i}
,
N_{i}\subseteq N_{i+1}
for
i=1,2,\ldots
and
M:=\cap_{i=1}^{\infty}M_{i}\in\mathscr{A}
, we have

(5.3)

E(M)=s-\lim_{i\to\infty}E(M_{i})\quad{\rm and}\quad E(N)=s-\lim_{i\to\infty}E(N_{i}),

where
N:=\cup_{i=1}^{\infty}N_{i}
.

Proof.

The proof is very straight forward.
∎

We will now define the support of a spectral measure. We shall suppose
\Omega
is a Hausdorff topological space which has a countable base of open sets. In what follows,
\mathscr{B}(\Omega)
will denote the Borel
\sigma
-algebra of sets generated by
\Omega
, i.e., the smallest
\sigma
-algebra which contains all open sets in
\Omega
. For the sake of brevity, we shall sometimes write that
E
is a spectral measure on
\mathscr{B}(\Omega)
in place of
E
is a spectral measure on
(\Omega,\mathscr{B}(\Omega))
.

Definition 5.1.9

(support of a spectral measure)
.

Let
E
be a spectral measure on
\mathscr{B}(\Omega)
. We shall define the support of
E
by

{\rm supp}\,E:=\Omega\setminus\bigcup_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{$M\in\mathscr{B}(\Omega)$ open}}}{{E(M)=0}}}M.

The following lemma will be important when proving the bounded case for the spectral theorem for normal operators on a Clifford module.

Lemma 5.1.10
.

Let
E
be a spectral measure on
(\Omega,\mathscr{B}(\Omega))
. Then

(5.4)

{\rm supp}\,E=\{\lambda\in\Omega:\text{$E(M)\neq 0$ for every open set $M\in\mathscr{B}(\Omega)$ with $\lambda\in M$}\}.

Proof.

The characterisation (
5.4
) is a straight forward consequence of Definition
5.1.9
.
∎

Let
\mathscr{A}
be an algebra of sets in
\Omega
. For any
\mathbb{R}_{n}
-valued measure
\nu
on
\Omega
, we shall let the
total variation
of
\nu
be given by

(5.5)

|\nu|:=\sup_{(M_{i})_{i=1}^{k}}|\nu(M_{i})|,

where
(M_{i})_{i=1}^{k}
is any sequence of mutually disjoint sets with
M_{i}\in\mathscr{A}
for
i=1,\ldots,k
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
is arbitrary.

Definition 5.1.11

(An
L_{2}
-space with respect to
\mathbb{R}_{n}
-valued measure)
.

Let
\mathscr{A}
be an algebra of sets in
\Omega
,
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathbb{S}
and
\nu
be a
\mathbb{R}_{n}
-valued measure on
\mathscr{A}
. Then we shall let

L_{2}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},\nu):=\{\text{$\mathscr{A}$-measurable $f:\Omega\to\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}$}:\int_{\Omega}|f(\lambda)|^{2}d|\nu|(\lambda)<\infty\}.

For
x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
and a spectral measure
E
on
\mathscr{B}(\Omega)
we let
\mu_{x}(M)=\langle E(M)x,x\rangle
for
M\in\mathscr{B}(\Omega)
and

L_{2}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E_{x}):=\{\text{$\mathscr{A}$-measurable $f:\Omega\to\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}$}:\int_{\Omega}|f(\lambda)|^{2}d|\mu_{x}|(\lambda)<\infty\}.

Remark 5.1.12
.

Let
\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathfrak{I}
,
E
,
x
,
E_{x}
and
\nu
be as in Definition
5.1.11
. Write the Jordan decomposition
\nu=\sum_{\alpha}=\{\nu_{+}^{(\alpha)}-\nu_{-}^{(\alpha)}\}e_{\alpha}
for
\nu
. Then it is easy to see that

(5.6)

L_{2}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},\nu)=\bigcap_{\alpha\in\raisebox{1.79993pt}{\Large$\wp$}(\{1,\ldots,n\})}\{L_{2}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},\nu_{+}^{(\alpha)})\cap L_{2}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},\nu_{-}^{(\alpha)})\},

and

\displaystyle L_{2}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E_{x})=\{\text{$\mathscr{A}$-measurable $f:\Omega\to\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}$}:

\displaystyle\;\int_{\Omega}|f(\lambda)|^{2}d({\rm Re}\mu_{x}(\lambda))

(5.7)

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\;\infty\},

where
\mu_{x}(M):=\langle E(M)x,x\rangle
for
M\in\mathscr{A}
.

Lemma 5.1.13
.

Let
E^{\prime}
be a spectral premeasure on
(\Omega,\mathscr{A}^{\prime})
. Then there exists a spectral measure
E
on
(\Omega,\mathscr{A})
, where
\mathscr{A}
is the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
\mathscr{A}^{\prime}
, such that
E(M)=E^{\prime}(M)
for all
M\in\mathscr{A}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

For any
x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
, we may use Lemma
5.1.4
to see that
\mu^{\prime}_{x}(\cdot)
given by
\mu^{\prime}_{x}(M)=\langle E(M)x,x\rangle
is a premeasure on
\mathscr{A}^{\prime}
. Moreover, using Lemma
2.2.6
, we obtain

\displaystyle|\mu^{\prime}_{x}(\Omega)|=

\displaystyle\;|\langle\mu^{\prime}(\Omega)x,x\rangle|=|\langle x,x\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;\|x\|^{2}.

Thus,
\mu^{\prime}_{x}
is a finite
\mathbb{R}_{n}
-valued premeasure on
\mathscr{A}^{\prime}
and hence all of the positive measure components in the Jordan decomposition of

\mu^{\prime}_{x}=\sum_{\alpha}(\mu^{\prime}_{x,\alpha,+}-\mu^{\prime}_{x,\alpha,-})e_{\alpha},

namely
(\mu_{x,\alpha,\pm})_{\alpha\in\raisebox{1.79993pt}{\Large$\wp$}(\{1,\ldots,n\})}
, are also finite positive measures. Thus, we may appeal to Theorem A in §13 of
[
42
]
to produce uniquely determined measures
(\mu_{x,\alpha,+})_{\alpha\in\raisebox{1.79993pt}{\Large$\wp$}(\{1,\ldots,n\})}
on the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathscr{A}
. Consequently,
\mu_{x}:=\sum_{\alpha}(\mu_{x,\alpha,+}-\mu_{x,\alpha,-})e_{\alpha}
is the unique
\mathfrak{S}(\mathbb{R}_{n})
-valued measure on
\mathscr{A}
such that
\mu_{x}(M)=\mu^{\prime}_{x}(M)
for
M\in\mathscr{A}^{\prime}
.

Let
x,y\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
and
\mu_{x,y}
be the
\mathbb{R}_{n}
-valued measure given by

(5.8)

\mu_{x,y}(M):=\frac{\sum_{\alpha}(\mu_{x+ye_{\alpha}}-\mu_{x-ye_{\alpha}})e_{\alpha}}{4\dim\mathfrak{S}(\mathbb{R}_{n})}\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad M\in\mathscr{A}.

Next, let

\tilde{\mathscr{A}}:=\{M\in\mathscr{A}:\text{the map $x\mapsto\mu_{x,y}(M)$ is right linear for all $y\in\mathcal{H}_{n}$}\}.

One can use (
5.8
) and Lemma
5.1.8
to show that
\tilde{\mathscr{A}}
has the following property. The union
\cup_{m=1}^{\infty}M_{i}\in\tilde{\mathscr{A}}
for every sequence of sets
(M_{i})_{m=1}^{\infty}
such that
M_{i}\in\tilde{\mathscr{A}}
for
i=1,2,\ldots
with
M_{i}\subseteq M_{i+1}
. Thus, as
\mathscr{A}^{\prime}\subseteq\tilde{\mathscr{A}}
, we may use Theorem B in §6 of
[
42
]
to deduce that
\mathscr{A}\subseteq\tilde{\mathscr{A}}
and hence
\tilde{\mathscr{A}}=\mathscr{A}
.

In much the same way as above, one can show that the map
y\mapsto\mu_{x,y}(M)
is anti-right linear, i.e.,
\mu_{x,ya+z}(M)=\bar{a}\mu_{x,y}(M)+\mu_{x,z}(M)
for all
M\in\mathscr{A}
and
x,z\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
. It is easy to check that

\mu^{\prime}_{x+xe_{\alpha}}(M)-\mu^{\prime}_{x-xe_{\alpha}}(M)=2(\mu^{\prime}_{x,x}(M)e_{\alpha}-e_{\alpha}\mu^{\prime}_{x,x}(M))

and hence by the uniqueness of
\mu
we have

\mu_{x+xe_{\alpha}}(M)-\mu_{x-xe_{\alpha}}(M)=2(\mu_{x}(M)e_{\alpha}-e_{\alpha}\mu_{x}(M)).

Consequently,

\mu_{x,x}(M)=2\sum_{\alpha}(\mu_{x}(M)-e_{\alpha}\mu_{x}(M)e_{\alpha}),

in which case
\mu_{x,x}(M)\in\mathfrak{P}(\mathbb{R}_{n})
and
{\rm Re}\,\mu_{x,x}(M)\geq 0
for all
M\in\mathscr{A}
and
x\in\mathcal{H}_{N}
.
For any
M\in\mathscr{A}
, we may use the polarisation formula (
2.20
) to check that
\langle E^{\prime}(M)x,y\rangle=\mu^{\prime}_{x,y}(M)
and Lemma
2.2.6
(ii) to deduce

\displaystyle|\mu_{x,y}(M)|\leq

\displaystyle\;|\mu_{x,y}(\Omega)|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;|\mu^{\prime}_{x,y}(\Omega)|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;|\langle E^{\prime}(\Omega)x,y\rangle|=|\langle x,y\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\|x\|^{2}\|y\|^{2}\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad x,y\in\mathcal{H}_{n}.

Thus, for any
M\in\mathscr{A}
, we may invoke Theorem
3.0.26
to obtain a positive operator
E(M)\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
such that
\langle E(M)x,y\rangle=\mu_{x,y}(M)
for
x,y\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
. For
M\in\mathscr{A}^{\prime}
, we have

E^{\prime}(M)x,x\rangle=\langle E(M)x,x\rangle\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}

and, hence, one can use the polarisation formula (
2.20
) to check that

E^{\prime}(M)=E(M)
for
M\in\mathscr{A}^{\prime}
.

Finally, let
\mathscr{P}:=\{M\in\mathscr{A}:E(M)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{n})\}
. It is very easy to see that
\mathscr{P}
has the monotone property and hence
\mathscr{A}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathscr{P}
. But then
\mathscr{A}\subseteq\mathscr{P}
. Thus, we may use Lemma
5.1.6
to deduce that
E
is a spectral measure.
∎

Lemma 5.1.14
.

Suppose
\Omega
is a locally compact Hausdorff space which has a countable base of open sets and
E
is a spectral measure on
\mathscr{B}(\Omega)
. For any
x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
, let
\mu_{x}(M):=\langle E(M)x,x\rangle
for
M\in\mathscr{B}(\Omega)
and let
\mu_{x}=\sum_{\alpha}\{\mu_{x,+}^{(\alpha)}-\mu_{x,-}^{(\alpha)}\}e_{\alpha}
be the Jordan decomposition for
\mu_{x}
. Then

(5.9)

\mu_{x,+}^{(\alpha)}
is a finite positive Borel measure for every
\alpha\in\raisebox{1.79993pt}{\Large$\wp$}(\{1,\ldots,n\})
.

Proof.

The last assertion of Lemma
5.1.6
ensures that
\mu_{x}
is a finite
\mathfrak{P}(\mathbb{R}_{n})
-valued Borel measure. Consequently,
\mu_{x,+}^{(\alpha)}
is a finite positive Borel measure for all
\alpha\in\raisebox{1.79993pt}{\Large$\wp$}(\{1,\ldots,n\})
. But then we may use the well-known fact that every positive Borel measure on a locally compact Hausdorff space which has a countable base of open sets is regular (see, e.g., Proposition 7.2.3 in
[
13
]
) to obtain (
5.9
).
∎

Theorem 5.1.15
.

Suppose
\Omega_{i}
is a locally compact Hausdorff space which has a countable base of open sets and
E_{i}:\mathscr{B}(\Omega_{i})\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is a spectral measure on
\mathscr{B}(\Omega_{i})
for
i=1,\ldots,d
. If
E_{i}E_{j}=E_{j}E_{i}
for
i,j=1,\ldots,d
, then there exists a unique spectral measure
E:\mathscr{B}(\Omega)\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
, where
\Omega:=\Omega_{1}\times\cdots\times\Omega_{d}
, such that

(5.10)

E(M_{1}\times\cdots\times M_{d})=E(M_{1})\cdots E(M_{d})\quad{\rm for}\quad M_{i}\in\mathscr{B}(\Omega_{i})\;{\rm and}\;i=1,\ldots,d.

Proof.

Let us consider the case
d=2
(the more general case follows in much the same way). Let
\mathscr{A}^{\prime}
denote the algebra of sets generated by sets of the form
M_{1}\times M_{2}
, where
M_{i}\in\mathscr{B}(\Omega_{i})
for
i=1,2
. Thus, every
N\in\mathscr{A}^{\prime}
can be written as
N=\cup_{j=1}^{k}N_{j}
, where
N_{1},\ldots N_{k}
are mutually disjoint and are of the form

N_{j}=M_{1j}\times M_{2j}\in\mathscr{A}^{\prime}\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad j=1,\ldots,k.

We shall now define
E(N):=\sum_{j=1}^{k}E_{1}(M_{1j})E_{2}(M_{2j})
.

We claim that
E(N)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
. To this end, note that if
i\neq j
, then
N_{i}\cap N_{j}=\emptyset
forces
M_{1i}\cap M_{1j}=\emptyset
or
M_{2i}\cap M_{2j}=\emptyset
. In either case, we can make use of Lemma
5.1.3
to deduce

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\;E_{1}(M_{1i}\cap M_{1j})E_{2}(M_{2i}\cap M_{2j})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;E_{1}(M_{1i})E_{1}(M_{1j})E_{2}(M_{2i})E_{2}(M_{2j})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;E_{1}(M_{1i})E_{2}(M_{2i})E_{1}(M_{1j})E_{2}(M_{2j}).

Consequently,
E(N)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
. One can easily modify the proof of Theorem E in §8 of
[
42
]
to show that
E
is independent of the representation that we choose for
N\in\mathcal{B}(\Omega)
.

What remains is to show that
E
is countably additive, i.e., for any monotone sequence of sets
(N_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
, where
N_{i}\in\mathscr{B}(\Omega^{\prime})
, with
N:=\cup_{i=1}^{\infty}N_{i}
, we must show that
E(N)=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}E(N_{i})
. In view of the fact that
{\rm Re}\langle E(N^{\prime})x,x\rangle
is a positive measure for any choice of
N^{\prime}\in\mathscr{B}(\Omega^{\prime})
and
x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
, we can prove

{\rm Re}\langle E(N)x,x\rangle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}{\rm Re}\langle E(N_{i}x,x\rangle

in much the same way as the proof of Theorem 4.10 in
[
66
]
with the caveat that terms of the form
\langle E(N^{\prime})x,x\rangle
must be replaced by
{\rm Re}\langle E(N^{\prime})x,x\rangle
. Since
N_{i}\subseteq N
, we immediately have
\sum_{i=1}^{k}{\rm Re}\langle E(N_{i})x,x\rangle\leq{\rm Re}\langle E(N)x,x\rangle
and hence we arrive at

(5.11)

{\rm Re}\,\langle E(N)x,x\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}{\rm Re}\,\langle E(N_{i})x,x\rangle.

Since
E\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
, we have

\|E(N^{\prime})x\|^{2}={\rm Re}\,\langle E(N^{\prime})x,E(N^{\prime})x\rangle={\rm Re}\,\langle E(N^{\prime})x,x\rangle\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad N^{\prime}\in\mathscr{B}(\Omega)

and thus we may use (
5.11
) and the fact that all sums are taken with respect to the strong operator topology to obtain
E(N)=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}E(N_{i})
, i.e.,
E
is countably additive.

Finally, since the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
\mathscr{A}^{\prime}
is
\mathscr{B}(\Omega)
, we may use Lemma
5.1.13
to deduce that the spectral premeasure
E
has an extension to a spectral measure. With a slight abuse of notation, the aforementioned spectral measure on
\mathscr{B}(\Omega)
will be denoted by
E
. One can use the definition of
E
to verify (
5.10
). The uniqueness of a spectral
E
which obeys (
5.10
) drops out immediately from the fact that the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the algebra of sets of the form
M_{1}\times M_{2}
, where
M_{i}\in\mathscr{B}(\Omega_{i})
for
i=1,2
, is
\mathscr{B}(\Omega)
.

∎

5.2.
Spectral integrals of bounded measurable functions

We anticipate the definition of imaginary operator
given in Definition
8.0.2
.
We will call an operator
J_{0}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
a
partial imaginary operator
if
J_{0}
is a partial isometry and
J_{0}^{*}=-J_{0}
. We will call
J\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
an
imaginary operator
if
J
is unitary and
J^{*}=-J
.

Definition 5.2.1
.

Given a spectral measure
E
on
(\Omega,\mathscr{A})
and an imaginary operator
J\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
, we will say that
J
is associated with the spectral measure
E
if

(5.12)

JE(M)=E(M)J\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad M\in\mathscr{A}.

Given a spectral measure
E
on
(\Omega,\mathscr{A})
as above and an imaginary
J
associated with
E
, we wish to give meaning to the integral of a measurable function
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}
, where
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathbb{S}
, against a spectral measure
E
. Let
\mathfrak{B}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}})
denote the Banach space of all bounded
\mathscr{A}
-measurable functions
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}
, where
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathbb{S}
, equipped with the norm

\|f\|_{\infty}=\sup\{|f(\lambda)|:\lambda\in\Omega\}.

We let
\chi_{M}
denote the characteristic function with respect to
M\in\Omega
, i.e.,

\chi_{M}(\lambda)=\begin{cases}1&{\rm if}\quad\lambda\in M\\
0&{\rm if}\quad\lambda\notin M.\end{cases}

Let
\mathfrak{B}_{s}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}})
denote the subspace of simple functions in

\mathfrak{B}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}})
, i.e., the subspace of functions
f\in\mathfrak{B}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}})
which are of the form

(5.13)

f(\lambda)=\sum_{j=1}^{k}c_{j}\,\chi_{M_{j}}(\lambda),

where
c_{1},\ldots,c_{k}\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}
and
M_{1},\ldots,M_{k}
are pairwise disjoint sets belonging to
\mathfrak{B}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}})
.

Given
f\in\mathfrak{B}_{s}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}})
, we shall let

(5.14)

\mathbb{I}(f):=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\{{\rm Re}(c_{j})E(M_{i})+{\rm Im}(c_{j})E(M_{j})J\}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n}).

It can be easily checked that the finite additivity of
E
implies that the definition of
\mathbb{I}(f)
in (
5.14
) is independent of the (
5.13
).

Lemma 5.2.2
.

Let
f\in\mathfrak{B}_{s}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}})
. Then
\|\mathbb{I}(f)\|\leq\|f\|_{\infty}
.

Proof.

Since
M_{1}\ldots,M_{k}
are mutually disjoint sets belonging to
\mathscr{B}(\Omega)
, Lemma
5.1.3
asserts that
E(M_{i})E(M_{j})=0
whenever
i\neq j
. Thus, for any
x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{I}(f)x\|^{2}=

\displaystyle\;\sum_{i,j=1}^{k}{\rm Re}\left\langle\{{\rm Re}(c_{i})I+{\rm Im}(c_{i})J\}E(M_{i})x,\{{\rm Re}(c_{j})I+{\rm Im}(c_{j})J\}E(M_{j})x\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\sum_{i=1}^{k}{\rm Re}\left\langle\{{\rm Re}(c_{i})I+{\rm Im}(c_{i})J\}E(M_{i})x,\{{\rm Re}(c_{i})+{\rm Im}(c_{i})J\}x\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\sum_{i=1}^{k}{\rm Re}\left\langle\{{\rm Re}(c_{i})I+{\rm Im}(c_{i})J\}E(M_{i})x,\{{\rm Re}(c_{i})I+{\rm Im}(c_{i})J\}E(M_{i})x\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\sum_{i=1}^{k}\|({\rm Re}(c_{i})I+{\rm Im}(c_{i})J)E(M_{i})x\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;\sum_{i=1}^{k}|c_{i}|^{2}\|E(M_{i})x\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;\sum_{i=1}^{k}\|f\|^{2}_{\infty}\|E(M_{i})x\|^{2}=\|f\|_{\infty}^{2}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{k}E(M_{i})x\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;\|f\|_{\infty}^{2}\|x\|^{2}.

Consequently,
\|\mathbb{I}(f)\|\leq\|f\|_{\infty}
holds.
∎

Definition 5.2.3

(
\mathbb{I}(f)
for
f\in\mathfrak{B}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}})
)
.

We will now give a definition for
\mathbb{I}(f)
when
f\in\mathfrak{B}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}})
. Since
\mathfrak{B}_{s}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}})
is a dense subset of the Banach space
\mathfrak{B}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}})
with respect to the supremum norm
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
, given any
f\in\mathfrak{B}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}})
, we have the existence of a Cauchy sequence
(f_{j})_{j=1}^{\infty}
, where
f_{j}\in\mathfrak{B}_{s}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}})
for
j=1,2\ldots
, such that

\lim_{j\to\infty}\|f-f_{j}\|_{\infty}=0.

Lemma
5.2.2
can be used to show that
(\mathbb{I}(f_{j}))_{j=1}^{\infty}
is a Cauchy sequence of operators belonging to
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
. As
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is a complete metric space (see Remark
3.0.5
), we have the existence of an operator
\mathbb{I}(f)\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
such that

\lim_{j\to\infty}\|\mathbb{I}(f)-\mathbb{I}(f_{j})\|=0.

Moreover, Lemma
5.2.2
can be used to show that the limit
\mathbb{I}(f)
does not depend on the choice of Cauchy sequence.

Theorem 5.2.4
.

For any
f,g\in\mathfrak{B}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}})
, we have the following
:

(i)

\mathbb{I}(\bar{f})=\mathbb{I}(f)^{*}
.

(ii)

\mathbb{I}(fg)=\mathbb{I}(f)\mathbb{I}(g)
.

(iii)

\mathbb{I}(\mathfrak{I})=J
and
\mathbb{I}(cf+g)=c\,\mathbb{I}(f)+\mathbb{I}(g)
for all
c\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}
and
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathbb{S}
.

(iv)

\langle\mathbb{I}(f)x,y\rangle=\int_{\Omega}{\rm Re}(f(\lambda))d\langle E(\lambda)x,y\rangle+\int_{\Omega}{\rm Im}(f(\lambda))d\langle JE(\lambda)x,y\rangle
for all
x,y\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
.

(v)

\|\mathbb{I}(f)x\|^{2}=\int_{\Omega}|f(\lambda)|^{2}d({\rm Re}\,\langle E(\lambda)x,x\rangle)
for all
x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
.

(vi)

\|\mathbb{I}(f)\|\leq\|f\|_{\infty}
.

(vii)

For any sequence of functions
(f_{j})_{j=1}^{\infty}
, where
f_{j}\in\mathfrak{B}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}})
for
j=1,2,\ldots
, which converges pointwise
E
-a.e. on
\Omega
to
f
and there exists
\kappa>0
such that
|f_{j}(\lambda)|\leq\kappa
for all
\lambda\in\Omega
and
j=1,2,\ldots
, we have

s-\lim_{j\to\infty}\mathbb{I}(f_{n})=\mathbb{I}(f).

Proof.

In view of Definition
5.2.3
, it suffices to prove (i)-(vi) for
f,g\in\mathfrak{B}_{s}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}})
. Let
f(\lambda)=\sum_{j=1}^{k}c_{j}\chi_{M_{j}}(\lambda)
and
g(\lambda)=\sum_{j=1}^{k}d_{j}\chi_{N_{j}}(\lambda)
. Using the fact that
JE(M)=E(M)J
for all
M\in\mathscr{A}
and
J^{*}=-J
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}(f)^{*}=

\displaystyle\;\sum_{j=1}^{k}E(M_{j})^{*}\{{\rm Re}(c_{j})I+{\rm Im}(c_{j})J^{*}\}E(M_{j})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\sum_{j=1}^{k}E(M_{j})\{{\rm Re}(c_{j})I-{\rm Im}(c_{j})J\}E(M_{j})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\sum_{j=1}^{k}\{{\rm Re}(c_{j})I-{\rm Im}(c_{j})J\}E(M_{j})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\mathbb{I}(\bar{f}).

Thus, we have proved (i).

Next, since

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}(f)\mathbb{I}(g)=

\displaystyle\;\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\{{\rm Re}(c_{i})I+{\rm Im}(c_{i})J\}E(M_{i})\right)\left(\sum_{j=1}^{k}\{{\rm Re}(d_{j})I+{\rm Im}(d_{j})J\}E(N_{j})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\sum_{i,j=1}^{k}\{{\rm Re}(c_{i}){\rm Re}(d_{j})I-{\rm Im}(c_{i}){\rm Im}(d_{j})I

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;+

\displaystyle({\rm Re}(c_{i}){\rm Im}(d_{j})+{\rm Re}(d_{j}){\rm Im}(c_{i}))J\}E(M_{i}\cap N_{j})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\mathbb{I}(f)\mathbb{I}(g),

we have proved (ii).

To prove the first statement in (iii), simply observe that
f(\lambda)=\mathfrak{I}\,\chi_{\Omega}(\lambda)=\mathfrak{I}
and hence
\mathbb{I}(\mathfrak{I})=J
. The second statement in (iii) is an easy consequence of (ii) and the fact that
\mathbb{I}(\mathfrak{I})=J
.

The proof of (iv) is very straight forward. To prove (v), we may use (i), (ii) and (iv) to verify that

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{I}(f)x\|^{2}=

\displaystyle\;{\rm Re}\langle\mathbb{I}(f)x,\mathbb{I}(f)x\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;{\rm Re}\,\langle\mathbb{I}(\bar{f})\mathbb{I}(f)x,x\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;{\rm Re}\,\langle\mathbb{I}(|f|^{2})x,x\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;{\rm Re}\left(\int_{\Omega}|f(\lambda)|^{2}d\langle E(\lambda)x,x\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\int_{\Omega}|f(\lambda)|^{2}d({\rm Re}\,\langle E(\lambda)x,x\rangle)\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}.

Statement (v) is a direct consequence of (iv). Indeed, using (iv), we have

\|\mathbb{I}(f)x\|^{2}\leq\|f\|_{\infty}\,{\rm Re}\langle x,x\rangle=\|f\|_{\infty}\|x\|^{2}\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad x\in\mathcal{H}_{n},

in which case we have (v).

Finally, to prove (vii), we may use (vi) to see that

\|\{\mathbb{I}(f)-\mathbb{I}(f_{n})\}x\|^{2}=\int_{\Omega}|f(\lambda)-f_{n}(\lambda)|^{2}d({\rm Re}\langle E(\lambda)x,x\rangle)\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}

and hence (vii) follows immediately from the Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem.
∎

Lemma 5.2.5
.

Let
\mathscr{A}
be an algebra of sets in
\Omega
and
E
be a spectral measure on
\mathscr{A}
. Then the following facts hold
:

(i)

|E_{x,y}|(M)\leq\sqrt{{\rm Re}\,E_{x}(M)}\,\sqrt{{\rm Re}\,E_{y}(M)}
for all
x,y\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
and
M\in\mathscr{A}
.

(ii)

Let
f\in L_{2}(\Omega,\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E_{x})
and
g\in L_{2}(\Omega,\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E_{y})
. Then

\displaystyle\;\left|\int_{\Omega}{\rm Re}(f(\lambda)g(\lambda))\,d\langle E(\lambda)x,y\rangle+\int_{\Omega}{\rm Im}(f(\lambda)g(\lambda))\,d\langle E(\lambda)Jx,y\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;\int_{\Omega}|f(\lambda)g(\lambda)|d|E_{x,y}|(\lambda)

(5.15)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;2^{n}\|f\|_{L_{2}(\Omega,\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E_{x})}\|g\|_{L_{2}(\Omega,\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E_{y})},

where
E_{x,y}(M)=\langle E(M)x,y\rangle
for
M\in\mathscr{B}(\Omega)
and for all
x,y\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
.

Proof.

We will first prove (i). Suppose
M=\cup_{k=1}^{p}M_{k}
, where
M_{k}\in\mathscr{A}
and
M_{k}\cap M_{j}=\emptyset
whenever
k\neq j
. Using Lemma
2.2.6
, we have

\displaystyle|E_{x,y}(M_{k})|=

\displaystyle\;|\langle E(M_{k})x,y\rangle|=|\langle E(M_{k})x,E(M_{k})y\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;\|E(M_{k})x\|\|E(M_{k})y\|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\sqrt{{\rm Re}\,E_{x}(M_{k})}\,\sqrt{{\rm Re}\,E_{y}(M_{k})}.

Consequently, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{p}|E_{x,y}(M_{k})|\leq

\displaystyle\;\sum_{k=1}^{p}\sqrt{{\rm Re}\,E_{x}(M_{k})}\,\sqrt{{\rm Re}\,E_{y}(M_{k})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;\left(\sum_{k=1}^{p}{\rm Re}\,E_{x}(M_{k})\right)^{1/2}\,\left(\sum_{k=1}^{p}{\rm Re}\,E_{y}(M_{k})\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\sqrt{{\rm Re}\,E_{x}(M)}\,\sqrt{{\rm Re}\,E_{y}(M)}.

Finally, if we take the supremum over all possible disjoint unions of
M
in
\mathscr{A}
, then we obtain (i).

We will now prove (ii). We will verify (
5.15
) for simple functions, in which case (
5.15
) will hold by the density of simple functions in both
L_{2}
-spaces. Suppose
f(\lambda)=\sum_{k=1}^{p}f_{k}\chi_{M_{k}}(\lambda)
and
g(\lambda)=\sum_{k=1}^{p}g_{k}\chi_{M_{k}}(\lambda)
are simple functions in
L_{2}(\Omega,\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E_{x})
and
L_{2}(\Omega,\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E_{y})
, respectively. Using (
2.4
), Assertion (i) and (
2.4
), we have

\displaystyle\;\left|\int_{\Omega}{\rm Re}(f(\lambda)g(\lambda))d\langle E(\lambda)x,y\rangle+\int_{\Omega}{\rm Im}(f(\lambda)g(\lambda))d\langle E(\lambda)Jx,y\rangle\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\left|\sum_{k=1}^{p}\{{\rm Re}(f_{k}g_{k})\langle E(M_{k})x,y\rangle+{\rm Im}(f_{k}g_{k})\langle E(M_{k})Jx,y\rangle\}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;2^{n-1}\sum_{k=1}^{p}|f_{k}\,g_{k}||\{|E_{x,y}|(M_{k})+|E_{Jx,y}|(M_{k})\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;2^{n-1}\sum_{k=1}^{p}|f_{k}\,g_{k}|\left(\sqrt{{\rm Re}\,E_{x}(M_{k})}\,\sqrt{{\rm Re}\,E_{y}(M_{k})}+\sqrt{{\rm Re}\,E_{Jx}(M_{k})}\,\sqrt{{\rm Re}\,E_{y}(M_{k})}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;2^{n-1}\sum_{k=1}^{p}|f_{k}\,g_{k}|\left(\sqrt{{\rm Re}\,E_{x}(M_{k})}\,\sqrt{{\rm Re}\,E_{y}(M_{k})}+\sqrt{{\rm Re}\,E_{x}(M_{k})}\,\sqrt{{\rm Re}\,E_{y}(M_{k})}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;2^{n}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{p}|f_{k}|^{2}{\rm Re}\,E_{x}(M_{k})\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\;\left(\sum_{k=1}^{p}|g_{k}|^{2}{\rm Re}\,E_{y}(M_{k})\right)^{1/2}.

Thus, (
5.15
) holds for simple functions.
∎

5.3.
Spectral integrals of unbounded measurable functions

Fix
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathbb{S}
. Let
\mathfrak{F}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E)
denote the set of all
\mathscr{A}
-measurable functions

f:\Omega\to\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}\cup\{\infty\}
which are
E
-a.e. finite, i.e.,
E(\{\lambda\in\Omega:f(\lambda)=\infty\})=0
.

Definition 5.3.1

(bounding sequence)
.

Let
(M_{j})_{j=1}^{\infty}
be a sequence of sets, where
M_{j}\in\mathscr{B}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}})
and
M_{j}\subseteq M_{j+1}
for
j=1,2,\ldots
. We will call
(M_{j})_{j=1}^{\infty}
a
bounding sequence
for a subset
\mathfrak{G}
of
\mathfrak{F}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E)
if every
f\in\mathfrak{G}
is bounded on
M_{j}
and
E(\cup_{j=1}^{\infty}M_{j})=I
.

Remark 5.3.2
.

We note that if
(M_{j})_{j=1}^{\infty}
is a bounding sequence, then for the results appearing in Subsection
5.1
, we have
E(M_{j})\preceq E(M_{j+1})
for
j=1,2,\ldots
,

\lim_{j\to\infty}E(M_{j})x=x\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}

and
\cup_{j=1}^{\infty}E(M_{j})\mathcal{H}_{n}
is dense in
\mathcal{H}_{n}
.

Remark 5.3.3
.

Every finite subset
\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{k}\}\subseteq\mathfrak{F}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E)
has a bounding sequence. To see this, let

M_{j}:=\{\lambda\in\Omega:|f_{j}(\lambda)|\leq n\quad{\rm for}\quad j=1,\ldots,k\}

and
M=\cup_{j=1}^{\infty}M_{j}
. Thus,
\Omega\,\setminus\,M\subseteq\cup_{j=1}^{k}\{\lambda\in\Omega:f_{j}(\lambda)=\infty\}
, in which case
E(\Omega\,\setminus\,M)=0
. Consequently,
(M_{j})_{j=1}^{\infty}
is a bounding sequence for
\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{k}\}
.

Theorem 5.3.4
.

Let
f\in\mathfrak{F}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E)
and

(5.16)

\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(f)):=\{x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}:\int_{\Omega}|f(\lambda)|^{2}d({\rm Re}\langle E(\lambda)x,x\rangle)<\infty\}.

For any bounded sequence
(M_{j})_{j=1}^{\infty}
for
f
, we have the following
:

(i)

\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(f))=\{x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}:\text{$(\mathbb{I}(f\chi_{M_{j}})x)_{j=1}^{\infty}$ converges in $\mathcal{H}_{n}$}\}=\{x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}:\sup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\|\mathbb{I}(f\chi_{M_{j}})x\|<\infty\}
.

(ii)

For any
x\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(f))
, the limit of the sequence
(\mathbb{I}(f\chi_{M_{j}})_{k=1}^{\infty}
does not depend on the choice of the bounding sequence
(M_{j})_{j=1}^{\infty}
. Moreover, there is a linear operator
\mathbb{I}(f)\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
with domain
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(f))
given by

(5.17)

\mathbb{I}(f)x=\lim_{j\to\infty}\mathbb{I}(f\chi_{M_{j}})x\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad x\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(f)).

(iii)

The right submodule
\cup_{j=1}^{\infty}E(M_{j})\mathcal{H}_{n}\subseteq\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(f))\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{n}
and is a core for
\mathbb{I}(f)
. Moreover,

(5.18)

E(M_{j})\mathbb{I}(f)\subseteq\mathbb{I}(f)E(M_{j})=\mathbb{I}(f\chi_{M_{j}})\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad j=1,2,\ldots.

Proof.

The proof is broken into steps.

Step 1:

Prove
(i).

Let
x\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(f))
. Thus, by definition,
f\in L_{2}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E_{x})
and we may use Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem to obtain
f\chi_{M_{i}}\to f
in
L_{2}(\Omega,\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E_{x})
. Since
(M_{j})_{j=1}^{\infty}
is a bounding sequence for
f
, we have that
f
is bounded on
M_{j}
. Thus,
f\chi_{M_{j}}\in\mathfrak{B}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}})
and
\mathbb{I}(f\chi_{M_{j}})\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
given by Definition
5.2.3
. Using Theorem
5.2.4
(v), we obtain

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{I}(f\chi_{M_{i}})x-\mathbb{I}(f\chi_{M_{j}})x\|^{2}=

\displaystyle\;\|\mathbb{I}(f\chi_{M_{i}}-f\chi_{M_{j}})x\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\int_{\Omega}|f(\lambda)\chi_{M_{i}}(\lambda)-f(\lambda)\chi_{M_{j}}(\lambda)|^{2}d({\rm Re}\langle E(\lambda)x,x\rangle)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\|f\chi_{M_{i}}-f\chi_{M_{j}}\|^{2}_{L_{2}(\Omega,\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E_{x})}\quad{\rm for}\quad i,j=1,2,\ldots.

Thus, putting all of the above observations together, we have that

(\mathbb{I}(f\chi_{M_{i}})x)_{i=1}^{\infty}
is a Cauchy sequence in
\mathcal{H}_{n}
and hence
\mathbb{I}(f\chi_{M_{i}})x)_{i=1}^{\infty}
converges in
\mathcal{H}_{n}
.

Next, if
\{\mathbb{I}(f\chi_{M_{i}})x:i=1,2,\ldots\}
converges in
\mathcal{H}_{n}
, then
\{\|\mathbb{I}(f\chi_{M_{i}})x\|:i=1,2,\ldots\}
is bounded.

Finally, suppose that
\{\|\mathbb{I}(f\chi_{M_{i}})x\|:i=1,2,\ldots\}
is bounded. Then

\sup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\|\mathbb{I}(f\chi_{M_{i}})x\|<\infty.

Since
|f(\lambda)\chi_{M_{i}}(\lambda)|^{2}
converges monotonically to
|f(\lambda)|^{2}

E_{x}
-a.e. on
\Omega
, we may use Lebesgue’s monotone convergence theorem to obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|f(\lambda)|^{2}d({\rm Re}\langle E(\lambda)x,x\rangle)=

\displaystyle\;\lim_{i\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}f(\lambda)\chi_{M_{i}}(\lambda)|^{2}d({\rm Re}\langle E(\lambda)x,x\rangle)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\lim_{i\to\infty}\|\mathbb{I}(f\chi_{M_{i}})x\|^{2}<\infty.

Thus,
f\in L_{2}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E_{x})
and hence
x\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(f))
. We have managed to show

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(f))\subseteq

\displaystyle\;\{x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}:\text{$(\mathbb{I}(f\chi_{M_{j}})x)_{j=1}^{\infty}$ converges in $\mathcal{H}_{n}$}\}

\displaystyle\subseteq

\displaystyle\;\{x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}:\sup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\|\mathbb{I}(f\chi_{M_{j}})x\|<\infty\}

\displaystyle\subseteq

\displaystyle\;\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(f)),

in which case we have (i).

Step 2:

Prove
(ii).

Suppose
(N_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
is also a bounding sequence for
f
and
x\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(f)).
Using Theorem
5.2.4
(i) and (v), we have

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{I}(f\chi_{M_{i}}x-\mathbb{I}(f\chi_{N_{j}})x\|=

\displaystyle\;\|\mathbb{I}(f\chi_{M_{i}}-f\chi_{N_{j}})x\|_{L_{2}(\Omega,\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E_{x})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;\|f\chi_{M_{i}}-f\|_{L_{2}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E_{x})}+\|f\chi_{N_{j}}-f\|_{L_{2}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E_{x})}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\;0\quad\quad{\rm as}\quad i,j\to\infty.

Thus,

\lim_{i\to\infty}\mathbb{I}(f\chi_{M_{i}})x=\lim_{j\to\infty}\mathbb{I}(f\chi_{N_{j}})x\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad x\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(f)).

By (i), we have that
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(f))
is a right linear subspace of
\mathcal{H}_{n}
and (
5.17
) gives rise to a right linear operator
\mathbb{I}(f)\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
.

Step 3:

Prove
(iii).

Suppose
y\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
. Recall that
E(M_{i})=\mathbb{I}(\chi_{M_{i}})
and hence we may use Theorem
5.2.4
to obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}(f\chi_{M_{i}})y=

\displaystyle\;\mathbb{I}(f\chi_{M_{i}}\chi_{M_{j}})y=\mathbb{I}(f\chi_{M_{i}})E(M_{j})y

(5.19)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;E(M_{j})\mathbb{I}(f\chi_{M_{i}})y\quad\quad{\rm whenever}\quad i\geq j.

Consequently,
\sup\{\|\mathbb{I}(f\chi_{M_{i}})y\|:i=1,2,\ldots\}<\infty
and hence
E(M_{i})y\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(f))
, i.e., the union
\cup_{i=1}^{\infty}E(M_{i})\mathcal{H}_{n}\subseteq\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(f))
.

Letting
i\to\infty
in (
5.19
), we obtain

\mathbb{I}(f)E(M_{i})y=\mathbb{I}(f\chi_{M_{i}})y\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad y\in\mathcal{H}_{n}.

Suppose
x\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(f))
and letting
i\to\infty
in (
5.19
), we obtain the equality in (
5.18
).

Finally, using the fact that
E(M_{i})y\to y
as
i\to\infty
for all
y\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
and
\mathbb{I}(f)E(M_{i})x=E(M_{i})\mathbb{I}(y)\to\mathbb{I}(f)x
for all
x\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(f))
, we have that
\cup_{i=1}^{\infty}E(M_{i})\mathcal{H}_{n}
is a core for
\mathbb{I}(f)
.
∎

Remark 5.3.5
.

If
f\in\mathfrak{B}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E)
, then
(M_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
, where
M_{i}=\Omega
for
i=1,2,\ldots
, is a bounding sequence for
f
. Consequently, the operator
\mathbb{I}(f)\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
given by (
5.17
) coincides with
\mathbb{I}(f)\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
in Definition
5.2.3
.

Theorem 5.3.6
.

Suppose
f,g\in\mathfrak{F}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E)
. Then

\displaystyle\langle\mathbb{I}(f))x,\mathbb{I}(g)y\rangle=

\displaystyle\;\int_{\Omega}{\rm Re}(f(\lambda)\overline{g(\lambda)})d\langle E(\lambda)x,y\rangle

(5.20)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\;\int_{\Omega}{\rm Im}(f(\lambda)\overline{g(\lambda)})d\langle JE(\lambda)x,y\rangle

for
x\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(f))
and
y\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(g))
and

(5.21)

\|\mathbb{I}(f)x\|^{2}=\int_{\Omega}|f(\lambda)|^{2}d({\rm Re}\langle E(\lambda)x,x\rangle\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad x\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(f)).

Proof.

We will first show (
5.20
). Let
(M_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
be a bounding sequence for
\{f,g\}
and hence
fg\chi_{M_{i}}\in\mathfrak{B}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E)
. We may make use of Theorem
5.2.4
(i) and (ii) to obtain

\displaystyle\;\int_{\Omega}{\rm Re}(f(\lambda)\overline{g(\lambda)})d\langle E(\lambda)x,y\rangle+\int_{\Omega}{\rm Im}(f(\lambda)\overline{g(\lambda)})d\langle JE(\lambda)x,y\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\langle\mathbb{I}(f\bar{g}\chi_{M_{i}})x,y\rangle

(5.22)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\langle\mathbb{I}(f\chi_{M_{i}})x,\mathbb{I}(g\chi_{M_{i}})y\rangle.

Since
x\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(f)
, we have
f\in L_{2}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E_{x})
by (
5.16
). Similarly, since
y\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(g)
, we have
g\in L_{2}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E_{y})
. One can use Lemma
5.2.5
(ii) to see that the integrals

\int_{\Omega}{\rm Re}(f(\lambda)\overline{g(\lambda)}d\langle E(\lambda)x,y\rangle\;{\rm and}\;\int_{\Omega}{\rm Im}(f(\lambda)\overline{g(\lambda)})d\langle E(\lambda)Jx,y\rangle

are both convergent. We may also use Lemma
5.2.5
(ii) and the fact that
f\to f\chi_{M_{i}}
in
L_{2}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E_{x})
to see that

\displaystyle\;|\int_{\Omega}{\rm Re}(f(\lambda)\overline{g(\lambda)})\chi_{M_{i}}(\lambda)d\langle E(\lambda)x,y\rangle-\int_{\Omega}{\rm Re}(f(\lambda)\overline{g(\lambda)})d\langle E(\lambda)x,y\rangle

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\;\int_{\Omega}{\rm Im}(f(\lambda)\overline{g(\lambda)})\chi_{M_{i}}(\lambda)d\langle E(\lambda)Jx,y\rangle-\int_{\Omega}{\rm Im}(f(\lambda)\overline{g(\lambda)})d\langle E(\lambda)Jx,y\rangle|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;|\int_{\Omega}{\rm Re}(\{f(\lambda)\chi_{M_{i}}(\lambda)-f(\lambda)\}\overline{g(\lambda)})d\langle E(\lambda)x,y\rangle

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\;\int_{\Omega}{\rm Im}(\{f(\lambda)\chi_{M_{i}}(\lambda)-f(\lambda)\}\overline{g(\lambda)})d\langle E(\lambda)Jx,y\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;2^{n}\|f\chi_{M_{i}}-f\|_{L_{2}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E_{x})}\|g\|_{L_{2}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E_{y})}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\;0\quad{\rm as}\quad i\to\infty.

Thus, if we let
i\to\infty
in (
5.22
), we obtain (
5.20
).

Formula (
5.21
) is easily obtained from (
5.20
) by putting
y=x
and
g=f
.
∎

Theorem 5.3.7
.

Let
J
be an imaginary operator associated with the spectral measure
E
. For any
f,g\in\mathfrak{F}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E)
and
c\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}
, where
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathbb{S}
, we have the following
:

(i)

\mathbb{I}(\bar{f})=\mathbb{I}(f)^{*}
.

(ii)

\mathbb{I}(fg)=\overline{\mathbb{I}(f)\mathbb{I}(g)}
.

(iii)

\mathbb{I}(fc+g)=\overline{\mathbb{I}(f)({\rm Re}(c)I+{\rm Im}(c)J)+\mathbb{I}(g)}
for all
c\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}
.

(iv)

\mathbb{I}(f)
is a closed normal operator belonging to
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
.

(v)

\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(f)\mathbb{I}(g))=\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(fg))\cap\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(g))
.

Proof.

Let
(M_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
be a bounding sequence
f,g\in\mathfrak{F}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E)
(see Remark
5.3.3
). We note that it is easy to check that
(M_{i})_{I=1}^{\infty}
is a bounded sequence for the functions
f+g,fg
and
\overline{f}
. Consequently, Theorem
5.3.4
(iii) can be used to see that

(5.23)

\mathcal{E}:=\{\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}E(M_{i})x:x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}\}

is a core for
\mathbb{I}(f+g)
and
\mathbb{I}(fg)
. The remainder of the proof is broken into steps.

Step 1:

Prove
(i).

Suppose
x\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(f))
and
y\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(g))
. Using (
5.18
) and Theorem
5.2.4
(i) we obtain

\displaystyle\langle E(M_{i})\mathbb{I}(f)x,y\rangle=

\displaystyle\;\langle\mathbb{I}(f\chi_{M_{i}})x,y\rangle=\langle x,\mathbb{I}(\overline{f\chi_{M_{i}}y})\rangle=\langle x,\mathbb{I}(\overline{f}\chi_{M_{i}})y\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\langle x,E(M_{i})\mathbb{I}(\overline{f})y\rangle.

Letting
i\to\infty
above results in
\langle\mathbb{I}(f)x,y\rangle=\langle x,\mathbb{I}(\overline{f})y\rangle
, i.e.,
\mathbb{I}(\overline{f})\subseteq\mathbb{I}(f)^{*}
. Next, suppose
x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
and
y\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(f)^{*})
. Then we can again make use of (
5.18
) and Theorem
5.2.4
(i) to obtain

\langle x,E(M_{i})\mathbb{I}(f)^{*}y\rangle=\langle\mathbb{I}(f)E(M_{i})x,y\rangle=\langle\mathbb{I}(f\chi_{M_{i}})\,x,y\rangle=\langle x,\mathbb{I}(\overline{f\chi_{M_{i}}})y\rangle.

Thus,
E(M_{i})\mathbb{I}(f)^{*}y=\mathbb{I}(\overline{f\chi_{M_{i}}})y
and hence

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{I}(\overline{f\chi_{M_{i}}})y\|^{2}=

\displaystyle\;{\rm Re}\langle\mathbb{I}(\overline{f\chi_{M_{i}}})y,\mathbb{I}(\overline{f\chi_{M_{i}}})y\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;{\rm Re}\langle E(M_{i})\mathbb{I}(f)^{*}y,E(M_{i})\mathbb{I}(f)^{*}y\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\|E(M_{i})\mathbb{I}(f)^{*}y\|^{2}.

But then

\sup_{i=1,2,\ldots}\|\mathbb{I}(f\chi_{M_{i}})\|\leq\|\mathbb{I}(f)^{*}y\|,

in which case, Theorem
5.3.4
(i) ensures that
y\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(\overline{f}))
. Thus,
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(f)^{*})\subseteq\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(\overline{f}))
and hence
\mathbb{I}(\overline{f})\subseteq\mathbb{I}(f)^{*}
implies that
\mathbb{I}(f)^{*}=\mathbb{I}(\overline{f})
as required.

Step 2:

Prove
(ii).

We begin by noting that (
5.18
) asserts that
\mathcal{E}
is a dense right submodule such that
\mathcal{E}\subseteq\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(\overline{g})\mathbb{I}(\overline{f}))
. Consequently, we may use (i) and Theorem
3.0.24
to see that
\mathbb{I}(f)\mathbb{I}(g)
is closable. One can use (
5.18
) and Theorem
5.2.4
(i) to check that

(5.24)

\mathbb{I}(fg)E(M_{i})=\mathbb{I}(f)\mathbb{I}(g)E(M_{i})

and hence

(5.25)

E(M_{i})\mathbb{I}(f)\mathbb{I}(g)\subseteq\mathbb{I}(f)\mathbb{I}(g)E(M_{i})=\mathbb{I}(fg)E(M_{i}).

If we let
i\to\infty
in (
5.24
) and we make use of the fact that
\mathcal{E}
is a core for
\mathbb{I}(fg)
, then we obtain
\mathbb{I}(fg)\subseteq\overline{\mathbb{I}(f)\mathbb{I}(g)}
. On the other hand, as
\mathbb{I}(fg)E(M_{i})\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
, we may use (
5.25
) to conclude
E(M_{i})\overline{\mathbb{I}(f)\mathbb{I}(g)}\subseteq\mathbb{I}(fg)E(M_{i})
. Letting
i\to\infty
in
E(M_{i})\overline{\mathbb{I}(f)\mathbb{I}(g)}\subseteq\mathbb{I}(fg)E(M_{i})
and using (
5.17
), we obtain
\overline{\mathbb{I}(f)\mathbb{I}(g)}\subseteq\mathbb{I}(fg)
. But then we have (ii).

Step 3:

Prove
(iii).

The fact that
\mathbb{I}(fc)=\mathbb{I}(f)({\rm Re}(c)I+{\rm Im}(c)J)
, where
I
is the identity operator and
J
is the imaginary operator, is an immediate consequence of Theorem
5.2.4
(iii) and (ii) in the present theorem. Thus, in order to prove (iii), we need only consider
\mathbb{I}(f+g)
. Let
\mathcal{E}
be as in (
5.23
). Then, in view of (i), the set containments

\mathcal{E}\subseteq\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(\overline{f})+\mathbb{I}(\overline{g}))=\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(f)^{*}+\mathbb{I}(g)^{*})\subseteq\mathcal{D}(\{\mathbb{I}(f)+\mathbb{I}(g)\}^{*})

demonstrate that
\mathbb{I}(f)+\mathbb{I}(g)
is closable. Moreover,
\mathcal{E}
is a dense right submodule of
\mathcal{H}_{n}
. Using (
5.18
) and Theorem
5.2.4
(i), we obtain

\displaystyle(\mathbb{I}(f)+\mathbb{I}(g))E(M_{i})=

\displaystyle\;\mathbb{I}(f\chi_{M_{i}})+\mathbb{I}(g\chi_{M_{i}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\mathbb{I}(f\chi_{M_{i}}+g\chi_{M_{i}})

(5.26)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\mathbb{I}(f+g)E(M_{i})

and

\displaystyle E(M_{i})(\mathbb{I}(f)+\mathbb{I}(g))=

\displaystyle\;E(M_{i})\mathbb{I}(f)+E(M_{i})\mathbb{I}(g)

\displaystyle\subseteq

\displaystyle\;\{\mathbb{I}(f)+\mathbb{I}(g)\}E(M_{i})

(5.27)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\mathbb{I}(f+g)E(M_{i}).

If we let
i\to\infty
in (
5.26
) and make use of the fact that

\{\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}E(M_{i})x:x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}\}

is a core for
\mathbb{I}(f+g)
, then we obtain
\mathbb{I}(f+g)\subseteq\overline{\mathbb{I}(f)+\mathbb{I}(g)}
. Finally, (
5.27
) implies that
E(M_{i})\overline{\mathbb{I}(f)+\mathbb{I}(g)}\subseteq\mathbb{I}(f+g)E(M_{i})
and letting
i\to\infty
we have
\overline{\mathbb{I}(f)+\mathbb{I}(g)}\subseteq\mathbb{I}(f+g)
.

Step 4:

Prove
(iv).

It follows from (i) and (ii) that
\mathbb{I}(f)^{*}\mathbb{I}(f)=\mathbb{I}(\overline{f})\mathbb{I}(f)\subset\mathbb{I}(\overline{f}f)
. Note that
\mathbb{I}(f)^{*}\mathbb{I}(f)\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is closed. Thus, we may use Theorem
3.2.3
(ii) to conclude that
\mathbb{I}(f)^{*}\mathbb{I}(f)
is self-adjoint. On the other hand, (i) implies that
\mathbb{I}(\overline{f}f)
is self-adjoint, in which case, we have
\mathbb{I}(f)^{*}\mathbb{I}(f)=\mathbb{I}(\overline{f}f)
. We can replicated the above argument to obtain
\mathbb{I}(f)\mathbb{I}(f)^{*}=\mathbb{I}(f\overline{f})
and hence we have
\mathbb{I}(f)^{*}\mathbb{I}(f)=\mathbb{I}(f)\mathbb{I}(f)^{*}
.

Step 5:

Prove (v)
.

We begin by noting that (ii) implies that
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(f)\mathbb{I}(g))\subseteq\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(fg))\cap\mathcal{D}(g)
. Thus, in order to prove (v), we need only show the opposite set containment. Suppose
x\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(fg))\cap\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(g))
. Then we may use (
5.24
) to obtain

\mathbb{I}(fg)E(M_{i})x=\mathbb{I}(f)\mathbb{I}(g)E(M_{i})x
and letting
i\to\infty
and using the fact that
\mathbb{I}(f)
is closed, we have
\mathbb{I}(g)x\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(f))
. Thus, we have
x\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(f)\mathbb{I}(f))
as required.
∎

Theorem 5.3.8
.

For any
f,g\in\mathfrak{F}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E)
, we have the following
:

(i)

If
f=g

E
-a.e. on
\Omega
, then
\mathbb{I}(f)=\mathbb{I}(g)
.

(ii)

If
f
is real-valued
E
-a.e. on
\Omega
, then
\mathbb{I}(f)\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is a self-adjoint operator.

(iii)

If
f(\lambda)\geq 0

E
-a.e. on
\Omega
, then
\mathbb{I}(f)\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is a positive operator. Moreover,
(\mathbb{I}(\sqrt{f}))^{2}=\mathbb{I}(f)
and hence
\mathbb{I}(f)
has a positive square root.

Proof.

Suppose
f=g

E
-a.e.. Then
E(\{\lambda\in\Omega:f(\lambda)\neq g(\lambda)\})=0
and then it follows from (
5.16
) that
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(f))=\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(g))
. We also have that any bounding sequence
(M_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
for
f
is also a bounding sequence for
g
. Consequently, we may use (
5.17
) to deduce that
\mathbb{I}(f)=\mathbb{I}(g)
. Thus, we have proved (i).

Next, suppose
f
is real-valued
E
-a.e.. Then from (
5.20
) with
g(\lambda)=1
and
y=x
we obtain

(5.28)

\langle\mathbb{I}(f)x,x\rangle=\int_{\Omega}{\rm Re}(f(\lambda))d\langle E(\lambda)x,x\rangle=\int_{\Omega}f(\lambda)d\langle E(\lambda)x,x\rangle\in\mathfrak{S}(\mathbb{R}_{n})

for all
x\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(f))
. Thus,
\mathbb{I}(f)
is self-adjoint and we have proved (ii).

Next, suppose
f\geq 0

E
-a.e.. Then (
5.28
) can be used to show that
\mathbb{I}(f)
is a positive operator. Finally, in view of the first part of (iii), it is obvious that
\mathbb{I}(\sqrt{f})
is a positive operator. The fact that
(\mathbb{I}(\sqrt{f}))^{2}=\mathbb{I}(f)
is a direct consequence of Theorem
5.3.7
(ii) and (v).
∎

Definition 5.3.9

(
L_{\infty}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E)
)
.

Let
f\in\mathfrak{F}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E)
. We shall let

L_{\infty}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E):=\{\text{$E$-measurable $f:\Omega\to\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}$: $f$ is bounded on $\Omega$}\}

be endowed with the norm

\|f\|_{\infty}:=\inf_{{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle M\in\mathscr{A}}}{{{\rm with}\;E(M)=0}}}}\sup\{|f(\lambda)|:\lambda\in\Omega\,\setminus\,M\}.

Lemma 5.3.10
.

Let
f\in\mathfrak{F}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E)
. The operator
\mathbb{I}(f)\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
if and only if
f\in L_{\infty}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E)
. In this case,
\|\mathbb{I}(f)\|=\|f\|_{\infty}
.

Proof.

Suppose
f\in L_{\infty}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E)
. Then
|f(\lambda)|\leq\kappa

E
-a.e. for some
\kappa>0
and

\|\mathbb{I}(f)x\|^{2}=\int_{\Omega}|f(\lambda)|^{2}d({\rm Re}\langle E(\lambda)x,x\rangle)\leq\|f\|_{\infty}\|x\|^{2}.

Thus,
\mathbb{I}(f)\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
.

Next, suppose
\mathbb{I}(f)\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
and put

M_{i}:=\{\lambda\in\Omega:|f(\lambda)|\geq\|\mathbb{I}(f)\|+2^{-i}\}\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad i=1,2,\ldots.

and
M:=\cup_{i=1}^{\infty}M_{i}
. Note that

M=\{\lambda\in\Omega:|f(\lambda)|>\|\mathbb{I}(f)\|\}
. Using Theorem
5.3.7
(ii) and (
5.21
), we obtain

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{I}(f)\|^{2}\cdot\|E(M_{i})x\|^{2}\geq

\displaystyle\;\|\mathbb{I}(f)E(M_{i})x\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\|\mathbb{I}(f\chi_{M_{i}})x\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\int_{\Omega}|f(\lambda)\chi_{M_{i}}(\lambda)|^{2}d({\rm Re}\langle E(\lambda)x,x\rangle)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\int_{\Omega}|f(\lambda)|^{2}d({\rm Re}\langle E(\lambda)x,x\rangle)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\;(\|\mathbb{I}(f)\|+2^{-i})^{2}\|E(M_{i})x\|^{2}\quad{\rm for}\quad i\in\mathbb{N}\;{\rm and}\;x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}.

But then
E(M_{i})=0
for all
i=1,2,\ldots
, in which case
E(M)=0
, i.e.,
\|f\|_{\infty}\leq\|\mathbb{I}(f)\|
. Thus, we have the characterisation of elements in
L_{\infty}(\Omega,E)
and
\|\mathbb{I}(f)\|=\|f\|_{\infty}
.
∎

Lemma 5.3.11
.

Let
f\in\mathfrak{F}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E)
. The right linear operator
\mathbb{I}(f)\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is invertible if and only if
f(\lambda)\neq 0

E
-a.e. on
\Omega
. In this case,
\mathbb{I}(f)^{-1}=\mathbb{I}(1/f)
, with the understanding that
\frac{1}{\infty}=0
and
\frac{1}{0}=\infty
.

Proof.

Let
N:=\{\lambda\in\Omega:f(\lambda)=0\}
. We may use Theorem
5.3.7
(iii) and (v) to obtain

\mathbb{I}(f)E(N)=\mathbb{I}(f\chi_{N})=\mathbb{I}(0)=0.

Consequently,
\mathbb{I}(f)
is not invertible whenever
E(N)\neq 0
.

Next, suppose
E(N)=0
and hence
1/f\in\mathfrak{F}(\Omega,E)
. We may use the fact that
f(1/f)=1

E
-a.e. on
\Omega
to see that
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(f(1/f)))=\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(g))=\mathcal{H}_{n}
, where
g(\lambda)=1
. Consequently, Theorem
5.3.7
(v) implies that

\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(1/f)\mathbb{I}(f))=\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(f)).

Moreover, Theorem
5.3.7
(iii) implies that

\mathbb{I}(1/f)\mathbb{I}(f)\subseteq\mathbb{I}((1/f)f)=\mathbb{I}(1)=I.

Thus, putting these observations together, we have that
\mathbb{I}(f)
is invertible and
\mathbb{I}(f)^{-1}\subseteq\mathbb{I}(1/f)
. To see that
\mathbb{I}(1/f)\subseteq\mathbb{I}(f)
, we may simply replace
f
by
1/f
in the above proof.
∎

Definition 5.3.12

(intrinsic function)
.

Let
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathbb{S}
. Let
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
be an axially symmetric open set and let
\mathcal{U}=\{(u,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:u+\mathbb{S}v\subseteq\Omega\}
.
A function
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}_{n}
is called a left
slice function, if it is of the form

f(\lambda)=f_{0}(u,v)+\mathfrak{I}f_{1}(u,v)\qquad\text{for }\lambda=u+\mathfrak{I}v\in\Omega

with two functions
f_{0},f_{1}:\mathcal{U}\to\mathbb{R}_{n}
that satisfy the compatibility conditions

(5.29)

f_{0}(u,-v)=f_{0}(u,v),

(5.30)

f_{1}(u,-v)=-f_{1}(u,v).

We say that
f

is an
intrinsic function

if in addition
f_{0}
, and
f_{1}
are real valued.
We denote by
\mathcal{S}(\Omega)

the set of intrinsic functions defined on
\Omega
.

Remark 5.3.13
.

For intrinsic functions, when we
restrict
f
to
\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}\subseteq\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}
, we have that
f(\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}})
belongs to
\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}
,
since
f_{0}
, and
f_{1}
are real-valued.
To stress this fact we will often use the notation

\mathfrak{F}(\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}},\mathscr{B}(\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}),\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}})
instead of
\mathfrak{F}(\Omega,\mathscr{B}(\Omega))

when we work on a complex plane
\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}
, and when we consider the half plane

\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}
we write
\mathfrak{F}(\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+},\mathscr{B}(\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}})^{+},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+})
.
Observe that condition (
5.30
) forces

f_{1}(u,v)=0
for every
u+\mathfrak{I}v\in\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}\cap\mathbb{R}
whenever
v=0
.

Theorem 5.3.14
.

Let
E
be a spectral measure on
\mathscr{B}(\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+})
, where
\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}\subseteq\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}
for
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathbb{S}
, and
f\in\mathfrak{F}(\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+},\mathscr{B}(\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}),\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E)
be an intrinsic function. Then

\displaystyle\sigma_{S}(\mathbb{I}(f))\cap\mathbb{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{I}}=

\displaystyle\;\{s\in\mathbb{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{I}}:E(\{\lambda\in\Omega:|f(\lambda)^{2}-2{\rm Re}(s)f(\lambda)+|s|^{2}|<\varepsilon\})

\displaystyle\;\;\neq 0\text{ for all $\varepsilon>0$}\}.

Remark 5.3.15
.

The assumption that
f\in\mathfrak{F}(\Omega,\mathscr{B}(\Omega),\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E)
is intrinsic (see Definition
5.3.12
) is necessary for preserving the fact that
\sigma_{S}(f(T))
is axially symmetric (see Remark
3.1.7
).

Proof of Theorem
5.3.14
.

Notice that
s\in\rho_{S}(\mathbb{I}(f))
if and only if

(\mathbb{I}(f)^{2}-2{\rm Re}(s)\mathbb{I}(f)+|s|^{2}I)^{-1}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
.
Thus, Lemma
5.3.10
and Lemma
5.3.11
imply that
s\in\rho_{S}(\mathbb{I}(f))\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}
if and only if

f^{2}-2{\rm Re}(s)f+|s|^{2}\neq 0

E
-a.e. on
\Omega
and
1/(f^{2}-2{\rm Re}(s)f+|s|^{2})\in L_{\infty}(\Omega,\mathscr{B}(\Omega),\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E)
, or, equivalently, there exists
\kappa>0
such that

E(\{\lambda\in\Omega:|\ f(\lambda)-2{\rm Re}(s)f(\lambda)-|s|^{2}|\geq\kappa\})=0.

Thus,
s\in\sigma_{S}(\mathbb{I}(f))\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}
if and only if

E(\{\lambda\in\Omega:|f(\lambda)^{2}-2{\rm Re}(s)f(\lambda)+|s|^{2}|<\varepsilon\})\neq 0

for all
\varepsilon>0
.
∎

Remark 5.3.16
.

We wish to highlight that Theorem
5.3.14
will be very useful for showing that an operator-valued integral (
9.3
) appearing in the spectral theorem for a bounded normal operator is on the
S
-spectrum of
T
.

Theorem 5.3.17
.

Let
E
be a spectral measure on
\mathscr{B}(\Omega)
and
W\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
. Then
E(M)W=WE(M)
for all
M\in\mathscr{B}(\Omega)
if and only if
W\mathbb{I}(f)\subseteq\mathbb{I}(f)W
for every
f\in\mathfrak{F}(\Omega,\mathscr{B}(\Omega),\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E)
.

Proof.

Suppose
E(M)W=WE(M)
for all
M\in\mathscr{B}(\Omega)
. Then, as
\mathbb{I}(\chi_{M})=E(M)
, we have
W\mathbb{I}(f)\subseteq\mathbb{I}(f)W
for every
f\in\mathfrak{F}(\Omega,\mathscr{B}(\Omega),\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E)
. Conversely, suppose
W\mathbb{I}(f)\subseteq\mathbb{I}(f)W
for every
f\in\mathfrak{F}(\Omega,\mathscr{B}(\Omega),\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E)
. Then

\displaystyle{\rm Re}\,\langle E(M)Wx,Wx\rangle=

\displaystyle\;\|E(M)Wx\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\|WE(M)x\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;\|W\|^{2}\|E(M)x\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\|W\|^{2}{\rm Re}\langle E(M)x,x\rangle

for all
M\in\mathscr{B}(\Omega)
and
x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
. Thus,
Wx\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(f))
whenever
x\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(f))
. It is immediate to see that
W\mathbb{I}(f)=\mathbb{I}(f)W
whenever
f\in\mathfrak{B}_{s}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E)
and that this observation can be extended to all

f\in\mathfrak{B}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E)
. Consequently, we have
W\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(f))\subseteq\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(f))
and hence
W\mathbb{I}(f)\subseteq\mathbb{I}(f)W
.
∎

Theorem 5.3.18
.

Suppose
S\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
and
E
is a spectral measure on
(\Omega,\mathscr{A})
. Then
SE(M)=E(M)S
for every
M\in\mathscr{A}
if and only if
S\mathbb{I}(f)\subseteq\mathbb{I}(f)S
for every
f\in\mathfrak{F}(\Omega,\mathscr{B}(\Omega),\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E)
.

Proof.

If
S\mathbb{I}(f)\subseteq\mathbb{I}(f)S
for every
f\in\mathfrak{F}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},E)
, then
SE(M)=E(M)S
for every
M\in\mathscr{A}
is obvious. Conversely, suppose
SE(M)=E(M)S
for every
M\in\mathscr{A}
. Since

\displaystyle{\rm Re}(E_{Sx}(M))=

\displaystyle\;{\rm Re}(\langle E(M)Sx,E(M)Sx\rangle)={\rm Re}(\langle SE(M)x,SE(M)x\rangle)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\|SE(M)x\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;\|S\|^{2}\|E(M)x\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\|S\|^{2}{\rm Re}(\langle E(M)x,E(M)x\rangle,

we may use (
5.16
) to conclude that
Sx\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(f))
whenever
x\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(f))
. Since
SE(M)=E(M)S
for every
M\in\mathscr{A}
, we have that
S\mathbb{I}(f_{0})=S\mathbb{I}(f_{0})
for every simple function
f_{0}\in\mathfrak{B}_{s}
, which can easily be extended, by taking limits, to
S\mathbb{I}(f)=\mathbb{I}(f)S
for every
f\in\mathfrak{B}
. The fact that
S\mathbb{I}(f)=\mathbb{I}(f)S
for every
f\in\mathfrak{F}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},E)
follows immediately from (
5.17
).
∎

Definition 5.3.19

(transformation of a spectral measure)
.

Let
E
be a spectral measure on a Borel
\sigma
-algebra
\mathscr{A}
generated by a set
\Omega
,
J
be an associated imaginary operator with
E
and
\psi
be a function such that
\psi:\Omega\to\Omega^{\prime}
and
\mathscr{A}^{\prime}
be the
\sigma
-algebra of all subsets
M^{\prime}\subseteq\Omega^{\prime}
such that
\psi^{-1}(M^{\prime})\in\mathscr{A}
. We shall let

(5.31)

E^{\prime}(M^{\prime}):=E(\psi^{-1}(M^{\prime}))\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad M^{\prime}\in\mathscr{A}^{\prime}.

Remark 5.3.20
.

It is very easy to check that
E^{\prime}:\mathscr{A}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is a spectral measure on
\mathscr{A}^{\prime}
. Moreover, in view of (
5.31
) if
J
is an imaginary operator associated with
E
, then
J
is also an imaginary operator associated with the
E^{\prime}
.

Theorem 5.3.21
.

Let
\Omega,\Omega^{\prime},\psi,J,E
and
E^{\prime}
be as in Definition
5.3.19
. Suppose
h\in\mathfrak{F}(\Omega^{\prime},\mathscr{A}^{\prime},F)
. Then
h\circ\psi\in\mathfrak{F}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},E)
and

\displaystyle\;\int_{\Omega^{\prime}}{\rm Re}(h(\lambda^{\prime}))\,dE^{\prime}(\lambda^{\prime})+\int_{\Omega^{\prime}}{\rm Im}(h(\lambda^{\prime}))\,dE^{\prime}(\lambda^{\prime})J

(5.32)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\int_{\Omega}{\rm Re}(h(\psi(\lambda)))\,dE(\lambda)+\int_{\Omega}{\rm Im}(h(\psi(\lambda)))\,dE(\lambda)J

Proof.

The fact that
h\circ\psi\in\mathfrak{F}(\Omega,\mathscr{A},E)
follows immediately from Definition
5.3.19
. Since
\langle E(\cdot)x,x\rangle
and
\langle F(\cdot)x,x\rangle
are positive
\mathbb{R}_{n}
-valued measures on
\mathscr{A}
and
\mathscr{A}^{\prime}
for any
x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
, respectively, we have invoke Theorem
4.0.10
to obtain

\int_{\Omega^{\prime}}|h(\lambda^{\prime})|^{2}d\langle E^{\prime}(\lambda^{\prime})x,x\rangle=\int_{\Omega}|h(\psi(\lambda))|^{2}d\langle E(\lambda)x,x\rangle\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}

and

\displaystyle\;\int_{\Omega^{\prime}}{\rm Re}(h(\lambda^{\prime}))d\langle E^{\prime}(\lambda^{\prime})y,y\rangle+\int_{\Omega^{\prime}}{\rm Im}(h(\lambda^{\prime}))d\langle E^{\prime}(\lambda^{\prime})Jy,y\rangle

(5.33)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\int_{\Omega}{\rm Re}(h(\psi(\lambda)))d\langle E(\lambda)y,y\rangle+\int_{\Omega}{\rm Im}(h(\psi(\lambda)))d\langle E(\lambda)Jy,y\rangle

for all
y\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
such that
h
is
\langle E^{\prime}(\cdot)y,y\rangle
-integrable on
\Omega^{\prime}
. We may use (
5.16
) and (
5.33
) to realise that
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}_{E^{\prime}}(h))=\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}_{E}(h\circ\psi))
, where
\mathbb{I}_{E^{\prime}}
and
\mathbb{I}_{E}
denote the spectral integrals with respect to
E^{\prime}
and
E
, respectively. Notice that (
5.20
) with
g(\lambda)=1
and (
5.33
) can be used to obtain
\langle\mathbb{I}_{E^{\prime}}(h)y,y\rangle=\langle\mathbb{I}_{E}(h\circ\psi)y,y\rangle
for all
y\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}_{E}(h\circ\psi))
(which coincides with
\mathbb{I}_{E^{\prime}}(h)
). Finally, the polarisation formula (
2.20
), to deduce (
5.32
).

∎

6.
Spectral theorem for a bounded self-adjoint operator

The main goal of this section is to formulate and prove the spectral theorem for bounded self-adjoint operators on a Clifford module (see Theorem
6.0.1
).

Theorem 6.0.1

(spectral theorem for bounded self-adjoint operators)
.

Let
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
be self-adjoint. Then there exists a spectral measure
E
on the Borel
\sigma
-algebra
\mathscr{B}(\sigma_{S}(T))
such that

(6.1)

T=\int_{\sigma_{S}(T)}t\,dE(t).

The spectral measure
E
is unique in the sense that if
F
is a spectral measure on
\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R})
such that
T=\int_{\mathbb{R}}t\,dF(t)
, then
E(M\cap\sigma_{S}(T))=F(M)
for all
M\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R})
. Moreover,
W\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
commutes with
T
if and only if
WE(M)=E(M)W
for every
M\in\mathscr{B}(\sigma_{S}(T))
.

Before we can prove Theorem
6.0.1
, we need a number of lemmas. In the following one, we wish to stress that the polynomial
p
appearing in the spectral mapping identity (
6.2
) is a polynomial with real coefficients.

Lemma 6.0.2
.

Let
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
be self-adjoint. Then

(6.2)

\sigma_{S}(p(T))=p(\sigma_{S}(T))\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad p\in\mathbb{R}[t].

Proof.

Suppose
p(t)=\sum_{j=0}^{k}p_{j}t^{j}\in\mathbb{R}[t]
, with
p_{k}\neq 0
. We begin by noting that
p(T):=\sum_{j=0}^{n}p_{j}T^{j}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is self-adjoint. Thus, Lemma
3.2.4
asserts that
\sigma_{S}(p(T))\subseteq\mathbb{R}
. If
k=0
, then (
6.2
) holds trivially.

Suppose
k>0
. We will first show that
p(\sigma_{S}(T))\subseteq\sigma_{S}(p(T))
. We will only show that
\sigma_{S}(p(T))\subseteq p(\sigma_{S}(T))
(the proof of the other containment is very standard and does not divert at all from the classical case). Choose
t_{0}\in\sigma_{S}(p(T))
. Consider the polynomial
\varphi(t):=p(t)-t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}[t]
. If
\varphi
has all real zeros, say
t_{1},\ldots t_{k}
(not necessarily distinct), then
\varphi(t)=c\prod_{j=1}^{k}(t-t_{j})
for some constant
c\in\mathbb{R}
. Therefore,

\varphi(T)=p(T)-t_{0}\,I=c\prod_{j=1}^{k}(T-t_{j}\,I).

Since
\varphi(T)
is not invertible,
T-t_{j^{\prime}}\,I
must not be invertible for some
j^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
. Thus,
t_{j^{\prime}}\in\sigma_{S}(T)
, in which case
p(t_{j^{\prime}})=t_{0}
.

Next, if
\varphi
has non-real zeros, say
\zeta_{1},\bar{\zeta}_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{\ell},\bar{\zeta}_{\ell}
. Then

\varphi(t)=\psi(t)\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}(t-\zeta_{k})(t-\bar{\zeta}_{m})

for some polynomial
\psi\in\mathbb{R}[t]
which is constant or has all real zeros. Write
\zeta_{j}=u_{j}+v_{j}\,\mathfrak{I}
, where
v_{j}\neq 0
for
j=1,\ldots,\ell
. Since

(t-\zeta_{j})(t-\bar{\zeta}_{j})=(t-u_{j})^{2}+v_{j}^{2}\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad j=1,\ldots,\ell,

we have

\varphi(T)=\psi(T)\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}\{(T-u_{j}\,I)^{2}+v_{j}^{2}\,I\}.

Since
T-u_{j}\,I\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is self-adjoint, we have that
(T-u_{j}\,I)^{2}
is a positive operator, in which case
(T-u_{j}\,I)^{2}+v_{j}^{2}\,I
is invertible for
j=1,\ldots,\ell
. Consequently,
\varphi(T)
not being invertible implies that
\psi(T)
is not invertible. Hence,
\psi
cannot be a constant polynomial. We may now proceed as in the case when
\varphi
has all real zeros to obtain the desired conclusion.
∎

Lemma 6.0.3
.

Let
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
be self-adjoint. Then

(6.3)

\|p(T)\|=\max_{t\in\sigma_{S}(T)}|p(t)|\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad p\in\mathbb{R}[t].

Proof.

In view of (
3.10
) and (
3.23
), we have

\displaystyle\|p(T)\|^{2}=

\displaystyle\;\|p(T)^{*}\,p(T)\|=\|p(T)^{2}\|=r_{S}(p(T)^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\max\{|\tau|:\tau\in\sigma_{S}(p(T)^{2})\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\max\{p(t_{0})^{2}:t_{0}\in\sigma_{S}(T)\},

i.e., (
6.3
) holds.
∎

Theorem 6.0.4

(functional calculus for bounded self-adjoint operators)
.

Let
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
be self-adjoint. Then corresponding to any
f,g\in\mathscr{C}(\sigma_{S}(T),\mathbb{R})
, there exist self-adjoint operators
f(T),g(T)\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
which exhibit the following properties
:

(i)

(fg)(T)=f(T)g(T)
.

(ii)

(f+g)(T)=f(T)+g(T)
