.

(iii)

\|f(T)\|=\|f\|_{\infty}
.

(iv)

If
f|_{\sigma_{S}(T)}\geq 0
, then
f(T)
is a positive operator.

Proof.

Fix
f\in\mathscr{C}(\sigma_{S}(T),\mathbb{R})
. By the Weierstraß approximation theorem,
\mathbb{R}[t]
is uniformly dense in
\mathscr{C}(\sigma_{S},\mathbb{R})
with supremum norm

\|f\|_{\infty}:=\sup_{t\in\sigma_{S}(T)}\,|f(t)|\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad f\in\mathscr{C}(\sigma_{S}(T),\mathbb{R}).

Thus, there exists a sequence of real-valued polynomials
(f_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
such that
f
is the uniform limit of
(f_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
on
\sigma_{S}(T)
, i.e.,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\,\max_{t_{0}\in\sigma_{S}(T)}|f(t_{0})-f_{n}(t_{0})|.

Therefore,
(f_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
is a Cauchy sequence and (
6.3
) applied to
f_{i}
implies that

\lim_{i,j\to\infty}\|f_{i}(T)-f_{j}(T)\|=0.

Since
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is complete, as must have that
(f_{i}(T))_{i=1}^{\infty}
has a limit in the uniform operator topology, which we will denote by
f(T)
. One can easily check that
f(T)
does not depend on the choice of the Cauchy sequence
(f_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
.

Assertions (i)-(iii) are a direct consequence of the definition of
f(T)
for
f\in\mathscr{C}(\sigma_{S}(T),\mathbb{R})
, given above, just as in the classical complex Hilbert space setting. To prove (iv), observe that if
f|_{\sigma_{S}(T)}\geq 0
, then there exists
g\in\mathbb{R}[t]
such that
f_{\sigma_{S}(T)}=g^{2}|_{\sigma_{S}(T)}
. Thus,

\displaystyle\langle f(T)x,x\rangle=

\displaystyle\;\langle g(T)^{2}x,x\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\langle g(T)x,g(T)x\rangle

\displaystyle\succeq

\displaystyle\;0\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}.

Thus,
f(T)
is a positive operator.
∎

We are now ready to prove Theorem
6.0.1
.

Proof of Theorem
6.0.1
.

Fix
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
and
x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
. We shall utilise the functional calculus given in Theorem
6.0.4
to make sense of
f(T)
for
f\in\mathscr{C}(\sigma_{S}(T),\mathbb{R})
. Let
\ell_{x}:\mathscr{C}(\sigma_{S}(T),\mathbb{R})\to\mathfrak{S}(\mathbb{R}_{n})
, where
\mathfrak{S}(\mathbb{R}_{n})
denotes the real vector space of self-adjoint Clifford number in
\mathbb{R}_{n}
, be the linear functional given by

\ell_{x}(f)=\langle f(T)x,x\rangle\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad f\in\mathscr{C}(\sigma_{S}(T),\mathbb{R}).

The fact that
\ell_{x}(f)\in\mathfrak{S}(\mathbb{R}_{n})
follows at once from the fact that
f(T)=f(T)^{*}
(see Theorem
6.0.4
and
\ell_{x}(f)=\langle f(T)x,x\rangle
.

We claim that
\ell_{x}
is a positive linear functional. If
f|_{\sigma_{S}(T)}\geq 0
, then
f|_{\sigma_{S}(T)}=g^{2}|_{\sigma_{S}(T)}
for some
g\in\mathscr{C}(\sigma_{S}(T),\mathbb{R})
. Thus,

\displaystyle\ell_{x}(f)=

\displaystyle\;\langle g(T)^{2}x,x\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\,\langle g(T)x,g(T)x\rangle

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\;0\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad x\in\mathcal{H}_{n},

in which case,
\ell_{x}:\mathscr{C}(\sigma_{S}(T),\mathbb{R})\to\mathfrak{S}(\mathbb{R}_{n})
is a positive linear functional. Consequently, it follows from Theorem
4.0.11
that there exists a unique positive
\mathbb{R}_{n}
-valued Borel measure
\mu_{x}
on
\mathscr{B}(\sigma_{S}(T))
such that

(6.4)

\ell_{x}(f)=\int_{\sigma_{S}(T)}f(t)\,d\mu_{x}(t)\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad f\in\mathscr{C}(\sigma_{S}(T),\mathbb{R}).

Fix
x,y\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
. Utilising the polarisation formula (
2.20
), one can check that

(6.5)

\langle f(T)x,y\rangle=\int_{\sigma_{S}(T)}f(t)\,d\mu_{x,y}(t)\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad f\in\mathscr{C}(\sigma_{S}(T),\mathbb{R}),

where
\mu_{x,y}
denotes the
\mathbb{R}_{n}
-valued measure on
\mathscr{B}(\sigma_{S}(T))
given by

(6.6)

\mu_{x,y}:=\frac{\sum_{\alpha}(\mu_{x+ye_{\alpha}}-\mu_{x-ye_{\alpha}})e_{\alpha}}{4\,\dim\mathfrak{S}(\mathbb{R}_{n})}\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad x,y\in\mathcal{H}_{n}.

Moreover, one can use (
6.6
) and the uniqueness of the positive Borel measure in (
6.4
) to show that for any
x,y\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
, there is one and only one
\mathbb{R}_{n}
-valued measure on
\mathscr{B}(\sigma_{S}(T))
such that (
6.5
) holds.

An immediate consequence of the uniqueness of
\mu_{x,y}
in (
6.5
) is for any
x,y,z\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
and
a,b\in\mathbb{R}_{n}
, we have

\mu_{xa+yb,z}(M)=\mu_{x,z}(M)\,a+\mu_{y,z}(M)b

and

\mu_{x,ya+zb}(M)=\bar{a}\mu_{x,y}(M)+\bar{b}\mu_{x,z}(M)

for all
M\in\mathscr{B}(\sigma_{S}(T))
. We can also use (
6.5
) to show that
for any
x,y\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
, we have

\displaystyle|\mu_{x,y}(M)|\leq

\displaystyle\;|\mu_{x,y}(\sigma_{S}(T))|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;|\langle x,y\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;\|x\|\|y\|\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad M\in\mathscr{B}(\sigma_{S}(T)).

Thus, for any
M\in\mathscr{B}(\sigma_{S}(T))
we may use Theorem
3.0.26
to obtain an operator
E(M)\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
such that

(6.7)

\langle E(M)x,y\rangle:=\mu_{x,y}(M).

We now wish to show that
E:\mathscr{B}(\sigma_{S}(T))\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is a spectral measure (see Definition
5.1.2
). To see that
E
is a spectral measure, we shall make use of Lemma
5.1.6
. Since
\langle E(\cdot)x,x\rangle=\mu_{x}
is a positive Borel measure (and hence
\mu_{x}
is countably additive) for any
x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
, it suffices to show that
E(\sigma_{S}(T))=I
,
E(M)=E(M)^{*}
and
E(M)^{2}=E(M)
for
M\in\mathscr{B}(\sigma_{S}(T))
.

Putting
f(t)=1
into (
6.5
), we obtain
\langle x,y\rangle=\mu_{x,y}(\sigma_{S}(T))=\langle E(\sigma_{S}(T)x,y\rangle
for all
x,y\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
and hence
E(\sigma_{S}(T))=I
. Next, since

\overline{\langle f(T)x,y\rangle}=\int_{\sigma_{S}(T)}f(t)d\bar{\mu}_{x,y}(t)\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad f\in\mathscr{C}(\sigma_{S}(T),\mathbb{R}).

On the other hand,

\displaystyle\overline{\langle f(T)x,y\rangle}=

\displaystyle\;\langle y,f(T)x\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\langle f(T)y,x\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\int_{\sigma_{S}(T)}f(t)d\mu_{y,x}(t)\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad f\in\mathscr{C}(\sigma_{S}(T),\mathbb{R}).

Thus, for any
x,y\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
, we have

\int_{\sigma_{S}(T)}f(t)d\bar{\mu}_{x,y}(t)=\int_{\sigma_{S}(T)}f(t)d\mu_{y,x}(t)\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad f\in\mathscr{C}(\sigma_{S}(T),\mathbb{R})

and the aforementioned uniqueness of the
\mathbb{R}_{n}
-valued measure
\mu_{x,y}
such that (
6.5
) forces
\bar{\mu}_{x,y}=\mu_{y,x}
. Consequently, (
6.7
) implies that
E(M)=E(M)^{*}
for all
M\in\mathscr{B}(\sigma_{S}(T))
.

We will now prove that
E(M)^{2}=E(M)
for all
M\in\mathscr{B}(\sigma_{S}(T))
. Using the definition of
f(T)
for
f\in\mathscr{C}(\sigma_{S}(T),\mathbb{R})
, one can extend the identity in Theorem
6.0.4
(i) to the case when
f,g\in\mathscr{C}(\sigma_{S}(T),\mathbb{R})
. Thus, for any
x,y\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
, we have

\langle f(T)g(T)x,y\rangle=\langle(fg)(T)x,y\rangle=\int_{\sigma_{S}(T)}f(t)g(t)d\mu_{x,y}(t)

for all
f,g\in\mathscr{C}(\sigma_{S}(T),\mathbb{R}).
On the other hand,

\langle f(T)g(T)x,y\rangle=\int_{\sigma_{S}(T)}f(t)d\mu_{g(T)x,y}\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad f,g\in\mathscr{C}(\sigma_{S}(T),\mathbb{R}).

Thus, using the uniqueness of the
\mathbb{R}_{n}
-valued measure
\mu_{x,y}
in (
6.5
), we have
d\mu_{g(T)x,y}=g\,d\mu_{x,y}
for all
x,y\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
. Consequently, for any
x,y\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
, we have
\langle E(M)g(T)x,y\rangle=\int_{M}g(t)d\mu_{x,y}
for all
M\in\mathscr{B}(\sigma_{S}(T))
. Putting these observations together, for any
x,y\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
, we have

\int_{\sigma_{S}(T)}g(t)d\mu_{x,E(M)y}=\int_{\sigma_{S}(T)}g(t)\chi_{M}(t)d\mu_{x,y}(t)

for all
g\in\mathscr{C}(\sigma_{S}(T),\mathbb{R})
. Thus, using the uniqueness of the
\mathbb{R}_{n}
-valued measure in (
6.5
), we have
d\mu_{x,E(M)y}=\chi_{M}d\mu_{x,y}
, i.e.,

\mu_{x,E(M)y}(N)=\int_{N}\chi_{M}(t)d\mu_{x,y}(t)=\mu_{x,y}(M\cap N)\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad N\in\mathscr{B}(\sigma_{S}(T)),

i.e.,
\langle E(N)x,E(M)y\rangle=\langle E(M\cap N)x,y\rangle
. Using the fact that
E(M)=E(M)^{*}
, we arrive at
\langle E(M)E(N)x,y\rangle=\langle E(M\cap N)x,y\rangle
for all
M,N\in\mathscr{B}(\sigma_{S}(T))
. Setting
N=M
, we get
E(M)^{2}=E(M)
for all
M\in\mathscr{B}(\sigma_{S}(T))
. Thus,
E
is a spectral measure on
\sigma_{S}(T)
.

Formula (
6.1
) follows at once from (
6.5
) with
f(t)=t
together with (
6.7
).

Let
F
be another spectral measure on
\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R})
such that
(
6.1
)
holds with
F
in place of
E
. We shall let
\mathbb{I}_{F}(f)
denote the spectral integral for

f\in\mathfrak{F}(\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}),F)
(see Theorem
5.3.7
). Let
g:\sigma_{S}(T)\to\mathbb{R}
given by
g(t)=t
. Then

T=\mathbb{I}_{F}(g)=\int_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R})}t\,dF(t).

Theorem
5.3.14
asserts that

\displaystyle\sigma_{S}(\mathbb{I}(g))=

\displaystyle\;\{s\in\mathbb{R}:F(\{t\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}):|g(t)^{2}-2{\rm Re}(s)g(t)+|s|^{2}|<\varepsilon\})\neq 0

\displaystyle\;\;\;\text{ for all $\varepsilon>0$}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\{s\in\mathbb{R}:F(\{t\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}):|t^{2}-2st+s^{2}|<\varepsilon\})\neq 0

\displaystyle\;\;\;\text{ for all $\varepsilon>0$}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\{s\in\mathbb{R}:F(\{t\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}):|t-s|^{2}<\varepsilon\})\neq 0

\displaystyle\;\;\;\text{ for all $\varepsilon>0$}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;{\rm supp}\,F,

where we used (
5.4
) to obtain the last equality. Thus, as
\sigma_{S}(T)=\sigma_{S}(\mathbb{I}_{F}(g))
, we have
\sigma_{S}(T)={\rm supp}\,F
. On the other hand, let
\mathbb{I}(g)
denote the spectral integral of
g\in\mathfrak{F}(\sigma_{S}(T),E)
. Then, using the argument above, with
F
replaced by
E
, we obtain
\sigma_{S}(T)={\rm supp}\,E
and hence
\sigma_{S}(T)={\rm supp}\,E={\rm supp}\,F
. Using Theorem
5.2.4
(v), we obtain for any
x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
,

\int_{\sigma_{S}(T)}|f(t)|^{2}d({\rm Re}\langle E(t)x,x\rangle)=\int_{\sigma_{S}(T)}|f(t)|^{2}d({\rm Re}\langle F(t)x,x\rangle)

for every
f\in\mathfrak{B}
and, in particular, for every
f\in\mathscr{C}(\sigma_{S}(T),\mathbb{R})
. Thus, Theorem
4.0.7
forces
\langle E(M)x,x\rangle=\langle F(M)x,x\rangle
for all
M\in\mathscr{B}(\sigma_{S}(T))
. Consequently, the polarisation formula (
2.20
) forces
E=F|_{\sigma_{S}(T)}
.

Finally, suppose
WT=WT
for
W\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
. Using Theorem
5.2.4
(iv), we have for any
x,y\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
,

\langle f(T)Wx,y\rangle=\int_{\sigma_{S}(T)}{\rm Re}(f(t))d\langle E(t)Wx,y\rangle\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad f\in\mathfrak{B}.

In view of Definition
5.2.3
, we have that
Wf(T)=f(T)W
for every

f\in\mathfrak{B}
and hence, for any
x,y\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
,

\langle f(T)Wx,y\rangle=\langle f(T)x,W^{*}y\rangle=\int_{\sigma_{S}(T)}{\rm Re}(f(t))d\langle E(t)x,W^{*}y\rangle\quad{\rm for}\;f\in\mathfrak{B}.

Putting these observations together, we have, in particular,

\int_{\sigma_{S}(T)}f(t)d\langle E(t)Wx,y\rangle=\int_{\sigma_{S}(T)}f(t)d\langle E(t)x,W^{*}y\rangle\quad{\rm for}\;f\in\mathscr{C}(\sigma_{S}(T),\mathbb{R}).

Thus, using Corollary
4.0.8
, we have
\langle E(M)Wx,y\rangle=\langle E(M)x,W^{*}y
, i.e.,
\langle E(M)Wx,y\rangle=\langle WE(M)x,y\rangle
for every
x,y\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
and
M\in\mathscr{B}(\sigma_{S}(T))
, i.e.,
E(M)W=WE(M)
for every
M\in\mathscr{B}(\sigma_{S}(T))
.

∎

Remark 6.0.5
.

Let
T
and
E
be as in Theorem
6.0.1
. We wish to record that in the proof of Theorem
6.0.1
we showed that the support of the spectral measure of
A
is precisely the
S
-spectrum of
T
, i.e.,

{\rm supp}\,E=\sigma_{S}(T).

Corollary 6.0.6
.

Let
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
be a positive operator. Then there exists a unique positive operator
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
such that
A^{2}=T
. Moreover, if
(f_{j})_{j=1}^{\infty}
is any sequence of polynomials such that
f_{j}(t)\to t^{1/2}
uniformly on
[0,d]
, where
d=\max\sigma_{S}(T)
, then

(6.8)

\lim_{j\to\infty}\|f_{j}(T)-T^{1/2}\|=0.

Proof.

The spectral theorem for a bounded self-adjoint operator (see Theorem
6.0.1
asserts that there exists a uniquely determined spectral measure
E
on
\sigma_{S}(T)
such that
T=\int_{\sigma_{S}(T)}t\,dE(t)
. In view of Lemma
3.2.5
, we have
\sigma_{S}(T)\subseteq[0,d]
, where
d\geq 0
. Notice that have that
f(t)=t^{1/2}\in\mathfrak{B}(\sigma_{S}(T),\mathscr{B}(\sigma_{S}(T)),\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}})
(see Definition
5.2.3
), where
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathbb{S}
, and

A:=f(T)=\int_{\sigma_{S}(T)}t^{1/2}\,dE(t).

Thus,

\langle Ax,x\rangle=\int_{[0,d]}t^{1/2}d\langle E(t)x,x\rangle\succeq 0\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}

and hence
A
is a positive operator and we may use Theorem
5.2.4
(i) to check that
A^{2}=T
. The limit (
6.8
) is an immediate consequence of Theorem
5.2.4
(vii). We will now prove that there is only one positive operator
W\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
such that
W^{2}=T
. Suppose
\widetilde{A}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is a positive operator such that
\widetilde{A}^{2}=T
. Then it is easy to see that
\widetilde{A}T=T\widetilde{A}
and hence
\widetilde{A}g(T)=g(T)\widetilde{A}
for any
g\in\mathbb{R}[t]
. Consequently, we have

\widetilde{A}\left(\lim_{j\to\infty}f_{j}(T)\right)=\left(\lim_{j\to\infty}f_{j}(T)\right)\widetilde{A},

where
(f_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}
is any sequence of polynomials in
\mathbb{R}[t]
such that
f_{j}(t)\to t^{1/2}
uniformly on
[0,d]
, i.e.,

(6.9)

\widetilde{A}A=A\tilde{A}.

For any
x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
, let
y:=(A-\widetilde{A})x
. Thus, using (
6.9
), we obtain

(6.10)

\langle Ay,y\rangle+\langle\widetilde{A}y,y\rangle=\langle(A+\widetilde{A})(A-\widetilde{A})x,y\rangle=\langle(A^{2}-\widetilde{A}^{2})x,y\rangle=0.

Therefore, as
\langle Ay,y\rangle\succeq 0
and
\langle\widetilde{A}y,y\rangle\succeq 0
, we must have

(6.11)

\langle Ay,y\rangle=\langle\widetilde{A}y,y\rangle=0.

But then, as
A=D^{*}D
for some
D\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
and
\widetilde{A}=\widetilde{D}^{*}\widetilde{D}
for some
\widetilde{D}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
, we have that (
6.11
) implies that
\langle Dy,Dy\rangle=\langle\widetilde{D}y,\widetilde{D}y\rangle=0\in\mathbb{R}_{n}
, i.e.,

{\rm Re}\,\langle Dy,Dy\rangle={\rm Re}\,\langle\widetilde{D}y,\widetilde{D}y\rangle=0.

Thus,
\|Dy\|=\|Dy\|=0
, i.e.,
Dy=\widetilde{D}y=0
. But then we have

Ay=D^{*}Dy=\widetilde{D}^{*}D=\widetilde{A}y=0.

Therefore, for any
x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
, we have

\displaystyle\|(A-\widetilde{A})x\|^{2}=

\displaystyle\;{\rm Re}\,\langle(A-\widetilde{A})x,(A-\widetilde{A})x\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;{\rm Re}\,\langle(A-\widetilde{A})^{2}x,x\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;{\rm Re}\,\langle(A-\widetilde{A})y,x\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;0

and hence
(A-\widetilde{A})x=0
for every
x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
, i.e.,
A=\widetilde{A}
.
∎

Corollary 6.0.7
.

Suppose
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
and
B\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
are self-adjoint operators with spectral measures
E_{A}
and
E_{B}
, respectively. Then
AB=BA
if and only if
E_{A}(M)E_{B}(M)=E_{B}(M)E_{A}(M)
for all
M\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R})
.

Proof.

The last assertion in Theorem
6.0.1
with
T=A
and
W=B
ensures that
AB=BA
if and only if
BE_{A}(M)=E_{A}(M)B
. Applying the last assertion in Theorem
6.0.1
with
T=B
and
W=E_{B}(M)
we see that

AB=BA\Longleftrightarrow BE_{A}(M)=E_{A}(M)B\Longleftrightarrow E_{A}(M)E_{B}(M)=E_{B}(M)E_{A}(M)

for any
M\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R})
.
∎

7.
Polar decomposition for bounded operators

Let
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
. The aim of this section it show that there exists a uniquely determined positive operator
P\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
and a partial isometry
Q
such that
T=UQ
. This will be important for the spectral theorem for a normal operator in Section
9
.

Theorem 7.0.1

(polar decomposition for bounded operators)
.

Every
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
admits a factorisation

(7.1)

T=UQ,

where
Q:=|T|
is uniquely determined, where
|T|:=(T^{*}T)^{1/2}
, and
U:\overline{{\rm Ran}(Q)}\to\overline{{\rm Ran}(T)}
is a partial isometry. In the particular case that
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is normal, we can choose
U
such that
U
is unitary and we have the following
:

(7.2)

WT=TW\;{\rm and}\;WT^{*}=T^{*}W\Longrightarrow WQ=QW\;{\rm for}\;W\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})

and

(7.3)

WT=TW\;{\rm and}\;WT^{*}=T^{*}W\Longrightarrow WU=UW\;{\rm for}\;W\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n}).

Moreover,
T
is normal if and only if
QU=UQ
.

Proof.

Note that
T^{*}T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is a positive operator and has a unique positive square root
Q:=(T^{*}T)^{1/2}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
(see Corollary
6.0.6
). For any
x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
, we have

\displaystyle\|Tx\|^{2}=

\displaystyle\;{\rm Re}\,\langle Tx,Tx\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;{\rm Re}\,\langle T^{*}Tx,x\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;{\rm Re}\,\langle Q^{2}x,x\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;{\rm Re}\,\langle Qx,Qx\rangle

(7.4)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\|Qx\|^{2}.

Thus, if we let
x=y-z
, with
y,z\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
, then (
7.4
) implies that
Ty=Tz
whenever
Qy=Qz
for any
y,z\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
. Consequently, we may let
U:{\rm Ran}\,Q\to{\rm Ran}\,T
denote the operator belonging to
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
given by
U(Qx)=Tx
for
x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
. Next, extend
U
to all of
\mathcal{H}_{n}
via

(7.5)

Uy=\begin{cases}Tx&\quad{\rm if}\quad y=Qx,\\
0&\quad{\rm if}\quad y\in\overline{{\rm Ran}\,\,Q}^{\perp}.\end{cases}

Since
\langle Uy,z\rangle=\langle y,U^{*}z\rangle=0
for all
y\in{\rm Ran}\,\,Q
and
z\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
, we have
U^{*}:\mathcal{H}_{n}\to\overline{{\rm Ran}\,\,Q}^{\perp}
and hence
{\rm Ran}\,\,U\subseteq\overline{{\rm Ran}\,\,Q}
.

We now wish to show that

(7.6)

U^{*}Ux=x\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad x\in\overline{{\rm Ran}\,\,Q}.

Suppose
y,z\in{\rm Ran}\,Q
. Then

\langle y,z\rangle=\langle Uy,Uz\rangle=\langle y,U^{*}Uz\rangle

and hence
\langle U^{*}Uy-y,w\rangle=0
for all
w\in{\rm Ran}\,Q
. We have already noted that
U^{*}:\mathcal{H}_{n}\to\overline{{\rm Ran}\,Q}
. Hence for all
y\in\overline{{\rm Ran}\,Q}
, we have
U^{*}Uy-y\in\overline{{\rm Ran}\,Q}
. Consequently, we have (
7.6
). Putting all of these observations together, we arrive at the factorisation (
7.1
). The asserted uniqueness can be justified precisely the same way as the complex Hilbert space case.

Let us now suppose that
T
is normal. We first note that
\langle TT^{*}x,x\rangle=\langle T^{*}Tx,x\rangle
implies that
\langle Tx,Tx\rangle=\langle T^{*}x,T*x\rangle
and hence
{\rm Re}\,\langle Tx,Tx\rangle={\rm Re}\,\langle T^{*}x,T*x\rangle
, i.e.,
\|Tx\|=\|T^{*}x\|
for all
x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
. Therefore,
{\rm Ker}\,\,T={\rm Ker}\,\,T^{*}
and hence

{\rm Ran}\,\,Q^{\perp}={\rm Ker}\,\,Q={\rm Ker}\,\,T={\rm Ker}\,\,T^{*}=\overline{{\rm Ran}\,\,T}^{\perp}.

Thus, we can extend
U:\overline{{\rm Ran}\,Q}\to\overline{{\rm Ran}\,Q}
from (
7.5
) to
U:\mathcal{H}_{n}\to\mathcal{H}_{n}
(with a slight abuse of notation we shall denote the extension of
U
by
U
as well) via

Uy=y\quad{\rm if}\quad y\in\overline{{\rm Ran}\,Q}^{\perp}.

As
\mathcal{H}_{n}=\overline{{\rm Ran}\,Q}\oplus\overline{{\rm Ran}\,Q}^{\perp}
, we have that
U:\mathcal{H}_{n}\to\mathcal{H}_{n}
is unitary.

The implication
7.2
is an immediate consequence of the final assertion of Theorem
6.0.1
and Corollary
6.0.6
. We will now show (
7.3
). In view of (
7.2
), we have

(7.7)

(WU-UW)Q=0.

Thus, as
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is normal, we have
\mathcal{H}_{n}={\rm Ker}T\oplus\overline{{\rm Ran}\,T}
. Thus we may write any
x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
as
x=y+z
, where
y\in{\rm Ker}\,T={\rm Ker}\,Q
and
z\in\overline{{\rm Ran}\,T}
, we may use the fact that
{\rm Ker}\,Q={\rm Ker}\,T
and (
7.7
) to obtain

(WU-UW)x=(WU-UW)(y+z)=0,

i.e.,
WU=UW
.

The final assertion can be justified in the same way as the complex Hilbert space case (bearing in mind that one must use Corollary
6.0.6
which guarantees that bounded positive operators have a unique positive square root).
∎

8.
An additive decomposition for bounded operators and imaginary operators

In this section, we will show that all bounded operators on a Clifford module admit an additive decomposition which is analogous to the well-known additive decomposition

T=\left(\frac{T+T^{*}}{2}\right)+i\left(\frac{T-T^{*}}{2i}\right),

which holds in the complex Hilbert space case. In the particular case that the bounded operator is normal, this additive decomposition will be useful for proving the spectral theorem for a bounded normal operators in Section
9
.

Lemma 8.0.1
.

Let
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
be unitary. Then

(8.1)

\sigma_{S}(T)\subseteq\{s\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}:|s|=1\}.

Proof.

We claim that
\rho_{S}(T)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\setminus\{s\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}:|s|=1\}
. Since
TT^{*}=I
, it is obvious that
\|T\|=1
. Thus, Theorem
3.1.8
ensures that

\{s\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}:|s|>1\}\subseteq\rho_{S}(T).

Since
\mathcal{Q}_{0}(T)=T^{2}
is a unitary operator, we have that
\mathcal{Q}_{0}(T)
is invertible, i.e.,
0\in\rho_{S}(T)
. For any
s=s_{0}+s_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, with
0<|s|<1
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{s}(T)=

\displaystyle\;T^{2}-2{\rm Re}(s)T+|s|^{2}\,I

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;(|s|^{2})T^{2}\{(T^{*})^{2}+2{\rm Re}(s^{-1})T^{*}+|s^{-1}|^{2}T^{2}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;|s|^{2}T^{2}\mathcal{Q}_{s^{-1}}(T^{*}).

Thus, as
T^{*}
is unitary and
|s^{-1}|>1
, we have that
s^{-1}
belongs to
\rho_{S}(T^{*})
, in which case
s\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, with
0<|s|<1
, belongs to
\rho_{S}(T)
. Putting everything together we have (
8.1
).
∎

Definition 8.0.2

(imaginary operator)
.

We will call an operator
J_{0}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
a
partial imaginary operator
if
J_{0}
is a partial isometry and
J_{0}^{*}=-J_{0}
. We will call
J\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
an
imaginary operator
if
J
is unitary and
J^{*}=-J
.

Theorem 8.0.3
.

Corresponding to any operator
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
, there exist a self-adjoint operator
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
, a partial imaginary operator
J_{0}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
and a positive operator
B\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
such that

(8.2)

T=A+J_{0}B,

where
A:=(T+T^{*})/2
and
B:=|T-T^{*}|/2
are uniquely determined by
T
. Moreover, we may choose
J_{0}
in (
8.2
) to be an imaginary operator.

Proof.

If we let
A:=(T+T^{*})/2
, then
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is obviously self-adjoint. Consider the anti self-adjoint operator
Y:=T-A=(T-T^{*})/2\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
.
Since
Y
is normal, we may use Theorem
7.0.1
to find a positive operator
B\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
and a partial isometry
J_{0}
such that
Y=J_{0}B
. Moreover, by Theorem
7.0.1
, we have
BJ_{0}=J_{0}B
. Since
Y
is anti self-adjoint, we must have
J_{0}^{*}=-J_{0}
. Thus,
J_{0}
is a partial imaginary operator.

The uniqueness of
A
is obvious. The uniqueness of
B
follows from Theorem
7.0.1
. Finally, the fact that we may choose
J_{0}
to be unitary follows from Theorem
7.0.1
applied to the bounded normal operator
(T-T^{*})/2
.
∎

In the case that
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is normal, we have the following refinement of Theorem
8.0.3
.

Theorem 8.0.4
.

Corresponding to any normal operator
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
, there exist a self-adjoint operator
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
, a partial imaginary operator
J_{0}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
and a positive operator
B\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
such that
A
,
J_{0}
and
B
mutually commute and satisfy

(8.3)

T=A+J_{0}B.

In this case,
A
and
B
are as in Theorem
8.0.3
and

(8.4)

TJ_{0}=J_{0}T.

Moreover, we may choose
J_{0}
to be an imaginary operator
(
in this case we shall write
J
in place of
J_{0}
)
.

Proof.

In view of Theorem
8.0.4
, we only have to show that
A:=(T+T^{*})/2,B:=|T-T^{*}|/2
and
J_{0}
mutually commute and that (
8.4
) holds. The fact that
B
and
J_{0}
commute follows from the fact that
J_{0}B
is a polar factorisation for the bounded normal operator
Y:=(T-T^{*})/2
. Since
T
is normal, we have
A(T-T^{*})=(T-T^{*})A
and
A(T-T^{*})^{*}=(T-T^{*})^{*}A
. Thus, the fact that
A
,
B
and
J_{0}
mutually commute follows at once from (
7.2
) and (
7.3
). The final assertion (
8.4
) is an immediate consequence of (
8.3
) and the fact that
A
,
J
and
B
mutually commute.
∎

Theorem 8.0.5
.

Let
J
be an imaginary operator on a Clifford module
\mathcal{H}_{n}
, fix
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathbb{S}
and define

(8.5)

\mathcal{H}_{\pm}(J,\mathfrak{I}):=\{x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}:J\,x=x(\pm\mathfrak{I})\}.

Then the following statements hold
:

(i)

\mathcal{H}_{\pm}(J,\mathfrak{I})
are nontrivial, i.e.,
\mathcal{H}_{\pm}(J,\mathfrak{I})\neq\{0\}
and

(8.6)

\mathcal{H}_{+}(J,\mathfrak{I})\cap\mathcal{H}_{-}(J,\mathfrak{I})=\{0\}.

(ii)

\mathcal{H}_{\pm}(J,\mathfrak{I})
are
\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}
closed right linear subspaces of
\mathcal{H}_{n}
, with respect to
\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}
, i.e., if
x,y\in\mathcal{H}_{\pm}(J,\mathfrak{I})
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}
, then
x\,\lambda+y\in\mathcal{H}_{\pm}(J,\mathfrak{I})
. Consequently,
\mathcal{H}_{\pm}(J,\mathfrak{I})
may be both be viewed as a complex Hilbert space with respect to the
\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}
-valued inner product given by

(8.7)

\langle x,y\rangle_{\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}}:={\rm Re}(\langle x,y\rangle)-{\rm Re}(\langle x,y\rangle\mathfrak{I})\mathfrak{I}\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad x,y\in\mathcal{H}_{\pm}(J,\mathfrak{I}).

(iii)

\mathcal{H}_{n}=\mathcal{H}_{+}(J,\mathfrak{I})\oplus\mathcal{H}_{-}(J,\mathfrak{I})
.

(iv)

For any orthonormal basis
(\eta_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
of the complex Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{+}(J,\mathfrak{I})
, we have that, for any choice of
\mathfrak{J}\in\mathbb{S}
with
\mathfrak{I}\,\mathfrak{J}=-\mathfrak{J}\mathfrak{I}
,
(\eta_{i}\,\mathfrak{J})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
is an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{H}_{-}(J,\mathfrak{I})
.

Moreover, for any orthonormal basis
(\tilde{\eta})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
of the complex Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{-}(J,\mathfrak{I})
, we have that, for any choice of
\mathfrak{J}\in\mathbb{S}
with
\mathfrak{I}\,\mathfrak{J}=-\mathfrak{J}\mathfrak{I}
,
(\eta_{i}\,\mathfrak{J})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
is an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{H}_{+}(J,\mathfrak{I})
.

(v)

For any orthonormal basis
(\eta)_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
of the complex Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{+}(J,\mathfrak{I})
,
(\eta_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
is an orthonormal basis of the Clifford module
\mathcal{H}_{n}
.

(vi)

\sigma_{S}(J)\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}=\{\mathfrak{I}\}
.

(vii)

For any orthonormal basis
(\eta_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
of the complex Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{+}(J,\mathfrak{I})
and
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathbb{S}
, we have

(8.8)

Jx=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\eta_{i}\,\mathfrak{I}\,\langle x,\eta_{i}\rangle\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}.

Proof.

We will first prove (i). Suppose there exists
x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}\setminus\{0\}
such that
x-Jx\mathfrak{I}\neq 0
. Then

\displaystyle J(x-xJx\mathfrak{I})=

\displaystyle\;Jx+x\mathfrak{I}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;(x-Jx\mathfrak{I})\mathfrak{I}.

Thus,
y:=x-Jx\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{H}_{n}\setminus\{0\}
and
Jy=y\mathfrak{I}
and hence
\mathcal{H}_{+}(J,\mathfrak{I})\neq\{0\}
.

On the other hand, if there exists
x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}\setminus\{0\}
such that
x=Jx\mathfrak{I}
, then choose
\mathfrak{J}\in\mathbb{S}
such that
\mathfrak{I}\mathfrak{J}=-\mathfrak{J}\mathfrak{I}
. Thus,

Jx\mathfrak{I}\mathfrak{J}=x\mathfrak{J}
implies that
-J(x\mathfrak{J})\mathfrak{I}=x\mathfrak{J}
and hence

J(x\mathfrak{J})=(x\mathfrak{J})\mathfrak{I}.

Thus, we again arrive at the conclusion
\mathcal{H}_{+}(J,\mathfrak{I})\neq\{0\}
. The justification that
\mathcal{H}_{-}(J,\mathfrak{I})\neq\{0\}
can be carried out in a similar fashion.

We will now show (
8.6
). Suppose to the contrary that there exists a nonzero vector
x\in\mathcal{H}_{+}(J,\mathfrak{I})\cap\mathcal{H}_{-}(J,\mathfrak{I})
. Then we have
x\mathfrak{I}=x(-\mathfrak{I})
, i.e.,
x\mathfrak{I}=0
which forces
x=0
, a contradiction.

We will now prove (ii). Suppose
x,y\in\mathcal{H}_{+}(J,\mathfrak{I})
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}
. Then

J(x\lambda+y)=x\mathfrak{I}\lambda+y\mathfrak{I}=(x\lambda+y)\mathfrak{I}.

Thus,
\mathcal{H}_{+}(J,\mathfrak{I})
is a
\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}
right linear subspace of
\mathcal{H}_{n}
. The fact that
\mathcal{H}_{+}(J,\mathfrak{I})
is closed follows at once via the continuity of multiplication. The justification that
\mathcal{H}_{-}(J,\mathfrak{I})
is a closed right-linear subspace, with respect to
\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}
, can be carried out in a similar fashion. Finally, the fact that
\mathcal{H}_{+}(J,\mathfrak{I})
and
\mathcal{H}_{-}(J,\mathfrak{I})
may be viewed as a complex Hilbert spaces with respect to the
\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}
-valued inner product given by (
8.7
) follows immediately from
\mathcal{H}_{\pm}(J,\mathfrak{I})
being closed right subspaces of
\mathcal{H}_{n}
and checking that (
8.7
) is an
\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}
-valued inner product (to this end, it will be helpful to use the easily verified fact that
\langle x,y\rangle\lambda=\pm\lambda\langle x,y\rangle
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}
and
x,y\in\mathcal{H}_{\pm}(J,\mathfrak{I})
).

We will now prove (iii). Let
y=(x-Jx\mathfrak{I})/2
and
z=(x+Jx\mathfrak{I})/2
. Then
y\in\mathcal{H}_{+}(J,\mathfrak{I})
since

Jy=\frac{1}{2}(Jx+x\mathfrak{I})=\frac{1}{2}(x-Jx\mathfrak{I})\mathfrak{I}.

Similarly,
z\in\mathcal{H}_{-}(J,\mathfrak{I})
since

Jz=\frac{1}{2}(Jx-x\mathfrak{I})=\frac{1}{2}(x+Jx\mathfrak{I})(-\mathfrak{I}).

Thus, as
x=y+z
and (
8.6
) holds we have
\mathcal{H}_{n}=\mathcal{H}_{+}(J,\mathfrak{I})\oplus\mathcal{H}_{-}(J,\mathfrak{I})
.

We will now prove (iv). Let
\mathfrak{J}\in\mathbb{S}
be such that
\mathfrak{I}\mathfrak{J}=-\mathfrak{J}\mathfrak{I}
. Then for any
y\in\mathcal{H}_{+}(J,\mathfrak{I})
, we have

Jy\,\mathfrak{J}=(y\mathfrak{I})\mathfrak{J}=(\eta_{i}\mathfrak{J})(-\mathfrak{I}).

Thus,
y\mathfrak{J}\in\mathcal{H}_{-}(J,\mathfrak{I})
. One can easily push this observation further and establish that
\varphi:\mathcal{H}_{+}(J,\mathfrak{I})\to\mathcal{H}_{-}(J,\mathfrak{I})
given by
\varphi(y)=y\mathfrak{J}
is an isomorphism. Consequently, if
(\eta_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
is an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{H}_{+}(J,\mathfrak{I})
, then
(\eta_{i}\,\mathfrak{J})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
is an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{H}_{-}(J,\mathfrak{I})
.

We will now prove (v). Let
(\eta_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
be any orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{H}_{+}(J,\mathcal{I})
. Then we may apply (iv) and (iii) to see that
(\eta_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
is also an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{H}_{n}
.

We will now prove (vi). Recall that
J^{*}=-J
and
JJ^{*}=I
. Thus, combining (
3.28
) and (
8.1
), we have

\sigma_{S}(J)\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}:{\rm Re}(\lambda)=0\}\cap\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}:|\lambda|=1\}=\{\mathfrak{I}\}.

We will now prove (vii). Let
(\eta_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
be any orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{H}_{+}(J,\mathcal{I})
. In view of (v), we have that
(\eta_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
is an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{H}_{n}
. Consequently, in view of Lemma
2.2.6
(iv), we have

x=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\eta_{i}\langle x,\eta_{i}\rangle\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}

and hence

Jx=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}J\,\eta_{i}\langle x,\eta_{i}\rangle=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\eta_{i}\,\mathfrak{I}\langle x,\eta_{i}\rangle\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}.

∎

9.
Spectral theorem for a bounded normal operator and some consequences

In this section we will be prove one of the main results of this manuscript, namely the spectral theorem for a bounded normal operator on a Clifford module.

Let
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathbb{S}
be arbitrary. In the following lemma, we shall identify
\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty)
with
\mathbb{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{I}}:=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{I}}:{\rm Im}(\lambda)\geq 0\}
in the natural way. Consequently, a spectral measure on
\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty))
may be viewed as a spectral measure on
\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{I}})
.

Lemma 9.0.1
.

Let
E_{1}
be a spectral measure on
\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R})
,
E_{2}
be a spectral measure on
\mathscr{B}([0,\infty))
and
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathbb{S}
. Suppose
E_{1}(M_{1})E_{2}(M_{2})=E_{2}(M_{2})E_{1}(M_{1})
for every
M_{1}\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R})
and
M_{2}\in\mathscr{B}([0,\infty))
and let
E(M_{1}\times M_{2}):=E(M_{1})E(M_{2})
be the uniquely determined spectral measure on
\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+})
(see Theorem
5.1.15
). For any real-valued function
f\in\mathfrak{F}(\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+},E)
, we have

(9.1)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(t)\,dE_{1}(t)=\int_{\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}f({\rm Re}(\lambda))dE(\lambda)

and

(9.2)

\int_{[0,\infty)}g(u)\,dE_{2}(u)=\int_{\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}g({\rm Im}(\lambda))dE(\lambda)

Proof.

We will verify (
9.1
) for simple functions. For any
M\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R})
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}\chi_{M}({\rm Re}(\lambda))\,dE(\lambda)=

\displaystyle\;E(M\times[0,\infty))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;E_{1}(M)E_{2}([0,\infty))=E_{1}(M)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\int_{\mathbb{R}}\chi_{M}(t)dE_{1}(t)

Thus, by linearity, (
9.1
) holds for simple functions. Passing to a limit, we obtain (
9.1
) for any
f\in\mathfrak{F}(\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}),E)
.

Formula (
9.2
) can be justified in much the same way as (
9.1
).

∎

We are now ready to state and proof the spectral theorem for a bounded normal operator on a Clifford module.

Theorem 9.0.2

(spectral theorem for bounded normal operators)
.

Let
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
be normal. Then corresponding to any choice of
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathbb{S}
, there exists a spectral measure
E
on the Borel
\sigma
-algebra
\mathscr{B}(\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{I}})
such that

(9.3)

T=\int_{\sigma_{S}(T)\,\cap\,\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}{\rm Re}(\lambda)\,dE(\lambda)+\int_{\sigma_{S}(T)\,\cap\,\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}{\rm Im}(\lambda)\,dE(\lambda)\,J,

where
J
an anti self-adjoint and unitary operator obeying (
8.3
).
E
is unique in the sense that if
F
is a spectral measure on
\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{I}})
such that

T=\int_{\mathbb{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{I}}}{\rm Re}(\lambda)\,dF(\lambda)+\int_{\mathbb{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{I}}}{\rm Im}(\lambda)dF(\lambda)J,

then
E(M\cap\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{I}})=F(M)
for all
M\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{I}})
. Moreover,
W\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
commutes with
T
if and only if
WE(M)=E(M)W
for every
M\in\mathscr{B}(\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{I}})
.

Proof.

We begin by using (
8.3
) to write
T=A+JB
, where
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is self-adjoint,
B\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is positive and
J
is anti self-adjoint and unitary and
A,B
and
J
all mutually commute. Since
A
is self-adjoint and
B
is positive, we may use Theorem
6.0.1
to obtain spectral measures
E_{1}
(resp.,
E_{2}
) on
\mathscr{B}(\sigma_{S}(A))
(resp.,
\mathscr{B}(\sigma_{S}(B))
) such that

A=\int_{\sigma_{S}(A)}t\,dE_{1}(t)\quad{\rm and}\quad B=\int_{\sigma_{S}(B)}t\,dE_{2}(t).

Note that since
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is self-adjoint, we have that
\sigma_{S}(T)
is a non-empty compact subset of
\mathbb{R}
and since
B\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is positive, we have that
\sigma_{S}(B)
is a non-empty compact subset of
[0,\infty)
(see Lemma
3.2.4
). Moreover, in view of Remark
6.0.5
, we have

{\rm supp}\,E_{1}=\sigma_{S}(A)\quad{\rm and}\quad{\rm supp}\,E_{2}=\sigma_{S}(B).

Since
A
and
B
commute, we have that
E_{1}(M_{1})E_{2}(M_{2})=E_{1}(M_{1})E_{2}(M_{2})
for every
M\in\mathscr{B}(\sigma)
. Thus, we invoke Theorem
5.1.15
with
\Omega_{1}=\mathscr{B}(\sigma_{S}(A))
and
\Omega_{2}=\mathscr{B}(\sigma_{S}(B))
to obtain a uniquely determined spectral measure
E
on
\mathscr{B}(\sigma_{S}(A)\times\sigma_{S}(B))
given
by
E(M_{1}\times M_{2})=E(M_{1})E(M_{2})
for
M_{1}\in\mathscr{B}(\sigma_{S}(A))
and
M_{2}\in\mathscr{B}(\sigma_{S}(B))
.

Let
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathbb{S}
be arbitrary and identify
\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty)
with
\mathbb{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{I}}:=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{I}}:{\rm Im}(\lambda)\geq 0\}
in the natural way. Consequently, we will view
E
as a spectral measure on the non-empty compact subset
\sigma_{S}(A)\times\sigma_{S}(B)\subseteq\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{I}})
. Lemma
9.0.1
with
f(\lambda)={\rm Re}(\lambda)
and
g(\lambda)={\rm Im}(\lambda)
imply that

(9.4)

A=\int_{\mathbb{R}}t\,dE_{1}(t)=\int_{\mathbb{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{I}}}{\rm Re}(\lambda)dE(\lambda)

and

(9.5)

B=\int_{[0,\infty)}u\,dE_{2}(u)=\int_{\mathbb{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{I}}}{\rm Im}(\lambda)dE(\lambda),

respectively. Since
{\rm supp}\,E_{1}=\sigma_{S}(A)
,
{\rm supp}\,E_{2}=\sigma_{B}(A)
and
{\rm supp}\,E\subseteq\sigma_{S}(A)\times\sigma_{B}(A)
, we may rewrite (
9.4
) and (
9.5
) as

(9.6)

A=\int_{\sigma_{S}(A)}t\,dE_{1}(t)=\int_{\sigma_{S}(A)\times\sigma_{S}(B)}{\rm Re}(\lambda)dE(\lambda)

and

(9.7)

B=\int_{\sigma_{S}(B)}u\,dE_{2}(u)=\int_{\sigma_{S}(A)\times\sigma_{S}(B)}{\rm Im}(\lambda)dE(\lambda),

respectively. Thus , as
T=A+BJ
, we can use (
9.4
) and (
9.5
) to obtain

(9.8)

T=\int_{\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}{\rm Re}(\lambda)dE(\lambda)+\int_{\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}{\rm Im}(\lambda)dE(\lambda)\,J.

Theorem
5.3.14
with
f(\lambda)=\lambda
implies that

\displaystyle\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}=

\displaystyle\;\sigma_{S}(\mathbb{I}(f))\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\{s\in\mathbb{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{I}}:E(\{\lambda\in\Omega:|f(\lambda)^{2}-2{\rm Re}(s)f(\lambda)+|s|^{2}|<\varepsilon\})\neq 0

\displaystyle\;\;\text{for all $\varepsilon>0$}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\{s\in\mathbb{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{I}}:E(\{\lambda\in\Omega:|\lambda^{2}-2{\rm Re}(s)\lambda+|s|^{2}|<\varepsilon\})\neq 0

(9.9)

\displaystyle\;\;\text{for all $\varepsilon>0$}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\{s\in\mathbb{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{I}}:E(\{\lambda\in\Omega:|\lambda-s|\cdot|\lambda-\bar{s}|<\varepsilon\})\neq 0

\displaystyle\;\;\text{for all $\varepsilon>0$}\}

(9.10)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\{s\in\mathbb{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{I}}:E(\{\lambda\in\Omega:|\lambda-s|^{2}<\varepsilon\})\neq 0

\displaystyle\;\;\text{for all $\varepsilon>0$}\},

since, if we write
\lambda=\lambda_{0}+\lambda_{1}\mathfrak{I}\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}
, with
\lambda_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\lambda_{1}\geq 0
, and
s=s_{0}+s_{1}\mathfrak{I}\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}
, with
s_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
and
s_{1}\geq 0
, then

\displaystyle|\lambda-\bar{s}|^{2}=

\displaystyle\;(\lambda_{0}-s_{0})^{2}+\lambda_{1}^{2}+2\lambda_{1}s_{1}+s_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\;(\lambda_{0}-s_{0})^{2}+\lambda_{1}^{2}-2\lambda_{1}s_{1}+s_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;|\lambda-s|^{2}.

Thus, in view of (
5.4
), (
9.10
) implies that
\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}={\rm supp}\,E
. Thus, we may rewrite (
9.8
) to obtain (
9.3
).

We will now show that
E
is unique. Suppose
E^{\prime}
on
\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+})
is another spectral measure such that (
9.3
) holds. One can argue as in the proof of Theorem
6.0.1
to see that
{\rm supp}\,E^{\prime}=\sigma_{S}(T)
which is non-empty and compact by Theorem

3.1.8
. Consequently, we may use Theorem
5.2.4
(iv) with
y=x
and
f\in\mathscr{C}(\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+},\mathbb{R})
to obtain

\int_{\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}f(\lambda)d\langle E(\lambda)x,x\rangle=\int_{\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}f(\lambda)d\langle E^{\prime}(\lambda)x,x\rangle.

Thus, one can show that
E=E^{\prime}
as in the proof of Theorem
6.0.1
.

The proof of the final assertion can be completed in the same way as the proof of the final assertion in Theorem
6.0.1
.
∎

Remark 9.0.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{I},\mathfrak{J}\in\mathbb{S}
and
\gamma:\mathbb{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{I}}\to\mathbb{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{J}}
denote the bijective map given by

(9.11)

\gamma(\lambda_{0}+\lambda_{1}\mathfrak{I})=\lambda_{0}+\lambda_{1}\mathfrak{J},

where
\lambda_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\lambda_{1}\geq 0
. An immediate consequence of the proof of Theorem
9.0.2
, is that if
E_{\mathfrak{I}}
, where
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathbb{S}
, is a spectral measure for a normal operator
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
and
E_{\mathfrak{J}}
, where
\mathfrak{J}\in\mathbb{S}
, then since
\gamma(\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+})=\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}
and
{\rm supp}\,E_{\mathfrak{I}}=\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}
, we have

(9.12)

E_{\mathfrak{I}}(M)=E_{\mathfrak{J}}(\gamma(M))\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad M\in\mathscr{B}(\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}).

In view of the above observations, we are justified in calling a spectral measure
E
on
\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}

the spectral measure of
T
.

Corollary 9.0.4

(Borel functional calculus in the bounded case)
.

Let
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
be normal,
J
be an imaginary operator satisfying (
8.3
) and
E
be the spectral measure of
T
. Fix
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathbb{S}
and put
\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}:=\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}
. For any
f,g\in\mathfrak{B}(\Omega^{+}_{\mathfrak{I}},\mathscr{B}(\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}),\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E)

(
see Subsection
5.2
)
, we have the spectral integrals
\mathbb{I}(f),\mathbb{I}(g)\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
are normal operators with the following properties
:

(i)

\mathbb{I}(\bar{f})=\mathbb{I}(f)^{*}
.

(ii)

\mathbb{I}(fg)=\mathbb{I}(f)\mathbb{I}(g)
.

(iii)

\mathbb{I}(\mathfrak{I})=J
and
\mathbb{I}(cf+g)=({\rm Re}(c)I+{\rm Im}(c)\,J)\mathbb{I}(f)+\mathbb{I}(g)
for all
c\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}
and
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathbb{S}
.

(iv)

For all
x,y\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
, we have

\langle\mathbb{I}(f)x,y\rangle=\int_{\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}{\rm Re}(f(\lambda))d\langle E(\lambda)x,y\rangle+\int_{\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}{\rm Im}(f(\lambda))d\langle JE(\lambda)x,y\rangle

(v)

\|\mathbb{I}(f)x\|^{2}=\int_{\Omega}|f(\lambda)|^{2}d({\rm Re}\,\langle E(\lambda)x,x\rangle)
for all
x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
.

(vi)

\|\mathbb{I}(f)\|\leq\|f\|_{\infty}
. Moreover,
\|\mathbb{I}(f)\|=\|f\|_{\infty}
if and only if
f\in L_{\infty}(\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+},\mathscr{B}(\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+},\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E)
.

(viii)

For any sequence of functions
(f_{j})_{j=1}^{\infty}
, where
f_{j}\in\mathfrak{B}
for
j=1,2,\ldots
, which converges pointwise
E
-a.e. on
\Omega
to
f
and there exists
\kappa>0
such that
|f_{j}(\lambda)|\leq\kappa
for all
\lambda\in\Omega
and
j=1,2,\ldots
, we have

s-\lim_{j\to\infty}\mathbb{I}(f_{n})=\mathbb{I}(f).

(viii)

If we let
\Pi_{\pm}(J,\mathfrak{I})
denote the orthogonal projection onto the right complex subspace
\mathcal{H}_{\pm}(J,\mathfrak{I})
, respectively
(
see Theorem
8.0.5
(ii))
, then

\displaystyle\langle\mathbb{I}(f)x,y\rangle=

\displaystyle\;\int_{\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}d\langle E(\lambda)\Pi_{+}(J,\mathfrak{I})x,y\rangle\,f(\lambda)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\;\int_{\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}d\langle E(\lambda)\Pi_{-}(J,\mathfrak{I})x,y\rangle\overline{f(\lambda)}\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad x,y\in\mathcal{H}_{n},

where both integrals above are meant in the sense of (
4.5
).

(ix)

If
f\in\mathfrak{B}(\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+},\mathscr{B}(\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}),\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E)
is nonnegative
E
-a.e., then
\mathbb{I}(f)\succeq 0
.

(x)

\mathbb{I}(f)^{-1}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
if and only if
f(\lambda)\neq 0

E
-a.e. and

f^{-1}\in L_{\infty}(\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+},\mathscr{B}(\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}),\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E).

In this case,

\mathbb{I}(f)^{-1}=\mathbb{I}(1/f).

(xi)

The spectral measure
E_{\mathbb{I}(f)}
of
\mathbb{I}(f)
satisfies the identity

(9.13)

E_{\mathbb{I}(f)}(M)=E(f^{-1}(M\cap f(\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+})).

Proof.

Let
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
be normal and fix
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathbb{S}
. Then by Theorem
9.0.2
, we can find a spectral measure
E
on
\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}:=\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}
such that (
9.3
) holds. Thus, we may invoke Theorem
5.2.4
with
\Omega=\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}
and
\mathscr{A}=\mathscr{B}(\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+})
to obtain (i)-(v), the first assertion in (vi) and (vii). The fact that
\mathbb{I}(f)\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is normal is an easy consequence of (i) and (ii). Indeed, as

\mathbb{I}(f)\mathbb{I}(f)^{*}=\mathbb{I}(f\bar{f})=\mathbb{I}(\bar{f}f)=\mathbb{I}(f)^{*}\mathbb{I}(f),

we have that
\mathbb{I}(f)
is normal.

The second assertion in (vi) follows immediately from Lemma
5.3.10
. We will now prove (vii). We may use Theorem
8.0.5
(iii) to write
x=x_{+}+x_{-}
and
y=y_{+}+y_{-}
, where
x_{+},y_{+}\in\mathcal{H}_{+}(J,\mathfrak{I})
and
x_{-},y_{-}\in\mathcal{H}_{-}(J,\mathfrak{I})
. We note that
\Pi_{\pm}(J,\mathfrak{I})x=x_{\pm}
. Then we may use (iv) and (
8.5
) to obtain

\displaystyle\langle\mathbb{I}(f)x,y\rangle=

\displaystyle\;\int_{\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}{\rm Re}(f(\lambda))\,d\langle E(\lambda)x,y\rangle

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\;\int_{\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}{\rm Im}(f(\lambda))\,d\langle E(\lambda)J(x_{+}+x_{-}),y\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\int_{\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}{\rm Re}(f(\lambda))\,d\langle E(\lambda)(x_{+}+x_{-}),y\rangle

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\;\int_{\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}{\rm Im}(f(\lambda))\,d\langle E(\lambda)(x_{+}\mathfrak{I}-x_{-}\mathfrak{I}),y\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\int_{\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}\,d\langle E(\lambda)x_{+},y\rangle f(\lambda)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\;\int_{\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}\,d\langle E(\lambda)x_{-},y\rangle\overline{f(\lambda)},

in which case we have the desired identity.

We will now prove (ix). If we let
y=x
in (iv) with
f(\lambda)\geq 0

E
-a.e., then we may use the fact that
E(M)\succeq 0
for all
M\in\mathscr{B}(\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}
to obtain

\langle\mathbb{I}(f)x,x\rangle=\int_{\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}f(\lambda)d\langle E(\lambda)x,x\rangle\succeq 0.

Assertion (x) is an immediate consequence of Lemma
5.3.10
and Lemma
5.3.11
. Finally, we will prove (xi). Using Theorem
5.3.21
with
\Omega=\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}
,
\Omega^{\prime}=f(\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+})\setminus\{\infty\}
,
\psi(\lambda)=f(\lambda)
,
h(\lambda^{\prime})=\lambda^{\prime}
and
F
be the spectral measure given by (
9.13
), we obtain

\displaystyle\;\int_{\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}{\rm Re}(\lambda^{\prime})dF(\lambda^{\prime})+\int_{\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}{\rm Im}(\lambda^{\prime})dF(\lambda^{\prime})J

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\int_{f(\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+})\setminus\{\infty\}}{\rm Re}(\lambda^{\prime})dF(\lambda^{\prime})+\int_{f(\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+})\setminus\{\infty\}}{\rm Im}(\lambda^{\prime})dF(\lambda^{\prime})J

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\int_{f(\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+})\setminus\{\infty\}}{\rm Re}(f(\lambda))dE(\lambda)+\int_{f(\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+})\setminus\{\infty\}}{\rm Im}(f(\lambda))dE(\lambda)J

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\mathbb{I}(f).

Thus, the asserted uniqueness in Theorem
9.0.2
ensures that the spectral measure for
\mathbb{I}(f)
is given by (
9.13
).
∎

Corollary 9.0.5

(subclasses of bounded normal operators)
.

Let
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
be normal with spectral measure
E
and
J
be an imaginary operator associated with
T

(
see Theorem
8.0.4
)
. We have the following
:

(i)

T
is self-adjoint if and only if
\sigma_{S}(T)\subseteq\mathbb{R}
. In this case,

T=\int_{\mathbb{R}}t\,dE(t).

(ii)

T
is positive if and only if
\sigma_{S}(T)\subseteq[0,\infty)
.
In this case,

T=\int_{[0,\infty)}t\,dE(t).

(iii)

T
is anti self-adjoint if and only if
\sigma_{S}(T)\subseteq\{s\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}:{\rm Re}(s)=0\}
. In this case,

T=\int_{\mathbb{R}}t\,dE(t)J.

(v)

T
is unitary if and only if
\sigma_{S}(T)\subseteq\{s\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}:|s|=1\}
. In this case,

T=\int_{|\lambda|=1}{\rm Re}(\lambda)\,dE(\lambda)+\int_{|\lambda|=1}{\rm Im}(\lambda)\,dE(\lambda).

(vi)

T
is imaginary if and only if
\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}=\{\mathfrak{I}\}
. In this case,

Tx=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\eta_{i}\,\mathfrak{I}\langle x,\eta_{i}\rangle\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad x\in\mathcal{H}_{n},

where
(\eta_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
is any orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{H}_{+}(T,\mathfrak{I})

(
see (
8.5
)
)
.

Proof.

Let us prove the claims.
We prove (i).
If
T
is self-adjoint, then Lemma
3.2.4
asserts that
\sigma_{S}(T)\subseteq\mathbb{R}
. The integral representation
T=\int_{\mathbb{R}}t\,dE(t)
appeared in Theorem
6.0.1
. Conservely, suppose
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is normal and
\sigma_{S}(T)\subseteq\mathbb{R}
. Then the fact that
T=T^{*}
follows at once from Corollary
9.0.4
with
f(\lambda)=\lambda
. Assertions (ii) (with the help of Lemma

3.2.5
), (iii) (with the help of Lemma
8.0.1
) and (iv) (with the help of Lemma
3.2.6
) can be proved in much the same manner using Theorem
9.0.2
and Corollary
9.0.4
.

Finally, Assertion (vi) is just (
8.8
).
∎

10.
Spectral theorem for an unbounded normal operator and some consequences

Before we can formulate and prove the spectral theorem for an unbounded normal operator on a Clifford module, we will need to define the bounded transform of a densely defined closed operator and also a lemma which highlights various properties of the aforementioned bounded transform.

Definition 10.0.1
.

Suppose
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is a densely defined closed operator. Let
C_{T}
be as
in the statement of Theorem
3.2.3
, i.e.,
C_{T}:=(I+T^{*}T)^{-1}
. In view of Theorem
3.2.3
(ii),
C_{T}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
and
C_{T}
is positive. In view of Corollary
6.0.6
,
C_{T}
has a unique positive square root
C_{T}^{1/2}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
. If we let

(10.1)

Z_{T}:=TC_{T}^{1/2},

then
Z_{T}
will be called the
bounded transform of
T
(we will justify this nomenclature in Lemma
10.0.2
).

Lemma 10.0.2
.

Let
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
be densely defined. Then the following statements hold
:

(i)

Z_{T}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
. Moreover,

(10.2)

\|Z_{T}\|\leq 1

and

(10.3)

C_{T}=(I+T^{*}T)^{-1}=I-Z_{T}^{*}Z_{T}.

(ii)

(Z_{T})^{*}=Z_{T^{*}}
and hence
Z_{T}
is self-adjoint whenever
T
is self-adjoint.

(iii)

Z_{T}
is normal whenever
T
is normal.

Proof.

The proof in the classical complex Hilbert space (see, e.g., Lemma 5.8 in
[
66
]
) can be carried over into the Clifford module setting. For completeness, we will provide the proof. The proof is broken into steps.

Step 1:

Prove
(i).

First note that

(10.4)

\{\text{$Cx$: $x\in\mathcal{H}$}\}=\mathcal{D}(I+T^{*}T)=\mathcal{D}(T^{*}T)

and hence if
x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
, then

\displaystyle\|TC_{T}^{1/2}C_{T}^{1/2}x\|^{2}=

\displaystyle\;\langle T^{*}TC_{T}x,C_{T}x\rangle

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;\langle(I+T^{*}T)C_{T}x,C_{T}x\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\langle C_{T}^{-1}C_{T}x,C_{T}x\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\langle x,C_{T}x\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\|C_{T}^{1/2}x\|^{2}.

Thus, if
y\in\{\text{$C_{T}^{1/2}x$: $x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}$}\}
, then

(10.5)

\|Z_{T}y\|=\|TC_{T}^{1/2}y\|\leq\|y\|.

Since
{\rm Ker}(C_{T})=\{0\}
, we have that
{\rm Ker}(C_{T}^{1/2})=\{0\}
and hence
\{\text{$C_{T}^{1/2}x$: $x\in\mathcal{H}$}\}
is a dense subset of
\mathcal{H}_{n}
. By assumption,
T
is a closed operator and since
C_{T}^{1/2}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
, we have that
Z_{T}
is closed as well.
Thus, we have

\{\text{$C_{T}^{1/2}x$: $x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}$}\}\subseteq\mathcal{D}(T),\ \ \ \ \mathcal{D}(Z_{T})=\mathcal{H}_{n}

and, in view of (
10.5
), we have
\|Z_{T}\|\leq 1
.

Next, it follows from (
10.5
) and
C^{1/2}T^{*}\subseteq Z_{T}^{*}
that

\displaystyle(I-C_{T})C_{T}^{1/2}=

\displaystyle\;C_{T}^{1/2}(I+T^{*}T)C_{T}-C_{T}^{1/2}C_{T}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;C_{T}^{1/2}T^{*}TC_{T}^{1/2}C_{T}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\subseteq

\displaystyle\;Z_{T}^{*}Z_{T}C_{T}^{1/2}.

Thus,
Z_{T}^{*}Z_{T}C_{T}^{1/2}=(I-C_{T})C_{T}^{1/2}
and, as
\{\text{$C_{T}^{1/2}x$: $x\in\mathcal{H}$}\}
is a dense subset of
\mathcal{H}_{n}
, we get (
10.3
).

Step 2:

Prove
(ii).

Using (
10.3
) we get that
C_{T^{*}}=(I+TT^{*})^{-1}
. If
x\in\mathcal{D}(T^{*})
, then let
y=C_{T^{*}}x
. Therefore,

x=(I+TT^{*})y

and

T^{*}x=T^{*}(I+TT^{*})y=(I+T^{*}T)T^{*}y.

Thus,
C_{T^{*}}x\in\mathcal{D}(T^{*})
and hence

(10.6)

C_{T}T^{*}x=T^{*}y=T^{*}C_{T^{*}}x.

It follows easily from (
10.6
) and (
10.3
) that
p(C_{T^{*}})x\in\mathcal{D}(T^{*})
and

p(C_{T})T^{*}x=T^{*}p(C_{T^{*}})x

for any real polynomial
p\in\mathbb{R}[t]
. By the Weierstraß approximation theorem, there exists a sequence of real polynomials
(p_{n})_{n=0}^{\infty}
which converge uniformly in supremum norm to the function
t\mapsto t^{1/2}
on
[0,1]
. We may use Corollary
6.0.6
to obtain

\lim_{j\to\infty}\|p_{j}(C_{T})-C_{T}^{1/2}\|=\lim_{j\to\infty}\|p_{j}(C_{T})-C_{T}^{1/2}\|=0.

Since
T
is a closed operator,
T^{*}
is also a closed operator. Thus, we have

\displaystyle C_{T}^{1/2}T^{*}x=

\displaystyle\;\lim_{j\to\infty}p_{j}(C_{T})T^{*}x=\lim_{j\to\infty}T^{*}p_{n}(C_{T^{*}})x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;T^{*}(C_{T^{*}})^{1/2}x\quad{\rm for}\quad x\in\mathcal{D}(T^{*}).

As
C_{T}^{1/2}T^{*}\subseteq(TC_{T}^{1/2})^{*}=Z_{T^{*}}
, we get that

Z_{T^{*}}x=C_{T}^{1/2}T^{*}x=T^{*}(C_{T^{*}})^{1/2}x=(Z_{T})^{*}x

for
x\in\mathcal{D}(T^{*})
. Finally, since
\mathcal{D}(T^{*})
is dense in
\mathcal{H}
and
Z_{T}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
, we have that
Z_{T^{*}}x=(Z_{T})^{*}x
for
x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
, i.e.,
Z_{T^{*}}=(Z_{T})^{*}
.

Step 3:

Prove
(iii)
.

Using the first of (ii) on
T
and
T^{*}
and the assumption
TT^{*}=T^{*}T
, we have

\displaystyle I-(Z_{T})^{*}Z_{T}=

\displaystyle\;(I+T^{*}T)^{-1}=(I+TT^{*})^{-1}=I-(Z_{T^{*}})^{*}Z_{T^{*}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;I-Z_{T}(Z_{T})^{*}

in which case it is clear that
Z_{T}
is normal.
∎

Suppose
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is an unbounded normal operator and consider the bounded transform
Z_{T}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
of
T
given by (
10.1
). In view of Lemma
10.0.2
, we have
Z_{T}
is a bounded normal operator. Thus, we may use Theorem
8.0.4
to find a self-adjoint operator
A_{Z_{T}}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
, a positive operator
B_{Z_{T}}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
and a imaginary operator
J
such that
A,B
and
J
mutually commute and obey

(10.7)

Z_{T}=A_{Z_{T}}+JB_{Z_{T}}.

Definition 10.0.3

(strongly commuting operators)
.

Suppose
T,\widetilde{T}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
are unbounded normal operators . We will say that
T
and
\widetilde{T}

strongly commute
if the bounded transforms of
T
and
\widetilde{T}
, i.e.,
Z_{T}
and
Z_{\widetilde{T}}
, respectively, commute.

We are now ready to formulate and prove the spectral theorem for an unbounded normal operator.

Theorem 10.0.4
.

Let
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
be an unbounded normal operator. Then corresponding to any choice of
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathbb{S}
and an imaginary operator
J
in (
10.7
), there exists a spectral measure
E
on the Borel
\sigma
-algebra
\mathscr{B}(\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{I}})
such that

(10.8)

T=\int_{\sigma_{S}(T)\,\cap\,\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}{\rm Re}(\lambda)\,dE(\lambda)+\int_{\sigma_{S}(T)\,\cap\,\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}{\rm Im}(\lambda)\,dE(\lambda)\,J.

E
is unique in the sense that if
F
is a spectral measure on
\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{I}})
such that
T=\int_{\mathbb{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{I}}}{\rm Re}(\lambda)\,dF(\lambda)+\int_{\mathbb{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{I}}}{\rm Im}(\lambda)dF(\lambda)J
, then
E(M\cap\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{I}})=F(M)
for all
M\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{I}})
.

Remark 10.0.5
.

Before proceeding to the proof of Theorem
10.0.4
, we wish to point out that a consequence of Theorem
10.0.4
is that for any unbounded normal operator
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
, we have

(10.9)

\sigma_{S}(T)\neq\emptyset.

Indeed, for any choice of
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathbb{S}
, Theorem
5.3.14
with
f(\lambda)=\lambda
implies that
{\rm supp}\,E=\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}
. Since
{\rm supp}\,E\neq\emptyset
, we have (
10.9
).

Proof of Theorem
10.0.4
.

The proof is broken into steps.

Step 1:

Show that there exists a spectral measure
E
on
\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{I}}
such that (
9.3
) holds
.

In view of Lemma
10.0.2
, we have
Z_{T}
is a bounded normal operator. Moreover,
\|Z_{T}\|\leq 1
. Let
J
be the imaginary operator appearing in (
10.7
). Since
Z_{T}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is normal, we can use Theorem
6.0.1
to obtain a uniquely determined spectral measure
E_{Z_{T}}
on
\mathscr{B}(\sigma_{S}(Z_{T}))\subseteq\overline{\mathbb{D}^{+}_{\mathfrak{I}}}
,where

\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{I}}:=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}:|\lambda|<1\}\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad\mathfrak{I}\in\mathbb{S},

such that

(10.10)

Z_{T}=\int_{\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{I}}}{\rm Re}(\lambda)dE_{Z_{T}}(\lambda)+\int_{\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{I}}}{\rm Im}(\lambda)dE_{Z_{T}}(\lambda)J.

We may use (
10.10
) and (
5.20
) to see that, for any
x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
and
M\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{I}})
, we have

(10.11)

\langle(I-(Z_{T})^{*}Z_{T})E_{Z_{T}}(M),E_{Z_{T}}(M)x\rangle=\int_{M}(1-|\lambda|^{2})d\langle E_{Z_{T}}(\lambda)x,x\rangle.

A simple consequence of Theorem
3.2.3
is
C_{T}^{-1}=I-(Z_{T})^{*}Z_{T}
is invertible and positive. Thus,
{\rm Ker}\,(I-(Z_{T})^{*}(Z_{T}))=\{0\}
and
I-(Z_{T})^{*}Z_{T}
is positive. Thus, (
10.11
) implies that
\sigma_{S}(Z_{T})\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}={\rm supp}\,E_{Z_{T}}\subseteq\overline{\mathbb{D}^{+}_{\mathfrak{I}}}
and
E_{Z_{T}}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+})=0
.
Consequently,

E_{Z_{T}}(\mathbb{D}^{+}_{\mathfrak{I}})=E_{Z_{T}}(\overline{\mathbb{D}^{+}_{\mathfrak{I}}}\,\setminus\,\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+})=I,

where
\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{I}}:=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}:|\lambda|=1\}
.

Let
\psi(\lambda)=\lambda(1-|\lambda|)^{-1/2}
. Notice that
\psi
is an intrinsic function (see Defintion
5.3.12
). We claim that
\mathbb{I}(\psi)=Z_{T}(C_{T}^{1/2})^{-1}
, where
\mathbb{I}
denotes the spectral integral with respect to the spectral measure
E_{Z_{T}}
. Indeed, since
E_{Z_{T}}(\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+})=I
and
\psi
is clearly finite
E_{Z_{T}}
-a.e. on
\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}
, (
5.16
) asserts that
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(\psi))=\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}((1-\lambda)^{-1/2}))
. Thus, we may make us of Theorem
5.3.7
(ii) and (iv) to obtain

\mathbb{I}(\psi)=\mathbb{I}(\lambda)\mathbb{I}((1-|\lambda|^{2})^{-1/2}).

Next, in view of Lemma
3.0.29
, we have that

\mathbb{I}((1-|\lambda|^{2})^{-1/2})=\mathbb{I}((1-|\lambda|^{2})^{1/2})^{-1}.

Finally, since
Z_{T}=\mathbb{I}(\lambda)
and the square root of a positive bounded operator is unique (see Corollary
6.0.6
), we have that

C_{T}^{1/2}=(I-(Z_{T})^{*}Z_{T})^{1/2}=\mathbb{I}((1-|\lambda|^{2})^{1/2}).

Therefore, we have proved the claim
\mathbb{I}(\psi)=Z_{T}(C_{T}^{1/2})^{-1/2}
.

In what follows, with a slight abuse of notation, we will view
\psi
as
\psi|_{\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}
. Let
E(M):=E_{Z_{T}}(\psi^{-1}(M))
for
M\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+})
. In view of Remark
5.3.20
,
E
is a spectral measure on
\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+})
and
J
is an imaginary associated operator with the spectral measure
E
. Using the fact that
T=Z_{T}\{(C_{T})^{1/2}\}^{-1}
,
\mathbb{I}(\psi)=Z_{T}\{(C_{T})^{1/2}\}^{-1}
and Theorem
5.3.21
, we obtain

\displaystyle T=

\displaystyle\;\mathbb{I}(\psi)=\int_{\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}{\rm Re}(\psi(\lambda))dE_{Z_{T}}(\lambda)+\int_{\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}{\rm Im}(\psi(\lambda))dE_{Z_{T}}(\lambda)J

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\int_{\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-|\lambda|^{2}}}\,dE_{Z_{T}}(\lambda)

(10.12)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\int_{\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}{\rm Re}(\lambda)dE(\lambda)+\int_{\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}{\rm Im}(\lambda)dE(\lambda)J.

Finally, using Theorem
5.3.14
, we have that
{\rm supp}\,E=\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}
, i.e., (
10.12
) is (
10.8
).

Step 2:

Show that
E
is unique
.

Suppose
F
is another spectral measure on
\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+})
such that the imaginary operator
J
is associated with
F
and

T=\int_{\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}{\rm Re}(\lambda)\,dF(\lambda)+\int_{\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}{\rm Im}(\lambda)\,dF(\lambda)\,J.

Let
F^{\prime}(M):=F(\psi(M))
for
M\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+})
. In view of Remark
5.3.20
,
F^{\prime}
is a spectral measure on
\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+})
and the imaginary operator
J
is associated with
F^{\prime}
. Moreover, since
E_{Z_{T}}(\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+})
, we have

\displaystyle Z_{T}=

\displaystyle\;\int_{\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}{\rm Re}(\lambda)dF^{\prime}(\lambda)+\int_{\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}{\rm Im}(\lambda)dF^{\prime}(\lambda)\,J

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\int_{\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}{\rm Re}(\lambda)dE_{Z_{T}}(\lambda)+\int_{\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}{\rm Im}(\lambda)dE_{Z_{T}}(\lambda)\,J.

But then the Borel functional calculus applied to
Z_{T}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
(see Corollary
9.0.4
) asserts that for any real-valued polynomial
p\in\mathbb{R}[\lambda]
and
x\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
, we have

\displaystyle\langle p(\lambda)x,x\rangle=

\displaystyle\;\int_{\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}p(\lambda)dF^{\prime}(\lambda)x,x\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\int_{\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}p(\lambda)dE_{Z_{T}}(\lambda)x,x\rangle.

Since
\mathbb{R}[\lambda]
is dense in the Banach space of real-valued continuous functions on the compact set
\overline{\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}
(denoted by
\mathscr{C}(\overline{\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}},\mathbb{R})
), we can use Corollary
4.0.8
to see that
\langle F^{\prime}(M)x,x\rangle=\langle E_{Z_{T}}(M)x,x\rangle
for any
M\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+})
, But then the polarisation formula (
2.20
) enables us to deduce that
F^{\prime}=E_{Z_{T}}
. Finally since
F(M)=F^{\prime}(\psi^{-1}(M))
for
M\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+})
, we have
F=E
on
\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+})
as required.
∎

Remark 10.0.6
.

Let
\mathfrak{I},\mathfrak{J}\in\mathbb{S}
and
\gamma:\mathbb{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{I}}\to\mathbb{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{J}}
denote the bijective map given by

(10.13)

\gamma(\lambda_{0}+\lambda_{1}\mathfrak{I})=\lambda_{0}+\lambda_{1}\mathfrak{J},

where
\lambda_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\lambda_{1}\geq 0
. An immediate consequence of the proof of Theorem
10.0.4
, is that if
E_{\mathfrak{I}}
, where
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathbb{S}
, is a spectral measure for an unbounded normal operator
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
and
E_{\mathfrak{J}}
, where
\mathfrak{J}\in\mathbb{S}
, then since
\gamma(\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+})=\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}
and
{\rm supp}\,E_{\mathfrak{I}}=\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}
, we have

(10.14)

E_{\mathfrak{I}}(M)=E_{\mathfrak{J}}(\gamma(M))\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad M\in\mathscr{B}(\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}).

In view of the above observations, we are justified in calling a spectral measure
E
on
\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}

the spectral measure of
T
.

Corollary 10.0.7

(Borel functional calculus in the unbounded case)
.

Let
T,E
and
J
be as in Theorem
10.0.4
and
\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}:=\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}
for
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathbb{S}
. For any
f,g\in\mathfrak{F}(\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+},\mathscr{B}(\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}),E)
and
c\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}
, we have the spectral integrals
\mathbb{I}(f),\mathbb{I}(g)\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
have the following properties
:

(i)

\mathbb{I}(\bar{f})=\mathbb{I}(f)^{*}
.

(ii)

\mathbb{I}(fg)=\overline{\mathbb{I}(f)\mathbb{I}(g)}
.

(iii)

\mathbb{I}(fc+g)=\overline{\mathbb{I}(f)({\rm Re}(c)I+{\rm Im}(c)J)+\mathbb{I}(g)}
for all
c\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}
.

(iv)

\mathbb{I}(f)
is a closed normal operator belonging to
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
.

(v)

\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(f)\mathbb{I}(g))=\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(fg))\cap\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{I}(g))
.

(vi)

If we let
\Pi_{\pm}(J,\mathfrak{I})
denote the orthogonal projection onto the right complex subspace
\mathcal{H}_{\pm}(J,\mathfrak{I})
, respectively
(
see Theorem
8.0.5
(ii))
, then

\displaystyle\langle\mathbb{I}(f)x,y\rangle=

\displaystyle\;\int_{\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}d\langle E(\lambda)\Pi_{+}(J,\mathfrak{I})x,y\rangle\,f(\lambda)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\;\int_{\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}d\langle E(\lambda)\Pi_{-}(J,\mathfrak{I})x,y\rangle\overline{f(\lambda)}\quad\quad{\rm for}\quad x\in\mathcal{D}(T),

where both integrals above are meant in the sense of (
4.5
).

(vii)

If
f\in\mathfrak{F}(\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+},\mathscr{B}(\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}),\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E)
is nonnegative
E
-a.e., then
\mathbb{I}(f)\succeq 0
.

(vii)

\mathbb{I}(f)^{-1}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
if and only if
f(\lambda)\neq 0

E
-a.e. In this case,
\mathbb{I}(f)^{-1}=\mathbb{I}(1/f)
.

(viii)

The spectral measure
E_{\mathbb{I}(f)}
of
\mathbb{I}(f)
satisfies the identity

(10.15)

E_{\mathbb{I}(f)}(M)=E(f^{-1}(M\cap f(\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+})).

Proof.

Let
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
be normal and fix
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathbb{S}
. Then by Theorem
10.0.4
, we can find a spectral measure
E
on
\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}
such that (
10.8
) holds. Thus, we may invoke Theorem
5.3.7
with
\Omega=\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}
and
\mathscr{A}=\mathscr{B}(\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+})
to obtain (i)-(v).

Assertions (vi)-(viii) are straight forward adaptations of

Theorem
5.2.4
(viii)-(xi) to the unbounded setting. The corresponding proofs can be completed in much the same way as the bounded setting.
∎

Corollary 10.0.8

(subclasses of bounded normal operators)
.

Let
T,E
and
J
be as in Theorem
10.0.4
and
\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}:=\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}
for
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathbb{S}
. We have the following
:

(i)

T
is self-adjoint if and only if
\sigma_{S}(T)\subseteq\mathbb{R}
. In this case,

T=\int_{\mathbb{R}}t\,dE(t).

(ii)

T
is positive if and only if
\sigma_{S}(T)\subseteq[0,\infty)
. In this case,

T=\int_{[0,\infty)}t\,dE(t).

(iii)

T
is anti self-adjoint if and only if
\sigma_{S}(T)\subseteq\{s\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}:{\rm Re}(s)=0\}
. In this case,

T=\int_{\mathbb{R}}t\,dE(t)J.

Proof.

The proofs of (i)-(iii) can be completed in much the same way of the proofs of Corollary
9.0.5
(i)-(iii) with Theorem
10.0.4
in place of Theorem
9.0.2
.
∎

The following corollary is an unbounded analogue of the Teichmüller decomposition.

Corollary 10.0.9
.

Corresponding to any normal operator
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
, there exist a self-adjoint operator
A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
, an imaginary operator
J\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
and a positive operator
B\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
such that
A
,
J
and
B
strongly commute and satisfy

(10.16)

T=A+JB.

In this case,
A=\int_{\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}{\rm Re}(\lambda)\,dE(\lambda)
and
B=\int_{\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}}{\rm Im}(\lambda)\,dE(\lambda)
, where
E
is the spectral measure for
T
, and

(10.17)

T
and
J
strongly commute.

Proof.

The additive decomposition (
10.16
) follows at once from (
10.8
). The fact that
A
,
B
and
J
strongly commute follows from the fact that
Z_{A}=A_{Z_{T}}
,
Z_{B}=B_{Z_{T}}
, where
A_{Z_{T}}
and
B_{Z_{T}}
are as in (
10.7
), and the fact that
A_{Z_{T}},B_{Z_{T}}
and
J
mutually commute. Finally, (
10.17
) is an immediate consequence of (
10.16
) and the fact that
A,B
and
J
strongly commute
∎

Theorem 10.0.10

(spectral mapping theorem for an unbounded normal operator)
.

Let
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
be normal with spectral measure
E
and
\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}:=\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{I}}
, where
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathbb{S}
. For any intrinsic continuous function
f\in\mathfrak{B}(\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+},\mathscr{B}(\Omega_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}),\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}},E)
, we have

(10.18)

\sigma_{S}(\mathbb{I}(f))\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}=\overline{f(\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}})},

If
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
is normal, then (
10.18
) becomes

(10.19)

\sigma_{S}(\mathbb{I}(f))\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}=f(\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}).

Proof.

We begin by noting that image of
f
is symmetric about the real axis in
\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}
(see (
5.29
) and (
5.30
)). We will first show that

\overline{f(\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}_{{\mathfrak{I}}})}\subseteq\sigma_{S}(\mathbb{I}(f))\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}.

Suppose
w_{0}\in\overline{f(\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}})}
and
w_{0}\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}
. For any
\varepsilon>0
, we can find
\lambda_{0}\in\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}
such that
f(\lambda_{0})\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}
and

(10.20)

|f(\lambda_{0})-w_{0}|<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.

Since
f
is continuous, we have the existence of
\delta>0
such that

\displaystyle U_{\delta}:=

\displaystyle\;\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}:|\lambda-\lambda_{0}|<\delta\}

\displaystyle\subseteq

\displaystyle\;\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}:|f(\lambda)-f(\lambda_{0})|<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\}

\displaystyle\subseteq

\displaystyle\;\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}:|f(\lambda)-w_{0}|<\varepsilon\}

\displaystyle=:

\displaystyle\;V_{\varepsilon}.

Since
w_{0}
and
f(\lambda_{0})
both belong to
\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}
, we may proceed as in the verification of (
9.10
) and show that

V_{\varepsilon}\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{I}}:|f(\lambda)^{2}-2{\rm Re}(w_{0})f(\lambda)+|w_{0}|^{2}|<\varepsilon^{2}\}.

Thus, as
\lambda_{0}\in\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}
and
\lambda_{0}\in U_{\delta}
, we have that
E(U_{\delta})\neq\emptyset
, in which case
E(V_{\varepsilon}\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+})\neq\emptyset
. But then Theorem
5.3.14
ensures that
w_{0}\in\sigma_{S}(\mathbb{I}(f))\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}
.

If
w_{0}
and
f(\lambda_{0})
both belong to
\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{-}
, then we can use the fact that the image of
f
is symmetric about the real axis and the fact that the
S
-spectrum is axially symmetric (see Remark
3.1.7
) and repeat the argument above with
\bar{w}_{0}
and
\bar{\lambda}_{0}
in place of
w_{0}
and
\lambda_{0}
, respectively, to arrive at the same conclusion.

We will now show that
\sigma_{S}(\mathbb{I}(f))\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}\subseteq\overline{f(\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}})}
. The inclusion
\sigma_{S}(\mathbb{I}(f))\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}\subseteq\overline{f(\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}})}
follows immediately from comparing the formula for
\sigma_{S}(\mathbb{I}(f)\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}

coming from Theorem
5.3.14
and the formula for
\overline{f(\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}})}
derived from (
9.9
).

Finally, (
10.19
) drops out easily from the fact that if
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
, then
\sigma_{S}(T)\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathfrak{I}}^{+}
is compact.
∎

Appendix A
Background on the
S
-spectrum and connections with functional calculi and function theory

In 1936, Birkhoff and von Neumann, in their paper
[
9
]
on the logic of quantum mechanics, showed that an set-theoretic abstraction of quantum mechanics can be formulated on Hilbert spaces over the reals, complex numbers and quaternions. Consequently, there was a strong motivation to prove the spectral theorem in the quaternionic setting (i.e., a Clifford module over
\mathbb{R}_{2}
) and since that time
several attempts have appeared in the literature. The main contributions are due to O. Teichmüller
[
67
]
in 1936
and to K. Viswanath
[
69
]
in 1971.
However, both authors do not make clear the notion of spectrum that is in use for quaternionic linear operators. Nevertheless, there are useful results on quaternionic operator theory in
[
67
]
and
[
69
]
.

The major breakthrough came in 2006
when I. Sabadini and the first author discovered the
S
-spectrum
\sigma_{S}(T)
and the
S
-functional calculus for
a quaternionic linear operator
T
. A prime motivation for this investigation was to give quaternionic quantum mechanics a rigorous mathematical
foundation. The strategy for the identification of the
S
-spectrum
was purely based on hyperholomorphic analysis methods and not on physical arguments (see the introduction of the book
[
16
]
for a detailed explanation).
The definition of
S
-spectrum
\sigma_{S}(T)
for an linear operator
T
on a quaternionic Banach space
V
is somewhat counter intuitive because it involves the second order operator

Q_{s}(T):=T^{2}-2s_{0}T+|s|^{2}\,I

and is given by

\sigma_{S}(T)=\{s\in\mathbb{H}\ :\ Q_{s}(T)\ \ {\rm is\ not\ invertible}\ \ \mathcal{B}(V)\},

where
\mathbb{H}
is the algebra of quaternions,
s_{0}
the real part of
s\in\mathbb{H}
,

|s|^{2}
is the modulus squared and
\mathcal{B}(V)
is the space of all bounded linear operators.

Before 2006 in the literature there were two different notions of spectrum in the quaternionic setting (as well as in the Clifford setting):
the left spectrum
\sigma_{L}(T)
and the right spectrum
\sigma_{R}(T)
and both definitions mimic the eigenvalue problem for
complex operators.
We point out that just in the finite dimensional case the quaternionic spectral theorem was precisely proved using the notion of right spectrum
by D. R. Farenick, B. A. F. Pidkowich in
[
30
]
that was published in 2003.
We also want to point out that in the literature on
quaternionic quantum mechanics physicists used the right spectrum to
describe the bounded states where there are just the eigenvalues,
see the book of S. Adler
[
1
]
and for more recent advanced see the paper of J. Gantner
[
34
]
on the equivalence of complex and quaternionic quantum mechanics.
Only in 2015 the authors with D. Alpay proved the spectral theorem for quaternionic normal operators based on the
S
-spectrum
\sigma_{S}(T)
in

[
4
]
which was published in 2016.
Fairly recently, there has been a renewed quaternionic quantum mechanics, which utilises the notion of
S
-spectrum (see, e.g.,
[
57
,
58
,
59
,
68
]
).
